ECSE 507 Lecture Notes (presentation)


These are notes used for lecturing ECSE 507 during the Winter 2024 semester.
Please note that these notes constantly evolve and beware of typos/errors.
These notes are heavily influenced by Boyd and Vandenberghe's excellent text Convex Optimization.
I appreciate being informed about typos/errors in these notes.

N.B.: This page is formatted to be projected--see here for the unformatted version (i.e., without excessive whitespace).

Click each subject to unfold.

Introduction
General Problem Interested in solving and studying minimization problems subject to constraints:

$latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & x \in D \end{cases}, &s=3$

where
  • $latex x&s=3$ are problem parameters;
  • $latex f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ is the objective function and $latex f_0(x) &s=3$ usually interpreted as cost for choosing $latex x &s=3$;
  • $latex D \subset\mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ is a constraint set often described "geometrically".
Example 1. Design Problem Interpretation Let
  • $latex x,y&s=3$=scalar-valued design variables
    (e.g., dimensions of manufactured object, yaw and pitch of jet).
  • $latex f_0(x,y)&s=3$ = penalty for choosing design $latex (x,y)&s=3$
    (e.g., cost in material, energy, time, deviation from desired path).
  • $latex D&s=3$ = design specifications
    (i.e., allowable/possible values for $latex (x,y)&s=3$).
    E.g., $latex D = \{ (x,y) : a<x<b, c<y<d\}&s=3$ specifies minimum and maximum design values.
Then the problem is to find optimal design values $latex (x,y)&s=3$ which minimize cost $latex f_0&s=3$ and satisfy the design specifications $latex (x,y) \in D&s=3$.






Example 2. Minimize function over ellipse Let
$latex f_0(x,y) = 1 - \frac{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{2} &s=3$
$latex D = \{ (x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : x^2 + 2 y^2 \leq 1 \} &s=3$.
Then the problem

$latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & 1-\frac{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{2}\\ \text{subject to} & x^2 + 2 y^2 \leq 1 \end{cases} &s=3$

is a familiar kind of purely geometric optimization problem.
It has two solutions: $latex (x,y,f_0(x,y)) = (\pm 1, 0 , \frac{1}{2}) &s=3$.
Many applied problems can look like this, but with an applied interpretation.















Problems With Structure General optimization problems can be numerically inefficient to solve or analytically difficult, unless $latex f_0&s=3$ and $latex D&s=3$ have additional structure/properties.
Identifying nice structure/properties of problem $latex \implies &s=3$ problem may become analytically solvable or numerically efficient.
Examples of nice structure:
linearity
$latex f_0(x) = c^T x$
$latex D$ defined in terms of linear equalities/inequalities;
e.g., $latex a_1^T x \geq 0, \ldots, a_m^T x \geq 0$, or succinctly written $latex A x \succeq 0$.






convexity or quasiconvexity
E.g., convex if $latex f_0(tx+(1-t)y) \leq tf_0(x) + (1-t)f_0(y)$
$latex 0<t<1$
$latex D$ is itself a convex set.






sparsity or other matrix structure
E.g., $latex f_0(x) = x^T C x$ and $latex D$ given by $latex Ax \succeq 0$
If $latex C,A$ sparse
$latex \implies$ lots of cancellation
$latex \implies$ improve computational efficiency
$latex \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 & 1 & 0 & 0 & 1\\ 0 & 1 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0\\ 1 & 0 & 0 & 1 & 0 & 1\\ 0 & 1 & 0 & 1 & 0 & 0\\ 0 & 1 & 0 & 0 & 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} ,\qquad \begin{bmatrix} 1&2&0&0\\ 2&1&0&0\\ 0&0&2&1\\ 0&0&1&2 \end{bmatrix} &s=3 $















Linear Programming Simplest case: $latex f_0&s=3$ is linear and $latex D&s=3$ defined in terms of linear constraints.
Notation: if $latex x, y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$, then

$latex x \succeq y &s=3$ means $latex x_i \geq y_i &s=3$ for $latex i =1,\ldots, n &s=3$.

$latex c^T &s=3$ = transpose of the column vector $latex c \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
Linear program: given $latex c \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$, $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}&s=3$, solve

$latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Ax \succeq b\\ &x \succeq 0 \end{cases}.&s=3$

Thus $latex f_0(x) = c^T x&s=3$ and $latex D = \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n: Ax \succeq b, x \succeq 0\}&s=3$.

Positives of linear programming:
  • Conceptually simple: relies heavily on linear algebra
  • There are classical numerical methods which are often very efficient.
  • If $latex x^\star \in D&s=3$ is local minimizer of $latex f_0&s=3$ on $latex D&s=3$, then it is automatically a global minimizer on $latex D&s=3$.
  • Can sometimes approximate smooth problems linearly; however, usually can only give "local" results.
    (E.g., $latex \sin(x) \sim x &s=3$ for $latex 0 \leq x \ll 1 &s=3$.)


Shortcomings of linear programming:
  • Many applied problems are not linear.
  • Many problems may not even be (suitably) approximated by linear programs.
    E.g., the "barrier"

    $latex I_{-}(x) = \begin{cases} 0 & x <0\\ \infty & x\geq0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    is better approximated by the a "logarithmic barrier" of the form $latex - c \log(-x) &s=3$ than any linear function.















Convex Optimization Convex optimization problem: $latex f_0&s=3$ and $latex D&s=3$ are convex.
This is the main focus of the course.

Positives of Convex Optimization:
  • Relatively conceptually simple.
  • Still often have efficient, albeit more sophisticated, numerical methods.
  • Many applied problems may be recast as or approximated by convex optimization problems.
  • If $latex x^\star \in D&s=3$ is local minimizer of $latex f_0&s=3$ on $latex D&s=3$, then it is automatically a global minimizer on $latex D&s=3$.


Shortcomings of Convex Optimization:
  • Problems may seriously fail to be analytically tractable or numerically efficient.
  • Exists nonlinear problems which cannot be approximated by convex problems.















    • Example (Least Squares) A standard and ubiquitous kind of convex optimization problem is the least squares problem.
    This problem takes the form:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \Vert Ax-b \Vert^2\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ &h_i(x) =0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases},&s=3$

    where
    • $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert&s=3$ is some norm
    • $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}&s=3$ a matrix
    • $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$ a fixed vector
    • $latex f_0(x) = \Vert A x-b \Vert^2&s=3$ is the (convex) objective function
    • $latex f_i,h_i&s=3$ are convex.
    N.B.: will come back to this problem.
    Example: Distance from points to ellipse If $latex \Vert x \Vert^2 = x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2&s=3$, $latex A&s=3$ is the identity matrix, and $latex D&s=3$ is an ellipsoid, then the solution is the point in the ellipsoid closest to the point $latex b&s=3$.
    The image below depicts this situation with

    $latex b = \begin{bmatrix}0\\2\end{bmatrix} &s=3$ and $latex D = \{(x,y): (x-2)^2 + 2(y-2)^2 \leq 1 \} &s=3$.

    Here, the optimal solution is $latex (x,y) = (2-\sqrt{2},2) &s=3$.















    Optimal Control Let
    $latex x(t),u(t),a(t),f(t) &s=3$ be functions $latex \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$.
    Assume $latex x(t) &s=3$ evolves by

    $latex \begin{aligned} \dot x(t) &= a(t) x(t) + f(t) u(t)\\ \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    Here,
    • $latex \dot x(t) &s=3$ is the time derivative of $latex x(t) &s=3$.
    • $latex a(t),f(t) &s=3$ are assumed to be given;
    • we think of $latex x(t) &s=3$ as being the state of some system at time $latex t &s=3$;
    • we think of $latex u(t) &s=3$ as an input we are allowed to choose to dictate the evolution $latex x(t) &s=3$ of $latex x(0) &s=3$; i.e., $latex u(t) &s=3$ "controls" the system;
    • When $latex u(t)=u(x(t),t) &s=3$, the system experiences "feedback."
    • the goal: choose control law $latex u(t) = u(x(t),t) &s=3$ so that $latex x(t) &s=3$ is as "desirable" as possible.
    Example: Optimal Control Problems Optimal control problem: choose the "best" control $latex u(t) &s=3$ which gives the "most" desirable $latex x(t) &s=3$.
    Typically "best" and "desirable" are determined by size/cost of $latex u(t) &s=3$ and $latex x(t) &s=3$; e.g., one may wish to minimize

    $latex \int u(t)^2 + x(t)^2 dt. &s=3 $

    Therefore: problem is to solve (roughly speaking)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \int u(t)^2 + x(t)^2 dt\\ \text{subject to} & x'(t) = a(t) x(t) + f(t) u(t)\\ \end{cases} &s=3 $

    This will be another focus of the course and we will see some optimal control problems can be recast as convex optimization problems.















    Rough Outline of Course
    Part 1: Basics of Convexity and Convex Optimization Problems.
    Part 2: Applications of Convex Optimization Problems.
    Part 3: Algorithms for Solving Convex Optimization Problems.
    Part 4: Topics in Optimal Control.















    Convex Geometry
    Convex Sets Convex set: a subset $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ satisfying:

    for all $latex x,y \in X&s=3$ and $latex t \in [0,1]&s=3$, there holds $latex tx + (1-t) y \in X&s=3$.

    I.e., $latex X&s=3$ contains all line segments whose endpoints belong to $latex X&s=3$.

    Examples: some standard convex sets.

    1. Closed or open polytopes in $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
      E.g., the interior of a tetrahedron.
    2. Euclidean balls, ellipsoids.
    3. Linear subspaces and affine spaces (e.g., lines, planes).
    4. Given a norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert&s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, the $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert&s=3$-ball

      $latex \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \Vert x - x_{0} \Vert \leq r \}&s=3$

      with center $latex x_0 \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ and radius $latex r>0&s=3$ is a convex set.
      Recall: a norm satisfies
      • $latex \Vert x+ y \Vert \leq \Vert x \Vert + \Vert y \Vert&s=3$ for all vectors $latex x,y &s=3$;
      • $latex \Vert c x \Vert = |c| \Vert x \Vert&s=3$ for all vectors $latex x &s=3$ and scalars $latex c &s=3$;
      • $latex \Vert x \Vert=0&s=3$ iff $latex x =0&s=3$.















    Affine Subsets Affine subset: A subset $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ satisfying:

    For all $latex x,y \in X&s=3$ and $latex t \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$, there holds $latex tx + (1-t) y \in X&s=3$.

    I.e., $latex X&s=3$ contains all lines which pass through two distinct points in $latex X&s=3$.
    N.B.: An affine subset is just a translated linear subspace:
    "a linear space that's forgotten its origin".
    Example 1. Let $latex X = \{ (x,y,0): x,y \in \mathbb{R} \}&s=3$ be the $latex xy&s=3$-plane in $latex \mathbb{R}^3&s=3$.
    Then any translation or rotation of $latex X&s=3$ is an affine subset.
    Example 2. If $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}&s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$, then $latex X=\{x \in \mathbb{R}^n: Ax = b\}&s=3$ is affine.
    ($latex X&s=3$ is just a translate of $latex \text{ker}\,A&s=3$.)
    Example 3. Let

    $latex A = \begin{bmatrix} 0&0&0\\ 0&0&0\\ 0&0&1 \end{bmatrix}\qquad \text{ and } \qquad b = \begin{bmatrix} 0\\0\\3 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$.

    Then the solution set to $latex Ax=b&s=3$ is

    $latex \{(x,y,3): x,y \in\mathbb{R}\}&s=3$,

    which is just $latex \text{ker}\, A&s=3$ translated by 3 in the $latex z&s=3$-direction:















    Cones Cone: A subset $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ satisfying:

    For all $latex x \in X&s=3$ and $latex t \geq 0&s=3$, there holds $latex tx \in X&s=3$.

    I.e., $latex X&s=3$ contains all "positive" rays emanating from the origin and passing through any of its points.
    Proposition. $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ is a convex cone iff for all $latex x_1,x_2 \in X&s=3$ and $latex \theta_1,\theta_2\geq0&s=3$, there holds $latex \theta_1 x_1 + \theta_2 x_2 \in X&s=3$.
    Proof.
    Step 1. ($latex \implies &s=3$) Suppose $latex X&s=3$ is a convex cone and let $latex x_1,x_2 \in X&s=3$ and $latex \theta_1,\theta_2\geq0&s=3$ be arbitrary.
    Want to show: $latex \theta_1 x_1 + \theta_2 x_2 \in X &s=3$.
    Step 2. Being conic implies $latex x = \frac{\theta_1}{t} x_1&s=3$ and $latex y=\frac{\theta_2}{1-t}x_2&s=3$ belong to $latex X&s=3$ for all $latex 0<t<1&s=3$.
    Step 3. Being convex implies $latex tx+ (1-t)y = \theta_1 x_1 + \theta_2 x_2 \in X&s=3$, as desired.
    Step 4. ($latex \impliedby &s=3$) Suppose $latex X&s=3$ is such that $latex \theta_1 x_1 + \theta_2 x_2 \in X&s=3$ for all $latex x_1,x_2 \in X&s=3$ and $latex \theta_1,\theta_2\geq0&s=3$.
    Want to show: $latex X &s=3$ is a convex cone.
    Step 5. $latex X&s=3$ being conic follows from taking $latex \theta_1\geq0&s=3$ arbitrary and $latex \theta_2 = 0&s=3$.
    Step 6. Convexity follows from taking $latex \theta_1 + \theta_2 = 1&s=3$ with $latex \theta_1,\theta_2 \geq0&s=3$ and $latex 0 \leq \theta_1 \leq 1 &s=3$.
    Indeed: $latex \theta_1 + \theta_2 = 1 &s=3$ and $latex t := \theta_1 &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex t x_1 + (1-t) x_2 \in X &s=3$ for $latex 0 \leq t \leq 1 &s=3$ since $latex 1-t = \theta_2 &s=3$.

    Examples.

    1. Hyperplanes $latex \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : a^{T} x = 0\}&s=3$ with normal $latex a \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$,
      halfspaces $latex \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : a^{T}x \leq 0 \}&s=3$,
      nonnegative orthants $latex \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n: x \succeq 0\}&s=3$ are all convex cones.
      (Here, $latex u \succeq v&s=3$ if $latex u_i \geq v_i&s=3$ for $latex i = 1,\ldots,n&s=3$.)
    2. Given a norm $latex \Vert\cdot\Vert&s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, the $latex \Vert\cdot\Vert&s=3$-norm cone is

      $latex \{ (x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^{n+1} : \Vert x \Vert \leq t \}&s=3$,

      which is a convex cone in $latex \mathbb{R}^{n+1}&s=3$.
    3. See "positive semidefinite cone" below.















    Polyhedra Polyhedron: Any subset $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ of the form

    $latex X = \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : a_j^T x \leq b_j,\, c_i^T x = d_i \}&s=3$
    $latex j=1,\ldots,m &s=3$
    $latex i=1,\ldots,p &s=3$

    given the vectors $latex a_j,c_i \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ and scalars $latex b_j,d_i \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$.
    Thus, $latex X&s=3$ is a finite intersection of halfspaces and hyperplanes.
    N.B.: Introducing equality constraints can be used to reduce dimension.
    Example. The polyhedron below is given by the indicated system of inequalities:



    $latex \begin{aligned} y-x& \leq0\\ -x-y&\leq -1\\ x &\leq 3\\ -x &\leq -1 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    It is an easy exercise to rewrite the inequalities in the notation $latex a_j^T x \leq b_j &s=3$ for suitable $latex a_j,b_j &s=3$.















    Positive Semidefiniteness
    Symmetric matrix: a matrix $latex X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &s=3$ satisfying $latex X = X^T &s=3$; i.e.,

    $latex X = \begin{bmatrix} x_{11} & x_{12} & \cdots & x_{1n} \\ x_{21} & x_{22} & \cdots & x_{2n}\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ x_{n1} & x_{n2} & \cdots & x_{nn} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} x_{11} & x_{21} & \cdots & x_{n1} \\ x_{12} & x_{22} & \cdots & x_{n2}\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ x_{1n} & x_{2n} & \cdots & x_{nn} \end{bmatrix} = X^T. &s=1$

    Set of symmetric matrices:

    $latex \boldsymbol{S}^n = \{ X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}: X = X^T \}.&s=3$






    Positive semidefinite matrix: $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$ satisfying $latex z^T X z \geq 0&s=3$ for all $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
    Equivalently, $latex X&s=3$ only has nonnegative eigenvalues.
    If $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$ is positive semidefinite, then write $latex X \succeq 0 &s=3$.
    If $latex X,Y \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$ and $latex X-Y\succeq0 &s=3$, then write $latex X \succeq Y &s=3$.
    Set of symmetric positive semidefinite matrices:

    $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^n = \{ X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n: X \succeq 0 \}.&s=3$

    (N.B.: $latex \succeq&s=3$ is not the same as component-wise inequality, as was the case for vectors.)




    Positive definite matrix: $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$ satisfying $latex z^T X z = 0&s=3$ if and only if $latex z=0&s=3$.
    Equivalently, $latex X&s=3$ only has positive eigenvalues.
    If $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n_+ &s=3$ is positive definite, then write $latex X \succ 0 &s=3$.
    If $latex X,Y \in \boldsymbol{S}^n_+ &s=3$ and $latex X-Y\succ0 &s=3$, then write $latex X \succ Y &s=3$.
    Set of symmetric positive definite matrices:

    $latex \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n = \{ X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n: X \succ 0 \}.&s=3$







    Example 1 Let $latex A = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 4 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$.
    Since $latex A &s=3$ has positive eigenvalues $latex \{2,4\} &s=3$, it follows that $latex A \succ 0 &s=3$.
    To see $latex A \succ 0 &s=3$ explicitly, observe

    $latex \begin{aligned} z^T A z &= \begin{bmatrix} z_1 & z_2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 0\\ 0 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1 \\ z_2 \end{bmatrix}\\ &= 2 z_1^2 + 4 z_2^2\\ &\geq0 \end{aligned} &s=3$


    with $latex z^T A z = 0 &s=3$ iff $latex z = \begin{bmatrix}0\\0\end{bmatrix} &s=3$.



    Example 2. Let $latex B = \begin{bmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}&s=3$.
    Since $latex B &s=3$ has nonnegative eigenvalues $latex \{ 4,0 \} &s=3$, it follows that $latex B \succeq 0 &s=3$.
    To see $latex B \succeq 0 &s=3$ explicitly, observe

    $latex \begin{aligned} z^TBz &= \begin{bmatrix}z_1 & z_2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1 \\ z_2 \end{bmatrix}\\ &=4 z_1^2\\ &\geq 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Evidently, $latex z^T B z=0 &s=3$ for $latex z = \begin{bmatrix} 0\\z_2 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$ and so $latex B \not\succ 0&s=3$.



    Example 3. Let $latex C =\begin{bmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 2 & 3 \end{bmatrix} \succ 0 &s=3$.
    One can conclude $latex C \succ0 &s=3$ by showing that $latex C &s=3$ has positive eigenvalues $latex \{ 5,1 \} &s=3$.
    To see it directly, compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} z^T C z &= \begin{bmatrix}z_1 & z_2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 3&2\\2&3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1 \\z_2 \end{bmatrix}\\ &= 3z_1^2 + 4z_1z_2 + 3z_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    But the discriminant (with respect to $latex z_1 &s=3$) of this quadratic satisfies $latex -20 z_2^2 \leq 0 &s=3$, from which we conclude the polynomial is positive unless $latex z_1=z_2=0 &s=3$ and hence $latex C \succ 0 &s=3$.



    Example 4. Let $latex D = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$.
    We can conclude $latex D \not\succeq 0 &s=3$, either compute its eigenvalues $latex \{3,-1\} &s=3$ or observe that $latex \det D = 1 - 4 = -3 &s=3$ and whence $latex D &s=3$ cannot have only nonnegative eigenvalues.















    Positive Semidefinite Cone Proposition 1. $latex \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$ is a $latex \frac{n(n+1)}{2}&s=3$-dimensional real vector space and $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^n&s=3$ is a convex cone in $latex \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$.
    Proof.
    Step 1. $latex \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$ is a vector space: if $latex X,Y \in \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$ and $latex c \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$, then it is easy to see:

    $latex (X+cY)^T = X^T + cY^T = X + cY&s=3$.

    and so $latex X+cY \in \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$.


    Step 2. $latex \text{dim}\,\boldsymbol{S}^n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}&s=3$: since $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$ implies $latex X=X^T&s=3$, we have the identification

    $latex \begin{aligned} X&= \begin{bmatrix} \boldsymbol{x_{11}} & \boldsymbol{x_{12}} & \boldsymbol{x_{13}} & \cdots & \boldsymbol{x_{1n}}\\ x_{12} & \boldsymbol{x_{22}} & \boldsymbol{x_{23}} &\cdots & \boldsymbol{x_{2n}}\\ x_{13} & x_{23} & \boldsymbol{x_{33}} &\cdots & \boldsymbol{x_{3n}}\\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ x_{1n} & x_{2n} & x_{3n} & \cdots & \boldsymbol{x_{nn}}\\ \end{bmatrix}\\ &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\iff\\ \xi&:= \begin{bmatrix} x_{11} & x_{12} & \cdots & x_{1n} & x_{22} & x_{23} & \cdots & x_{2n} & \cdots & x_{nn} \end{bmatrix}^T, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    where the bolded entries in $latex X&s=3$ indicate the unique contributions to making $latex \xi&s=3$.
    Counting the number of bold entries shows $latex \xi&s=3$ has $latex \frac{n(n+1)}{2}&s=3$ entries and hence $latex \xi \in \mathbb{R}^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}&s=3$.


    Step 3. $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^n&s=3$ is a convex cone: For $latex \theta_1,\theta_2\geq0&s=3$, $latex X,Y \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n&s=3$ and $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ there holds

    $latex z^T(\theta_1 X +\theta_2 Y)z = \theta_1 z^T X z + \theta_2 z^T Y z \geq 0&s=3$,

    and so $latex \theta_1 X + \theta_2 Y \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$.
    By the proposition in Convex Geometry.Cones, we conclude the desired result.










    Proposition 2. $latex \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix} \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^2&s=3$ iff

    $latex a,c\geq0&s=3$ and $latex \det \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix} = ac - b^2 \geq 0&s=3$.

    Proof.
    Step 1. Let

    $latex x = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \end{bmatrix} \quad \text{and} \quad X = \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix}, &s=3$


    recalling that $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^2&s=3$ iff $latex x^T X x \geq 0&s=3$ for all $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^2&s=3$.

    Step 2. (Case $latex a=0 &s=3$) First compute

    $latex x^T X x = \begin{bmatrix}x_1 & x_2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix} x_1\\x_2\end{bmatrix} = 2bx_1x_2+cx_2^2. &s=3$

    Observe that

    $latex 2bx_1x_2 + cx_2^2 \geq 0 &s=3$ for all $latex x_1,x_2 \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ iff $latex b=0,c\geq0 &s=3$.

    Note (in case $latex a=0 &s=3$): $latex ac - b^2 = 0 &s=3$ iff $latex b=0 &s=3$.
    Can thus conclude (in case $latex a=0 &s=3$):

    $latex x^TXx \geq 0 &s=3$ for all $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ iff $latex c\geq0, \det X =0 &s=3$.



    Step 3. (Case $latex a \neq 0 &s=3$) Completing the square gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^T X x &= \begin{bmatrix}x_1&x_2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_1\\x_2\end{bmatrix}\\ &=ax_1^2 + 2bx_1 x_2 + c x_2^2 \\ &= a(x_1 + a^{-1}bx_2)^2 + a^{-1}\det\, X\, x_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    But

    $latex a(x_1 + a^{-1}bx_2)^2 + a^{-1}\det\, X\, x_2^2 \geq0 &s=3$ for all $latex x_1,x_2 \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$

    iff

    $latex a>0 &s=3$ and $latex \det\,X \geq0 &s=3$.

    (To conclude $latex a>0 &s=3$, take $latex x_2=0&s=3$.)
    N.B.: strictly speaking, $latex c\geq0 &s=3$ was not used anywhere; however, $latex X \succeq0 &s=3$ immediately implies $latex c \geq0 &s=3$, and $latex a>0,ac-b^2 \geq0 &s=3$ also implies $latex c\geq0 &s=3$.

    Step 4. Putting Steps 2. and 3. together, we conclude:
    $latex \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix} \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^2&s=3$ iff

    $latex a,c\geq0&s=3$ and $latex \det \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\b&c \end{bmatrix} = ac - b^2 \geq 0&s=3$.











    Image of $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^2&s=3$ In light of Proposition 1 and Proposition 2, we can plot

    $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^2 \subset \boldsymbol{S}^2 \cong \mathbb{R}^3&s=3$.

    The image below depicts the boundary of

    $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^2 \cong \{ (x,y,z): xz \geq y^2 ,x\geq0,z\geq0\}&s=3$.
















    Separating Hyperplanes Let $latex A,B \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be two sets.
    Separating hyperplane: a hyperplane given by

    $latex P_{a,b} := \{ x : a^T x = b \} &s=3$

    for some $latex a \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex b \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ such that

    $latex \begin{aligned} a^Tx -b \geq 0 & \text{ on } A\\ a^Tx - b \leq 0 & \text{ on } B \end{aligned}. &s=3 $

    $latex P_{a,b} &s=3$ is said to separate $latex A &s=3$ and $latex B &s=3$.
    Thus, $latex P_{a,b} &s=3$ cuts $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ into two halfspaces with one containing all of $latex A &s=3$ and the other containing all of $latex B &s=3$.
    Separating Hyperplane Theorem. If $latex A,B \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ are two disjoint convex sets, then there exists $latex a \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex b \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ such that $latex P_{a,b} &s=3$ is a separating hyperplane which separates $latex A &s=3$ and $latex B &s=3$.
    Example 1. Consider the convex sets

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \{(x,y) : \left(x-1\right)^{2}+2\left(y-1\right)^{2\ }\leq1 \}\\ B &= \{(x,y) : \left(x+1\right)^{2\ }+\ \left(y+1\right)^{2\ }\leq1\}\\ C &= \{(x,y) : \left(x-2\right)^{2}+2\left(y-1\right)^{2\ }\leq1 \}\\ P:&=P_{(1,1),0} = \{(x,y) : y+x=0 \} \end{aligned}. &s=2 $

    These three sets are indicated in the image below.
    Note that $latex P &s=3$ separates the pairs $latex [B,A] &s=3$ and $latex [B,C] &s=3$. Moreover, the pair $latex [A,C] &s=3$ cannot be separated since $latex A &s=3$ and $latex C &s=3$ have significant overlap.
    Example 2. Consider the convex sets

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \{(x,y) : x^{2\ }+\ y^{2}<1 \}\\ B &= \{(x,y) : \left(x+2\right)^{2\ }+y^{2}<1 \}\\ P:&=P_{(1,0),-1} = \{(x,y) : x=-1 \} \end{aligned}. &s=2 $

    These sets are indicated in the image below.
    First note $latex A \cap B = \emptyset &s=3$ since neither contain their boundaries.
    As such, they have a separating hyperplane which is given by $latex P &s=3$.

    N.B.: Replacing $latex A,B &s=3$ with their respective closures $latex \overline{A},\overline{B} &s=3$, the plane $latex P &s=3$ still separates $latex \overline{A},\overline{B} &s=3$.
    Indeed, $latex x+1 \geq 0 &s=3$ for $latex (x,y) \in A &s=3$ and $latex x+1 \leq 0 &s=3$ for $latex (x,y) \in B &s=3$.















    Supporting Hyperplanes Let $latex A \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be a fixed set and fix a boundary point

    $latex x_0 \in \text{bd}A := \overline{A} \setminus \text{int}A &s=3$.

    If the plane

    $latex P_{a,a^Tx_0} = \{ x : a^Tx = a^Tx_0 \} &s=3$

    separates $latex A &s=3$ and the singleton $latex \{x_0\} &s=3$, then $latex P_{a,a^Tx_0} &s=3$ is called the supporting hyperplane of $latex A &s=3$ at $latex x_0 &s=3$.
    Equivalently, $latex A &s=3$ lies entirely in a halfspace with boundary given by $latex P_{a,a^Tx_0} &s=3$.

    (Here: $latex \overline{A} &s=3$ indicates the closure of $latex A &s=3$ and $latex \text{int} A &s=3$ indicates its interior.)

    Example. Consider the convex sets

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \{ (x,y) : (x-2)^2 + (y-2)^2 \leq 1 \}\\ P :&= P_{(-1,0),-1} = \{ (x,y) : x = 1 \} \end{aligned} &s=2$

    with boundary point $latex x_0 = (1,2) \in \partial A &s=3$.
    Letting $latex a = \begin{bmatrix} -1\\0\end{bmatrix} &s=3$, we note $latex a^T x_0 + 1 = 0 \geq 0 &s=3$.
    Next, observing that, if $latex (x,y) \in A &s=3$, then $latex x \geq 1 &s=3$ and so

    $latex a^Tx + 1 = \begin{bmatrix} -1&0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x\\y \end{bmatrix} +1 = -x +1 \leq 0&s=3$.

    Thus $latex P &s=3$ separates $latex A &s=3$ and $latex \{ x_0 \} &s=3$, showing that $latex P &s=3$ is a supporting hyperplane of $latex A &s=3$ at the boundary point $latex x_0 &s=3$; see image below.















    Hulls Let $latex X \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ be a fixed subset.
    Convex hull: the set

    $latex \{ \theta_1 x_1 + \cdots + \theta_k x_k : x_i \in X,\, \theta_i \geq 0,\, i = 1,\ldots,k,\, \theta_1 + \cdots + \theta_k= 1\}&s=1$.

    This is just the collection of all convex combinations of points in $latex X&s=3$ and is itself convex.
    Example: The images below depict a set of three points and its convex hull.


    Affine hull: the set

    $latex \{ \theta_1 x_1 + \cdots + \theta_k x_k : x_i \in X,\, i = 1,\ldots,k,\, \theta_1 + \cdots + \theta_k= 1\}&s=1$.

    This is just the collection of all affine combinations of points in $latex X&s=3$ and is itself affine.
    Example: The images below depict two points and their affine hull.
      


    Conic hull: The set

    $latex \{ \theta_1 x_1 + \cdots + \theta_k x_k : x_i \in X,\, \theta_i \geq 0,\, i = 1,\ldots,k\}&s=1$.

    This is just the collection of all conic combinations of points in $latex X&s=3$ and is itself a cone.
    Example: The images below depict two points $latex x_1 = (0.5,1) &s=3$, $latex x_2 = (1.5,0.5) &s=3$ and their conic hull.
      
    Details To see that the conic hull really is the shaded region, note that, by taking $latex \theta_1 = t s_1 &s=3$ and $latex \theta_2 = (1-t)s_2 &s=3$, where $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$ and $latex s\geq0 &s=3$, the conic hull contains all points of the form $latex ts_1x_1 + (1-t)s_2x_2 &s=3$.
    Thus, it contains all line segments connecting any two points on the nonnegative rays $latex \{ s x_1 : s \geq 0 \} &s=3$ and $latex \{ s x_2 : s \geq 0 \} &s=3$.






    N.B.:
    1. Conic hulls are convex cones.
    2. Taking the "___ hull" of $latex X&s=3$ does indeed result in a "___" set.
    3. The "___ hull" is a construction of the smallest "___" subset containing $latex X&s=3$.















    Generalized Inequalities Proper cone: a convex cone $latex K \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ satisfying
    1. $latex K &s=3$ is closed (i.e., $latex K &s=3$ contains its boundary)
    2. $latex K &s=3$ has nonempty interior
    3. $latex x,-x \in K \implies x=0 &s=3$.
    Generalized inequality: given a proper cone $latex K &s=3$, a partial ordering $latex \prec_K &s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ defined by

    $latex x \preceq_K y \iff y-x \in K &s=3$.

    N.B.: $latex \preceq_K &s=3$ is a partial ordering and so $latex x \preceq_K y &s=3$ is not well-defined for all $latex x,y &s=3$. Generalized strict inequality: given a proper cone $latex K &s=3$, a partial ordering $latex \prec_K &s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ defined by

    $latex x \prec_K y \iff y-x \in \text{int}\,K &s=3$.

    Examples
    1. (CO Example 2.14)
      If $latex K = \mathbb{R}^n_+ &s=3$, then $latex \preceq_K &s=3$ is the standard componentwise vector inequality:

      $latex v \preceq w \iff v_i \leq w_i, \, i = 1,\ldots,n &s=3$.

      N.B.: $latex \preceq_{\mathbb{R}_+}&s=3$ is the standard inequality on $latex \mathbb{R} &s=3$.
    2. (CO Example 2.15)
      If $latex K = \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$, then

      $latex \begin{aligned} A &\preceq_K B \implies B-A \text{ is positive semidefinite}\\ A &\prec_K B \implies B-A \text{ is positive definite} \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    3. Let

      $latex K = \{(x_1,x_2) : x_1 \leq 2x_2, x_2 \leq 2x_1 \} &s=3$.

      Then $latex K &s=3$ is a proper cone.
      In the image below:
      • $latex K &s=3$ is the cone with vertex $latex (0,0) &s=3$.
      • The cone with vertex $latex x = (-1,1) &s=3$ depicts those $latex y \in \mathbb{R}^2 &s=3$ with $latex x \preceq_K y &s=3$.
      • The cone with vertex $latex x = (1,-1) &s=3$ depicts those $latex y \in \mathbb{R}^2 &s=3$ with $latex x \preceq_K y &s=3$.
      N.B.: $latex (0,2)-(-1,1) = (1,1) \in K &s=3$, and so $latex (1,1) \preceq_K (0,2) &s=3$, as indicated in the image.
      Moreover, $latex (-1,1) &s=3$ and $latex (1,-1) &s=3$ are not comparable.















    Convex Function Theory
    Conventions and Notations
    1. Writing $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ always means a partial function with domain $latex \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ possibly smaller than $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
      "Function" will mean "partial function."
    2. If $latex \text{dom}\, f \neq \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, we may work with the extension $latex \tilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} \cup \{ +\infty \}&s=3$ given by

      $latex \tilde{f}(x) = \begin{cases} f(x) & x \in \text{dom}\, f\\ +\infty & x \notin \text{dom}\, f \end{cases}.&s=3$

      It is common to implicitly assume $latex f &s=3$ has been extended and to write $latex f &s=3$ for the partial function $latex f&s=3$ and its extension $latex \tilde{f}&s=3$.
    3. Given a set $latex C \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, its indicator function is

      $latex \tilde{I}_C = \begin{cases} 0 & x \in C\\ +\infty & x \notin C \end{cases}.&s=3$

    4. We write

      $latex \begin{aligned} \mathbb{R}_+ &:= \{ x \in \mathbb{R}: x\geq0 \}\\ \mathbb{R}_{++} &:= \{ x \in \mathbb{R}: x > 0 \} \end{aligned}. &s=3$
















    Convex Functions Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ be a function with convex domain $latex \text{dom}\, f&s=3$.
    Convexity: for all $latex x,y \in \text{dom}\, f, t \in [0,1]&s=3$ there holds

    $latex f(t x + (1-t)y) \leq t f(x) + (1-t)f(y)&s=3$

    (This inequality is often called Jensen's inequality.)



    Strict convexity: for all $latex x,y \in \text{dom}f,\, x\neq y, t \in (0,1)&s=3$ there holds

    $latex f(t x + (1-t)y) < t f(x) + (1-t)f(y)&s=3$.


    Example: failure of strict convexity In the figure, the solid line indicates part of the graph of $latex x^4 &s=3$ and the dashed line indicates part of the graph of a linear function.
    This function fails to be everywhere strictly convex due to linear functions satisfying

    $latex f(tx + (1-t)y) = tf(x) + (1-t)f(y) &s=3$.





    Concavity and strict concavity: when $latex -f&s=3$ is, respectively, convex and strictly convex.







    Remarks.
    1. It is instructive to compare convexity/concavity with linearity and view the former as weak versions of linearity.

    2. It is common to extend the definition of convexity to extended functions, i.e., those of the form $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} \cup \{+\infty\} &s=3$.

      For example, the indicator function $latex \tilde{I}_{(-\infty,2)} &s=3$ is convex in this sense.
      To give insight, consider the image below, where the thick line is the "graph" of $latex \tilde{I}_{(-\infty,2)} &s=3$ and the dashed line is the "secant line" connecting the points $latex (1,0) &s=3$ to $latex (x,\infty) &s=3$ for any $latex x\geq 2 &s=3$.
















    Examples
    1. All linear functions are convex and concave on their domains.
    2. $latex e^x&s=3$ is convex on $latex \mathbb{R}&s=3$.
    3. $latex |x|^p&s=3$ is convex on $latex \mathbb{R} &s=3$ for $latex p \geq 1 &s=3$.
    4. $latex x^p&s=3$ is convex on $latex \mathbb{R}_{++}&s=3$ for $latex p \geq 1&s=3$ or $latex p \leq 0&s=3$ and concave for $latex 0 \leq p \leq 1&s=3$.
    5. $latex - \log\det X&s=3$ is convex on $latex \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n&s=3$
    6. If $latex C \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ is convex, then its indicator function $latex \tilde{I}_C&s=3$ is convex (in the extended value sense).















    One Dimensional Characterization Proposition. Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ have convex domain and, given

    $latex x \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$ and $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$,

    define the function $latex g:\mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ by

    $latex g(t) = f(x+tv)&s=3$

    with

    $latex \text{dom}\,g := \{ t \in \mathbb{R} : x + tv \in \text{dom}\, f \}&s=3$.

    Then $latex f &s=3$ is convex iff $latex g &s=3$ is convex for all $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ and $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ such that $latex g &s=3$ is well-defined.
    Proof.
    Step 1. First note that $latex \text{dom}\,g &s=3$ is convex: it is the intersection of $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ with the line passing through $latex x &s=3 &s=3$ with direction $latex v &s=3$.



    Step 2. ($latex \implies &s=3$) Suppose $latex f &s=3$ is convex and let $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ and $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be arbitrary.
    Then, for $latex \theta \in [0,1] &s=3$ and $latex t_1,t_2 \in \text{dom}\, g&s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\theta t_1 + (1-\theta) t_2) &= f(x + (\theta t_1 + (1-\theta)t_2)v)\\ &= f(x + \theta t_1 v + (1-\theta) t_2 v)\\ &= f(\theta(x + t_1 v) + (1-\theta)(x+t_2 v) ) \\ &\leq \theta f(x+t_1 v) + (1-\theta)f(x + t_2 v)\\ &= \theta g(t_1) + (1-\theta)g(t_2), \end{aligned} &s=3$

    proving that $latex g &s=3$ is convex.



    Step 3. ($latex \impliedby &s=3$) Suppose now that each $latex g &s=3$ is convex.
    Fix $latex x,y \in \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ and let $latex \theta \in [0,1] &s=3$.
    We want to show

    $latex f(\theta x + (1-\theta)y) \leq \theta f(x) + (1-\theta)f(y) &s=3$.

    Let $latex v = y-x &s=3$ and

    $latex g(t) = f(x + t(y-x))&s=3$,

    noting that $latex g(0)=f(x), g(1)=f(y)&s=3$.
    Since $latex g &s=3$ is convex, we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(\theta x + (1-\theta)y) &= f(x + (1-\theta)(y-x))\\ &= g(1-\theta)\\ &=g(\theta \cdot 0 + (1-\theta)\cdot 1)\\ &\leq \theta g(0) + (1-\theta) g(1)\\ &=\theta f(x) + (1-\theta) f(y). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    This is enough to conclude $latex f &s=3$ is convex.















    First Order Characterization Proposition. If $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ is differentiable with convex domain $latex \text{dom}\, f&s=3$, then $latex f&s=3$ is convex iff

    $latex f(x) + \nabla f(x)^{T}(y-x) \leq f(y), \quad \forall x,y \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$.


    Proof (sketch). We prove it in case $latex f:\mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$; the higher dimensional case follows by using that $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ with convex domain is convex iff it is convex as a single variable function when restricted to lines intersecting $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$.
    Throughout, let $latex x,y \in \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ and $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$.
    Step 1. ($latex \implies &s=3$) If $latex f &s=3$ is convex, then we obtain the following inequalities

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(ty + (1-t)x) \leq tf(y) + (1-t)f(x)\quad&\text{(convexity)}\\ f(x) + \frac{f(x+t(y-x))-f(x)}{t} \leq f(y)\quad&\text{(rearranging)}\\ f(x) + \frac{f(x+t(y-x))-f(x)}{t(y-x)}(y-x) \leq f(y)\quad &\text{(rearranging)}\\ f(x) + f'(x)(y-x) \leq f(y)\quad&\text{(taking }t \to 0 \text{)}. \end{aligned} &s=2$





    Step 2. ($latex \impliedby &s=3$) Supposing

    $latex f(x) + f'(x)(y-x) \leq f(y) &s=3$

    we set $latex z = tx + (1-t)y &s=3$ and add the two inequalities

    $latex \begin{aligned} t f(z) + tf'(z)(x - z) &\leq tf(x)\\ (1-t) f(z) + (1-t)f'(z)(y - z) &\leq (1-t)f(y) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    to obtain

    $latex f(tx+(1-t)y)=f(z) \leq tf(x) + (1-t)f(y). &s=3$








    Remarks.
    1. For fixed $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$, the mapping

      $latex A_x: \, y \mapsto f(x) + \nabla f(x)^{T}(y-x) &s=3$

      is affine whose graph is a hyperplane passing through the point $latex (x,f(x)) &s=3$.
      Therefore, the inequality $latex A_x(y) \leq f(y) &s=3$ means this hyperplane is a tangent plane at $latex (x,f(x)) &s=3$ of the graph of $latex f &s=3$ lying under the graph of $latex f &s=3$.
      In fact, this plane is a supporting hyperplane of the epigraph

      $latex \text{epi}\,f := \{ (x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^{n} \times \mathbb{R} : x \in \text{dom}\,f, t \geq f(x)\} &s=2$

      at the point $latex (x,f(x)) &s=3$.


    2. The affine mapping $latex A_x &s=3$ is just the first order Taylor approximation of $latex f &s=3$ at $latex x &s=3$.
      Thus, differential convex functions are such that their first order Taylor approximations serve as a global underestimators of $latex f &s=3$.

    Example. In the image below:
    • solid line is the graph of $latex f(x) = e^x &s=3$;
    • shaded region is the convex set given by $latex \text{epi}\,f = \{ (x,t): t\geq e^x \} &s=3$;
    • dashed line is the supporting hyperplane at $latex (-1,e^{-1}) &s=3$ given by the graph of $latex e^{-1} + e^{-1}(x+1) &s=3$.

















    Second Order Characterization Proposition. If $latex f&s=3$ is twice-differentiable with $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ convex, then $latex f&s=3$ is convex iff

    $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \succeq 0, \quad \forall x \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$.


    Recall: if $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ is twice-differentiable, then its Hessian is

    $latex \nabla^2 f(x) = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1^2}(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1 \partial x_2}(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1 \partial x_n}(x) \\ \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2 \partial x_1 }(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2^2 }(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2 \partial x_n}(x) \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n \partial x_1}(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n \partial x_2}(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n^2}(x) \\ \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}. &s=2$


    Proof (sketch).
    Step 0. The proof is a little more involved, so let us just give two intuitive justifications.



    Justification 1. The second order Taylor approximation gives

    $latex f(y) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T (y-x) + \frac{1}{2} (y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)(y-x) &s=2$

    up to some small error. But

    $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \succeq 0 &s=3$

    implies

    $latex (y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)(y-x) \geq0 &s=3$

    and so

    $latex f(y) \geq f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T (y-x) &s=3$

    (again, up to some small error).
    The first order approximation from Convex Function Theory.First Order Characterization then implies convexity.



    Justification 2. Another intuitive justification is that $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \succeq 0 &s=3$ means the graph of $latex f &s=3$ curves everywhere upward like a paraboloid, which evidently suggests convexity.





    Remarks.
    1. Recall: for $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=3$, there holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2 f(x) \succeq 0 & \iff \nabla^2 f(x) \text{ is positive semidefinite}\\ & \iff \nabla^2 f(x) \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n. \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    2. $latex \nabla^2 f (x) \succ 0&s=3$ for all $latex x \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$ implies $latex f&s=3$ is strictly convex.
      Converse is false since $latex x^4 &s=3$ is strictly convex.
















    Level Sets Fix a function $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ and let $latex c \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$
    $latex c&s=3$-Level set: the set

    $latex S(c):=\{ x \in \text{dom}\, f : f(x) = c\}&s=3$.

    The figure below depicts level sets of $latex x^2 + e^{y^2} &s=3$ with $latex c=5,10,17,26. &s=3$


    $latex c&s=3$-Sublevel set: the set

    $latex S_c = \{ x \in \text{dom}\,f : f(x) \leq c\}&s=3.$

    The figure below depicts the sublevel sets of $latex x^2+e^{y^2} &s=3$ with $latex c=5,10,17,26&s=3$.
    Each shade of gray indicates a new sublevel set and of course $latex S_c \subset S_{c'} &s=3$ for $latex c<c' &s=3$.


    $latex c&s=3$-Superlevel set: the set

    $latex S^c = \{ x \in \text{dom}\,f : f(x) \geq c \}&s=3$.

    The figure below depicts the superlevel sets of $latex x^2+e^{y^2} &s=3$ with $latex c=5,10,17,26&s=3$.
    Each shade of gray indicates a new superlevel set and of course $latex S^c \supset S^{c'} &s=3$ for $latex c<c' &s=3$.





    Proposition. If $latex f&s=3$ is convex, then the sublevel set $latex S_c&s=3$ is convex for all $latex c \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$.
    Equivalently, if $latex f&s=3$ is concave, then the superlevel set $latex S^c&s=3$ is convex for all $latex c \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$.

    Proof. Want to show: $latex x,y \in S_c &s=3$ implies $latex tx + (1-t)y \in S_c &s=3$ for all $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$.
    If $latex x,y \in S_c &s=3$, then $latex f(x),f(y) \leq c &s=3$ and so convexity of $latex f&s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(tx+(1-t)y) &\leq t f(x) + (1-t)f(y) \\ &\leq t c + (1-t)c \\ &= c \end{aligned} &s=3$


    and hence $latex tx + (1-t)y \in S_c &s=3$ as desired.















    Graphs Fix a function $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$.
    Graph: the set

    $latex \{ (x,f(x)):x \in \text{dom}\,f \} \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}&s=3$.

    Example: graph of $latex e^x &s=3$ is given below.


    Epigraph: the set

    $latex \text{epi}\, f = \{ (x,t): x \in \text{dom}\, f, f(x) \leq t \} \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}.&s=2$

    Example: epigraph of $latex e^x &s=3$ is given below.


    Hypograph: the set

    $latex \text{hypo}\, f = \{ (x,t) : x \in \text{dom}\, f, f(x) \geq t \} \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}. &s=2$

    Example: hypograph of $latex e^x &s=3$ is given below.





    Proposition. $latex f&s=3$ is convex iff $latex \text{epi}\, f&s=3$ is convex.
    Equivalently, $latex f&s=3$ is concave iff $latex \text{hypo}\, f&s=3$ is convex.
    Proof. (sketch) We consider the case $latex f:\mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ for simplicity.
    Step 1. ($latex \implies &s=3$) Suppose $latex f &s=3$ is convex and let $latex x,y \in \text{epi}\,f &s=3$ be distinct points.
    If $latex x,y &s=3$ both lie on a vertical line, then clearly $latex tx + (1-t)y \in \text{epi}\,f &s=3$ for $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$; thus, suppose otherwise.
    Let $latex \ell &s=3$ be the line passing through $latex x,y &s=3$ and let $latex x',y' &s=3$ be the two intersection points of $latex \ell &s=3$ with the graph of $latex f &s=3$.
    (If at most one intersection point exists, then it is easy to see that the line connecting $latex x&s=3$ and $latex y&s=3$ is in $latex \text{epi}\,f &s=3$.)
    By convexity of $latex f &s=3$, the line formed by $latex tx' + (1-t)y' &s=3$ for $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$ lies in $latex \text{epi}\,f &s=3$, which is enough to conclude the line given by $latex tx + (1-t)y &s=3$ for $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$ lies in $latex \text{epi}\,f&s=3$.
    This shows $latex \text{epi}\, f &s=3$ is convex.
    Step 2. ($latex \impliedby &s=3$) Suppose now $latex \text{epi}\,f &s=3$ is convex.
    Let $latex x,y &s=3$ be two distinct points on the graph of $latex f &s=3$.
    Then $latex x,y \in \text{epi}\, f&s=3$.
    But convexity of $latex \text{epi}\,f&s=3$ implies the line formed by $latex tx+(1-t)y &s=3$ for $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$ lies entirely in $latex \text{epi}\,f&s=3$.
    This is enough to conclude $latex f &s=3$ is convex.















    Convex Calculus The following list details some operations and actions that preserves convexity.
    The main point: to conclude a function $latex f&s=3$ is convex, often one verifies $latex f&s=3$ may be built by other convex functions using, for example, the operations below.
    N.B.: Conclusions only holds on common domains of the functions.
    Conical combinations:

    $latex f_1,\ldots,f_m&s=3$ convex and $latex c_1,\ldots,c_m \geq0&s=3$

    $latex \implies &s=3$

    $latex c_1 f_1 + \cdots + c_m f_m&s=3$ convex.





    Weighted averages:

    $latex f(x,y)&s=3$ convex in $latex x&s=3$, $latex w(y) \geq0&s=3$ $latex \implies&s=3$ $latex \int f(x,y) w(y) dy&s=3$ convex.





    Affine change of variables:

    $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ convex, $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times m}&s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$

    $latex \implies&s=3$

    $latex f(A x + b)&s=3$ convex.





    Maximum:

    $latex f_1,\ldots,f_m&s=3$ convex

    $latex \implies&s=3$

    $latex f(x) := \max\{f_1(x),\ldots,f_m(x)\}&s=3$ convex.





    Supremum:

    $latex f(x,a)&s=3$ convex in $latex x&s=3$ for each $latex a \in \mathcal{A}&s=3$

    $latex \implies&s=3$

    $latex h(x):=\sup\{f(x,a):a \in \mathcal{A}\}&s=3$ convex.



    Justification. For $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} h(tx + (1-t)y) &= \sup \{ f(tx + (1-t)y,a) : a \in \mathcal{A} \}\\ &\leq \sup \{ tf(x,a) + (1-t) f(y,a) : a \in \mathcal{A}\}\\ &\leq t \sup\{ f(x,a) :a \in \mathcal{A}\} + (1-t)\sup\{f(y,a):a \in \mathcal{A} \}\\ &=th(x) + (1-t)h(y). \end{aligned} &s=3$



    Example. Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(x)&:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} \text{ be given}\\ f(x,y)&:= y^T x - g(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: for each $latex x &s=3$, the mapping $latex y \mapsto f(x,y) &s=3$ is affine and hence convex.
    Thus

    $latex h(y):=\sup\{y^Tx - g(x) : x \in \text{dom}\,g \} &s=3$

    defines a convex function.




    Infimum:

    $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^m \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ convex in $latex (x,y)&s=3$

    $latex C \subset \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$ convex

    $latex \inf_{y \in C}f(x,y)&s=3$ finite for some $latex x&s=3$

    $latex \implies&s=3$

    $latex g(x):=\inf_{y \in C}f(x,y)&s=3$ convex on

    $latex \text{dom}\,g := \{ x : (x,y) \in \text{dom}\, f \text{ for some } y \in C\}&s=2$.




















    Fenchel conjugation Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ be given (not necessarily convex).
    Fenchel conjugate: $latex f^*(y) = \sup \{ y^T x - f(x) : x \in \text{dom}\,f\}&s=3$.

    N.B.:

    $latex \text{dom}\, f^* = \{ y \in \mathbb{R}^n : f^*(y) < \infty \} &s=3$;

    i.e., those $latex y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ for which $latex y^Tx - f(x) &s=3$ is bounded above on $latex \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ as a function of $latex x &s=3$.

    Intuition. Suppose $latex f: \mathbb{R}_+ \to \mathbb{R}_+ &s=3$ is a differentiable convex function denoting the cost to produce $latex x &s=3$ items.
    For a given unit price $latex y \in \mathbb{R}_+ &s=3$, the profit of selling $latex x &s=3$ units is

    $latex P(x,y) = yx - f(x) &s=3$.

    Thus $latex f^*(y) &s=3$ is just the optimal profit for selling at price $latex y &s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex f &s=3$ convex implies $latex P(\cdot,y) &s=3$ is concave for each $latex y &s=3$.
    Thus, $latex P(x,y_0) &s=3$ is maximal at $latex x_0 &s=3$ satisfying $latex P'(x_0,y_0) = 0 &s=3$, i.e., when $latex y_0 = f'(x_0) &s=3$.
    Viz.: the $latex x_0 &s=3$ where $latex f &s=3$ has slope $latex y_0 &s=3$.
    The tangent line through $latex (x_0,f(x_0))&s=3$ is then given by $latex y=y_0 (x - x_0) + f(x_0) &s=3$.
    Lastly, note that the $latex y&s=3$-intercept of this line is $latex -y_0x_0 + f(x_0)=-f^*(y_0) &s=3$,


    Remarks
    1. Often $latex f^* &s=3$ is just called the conjugate function of $latex f &s=3$.
    2. Since $latex f^*&s=3$ is the supremum of a family of affine functions, $latex f^*&s=3$ is always convex, even if $latex f&s=3$ is not.
      (Follows from Convex Function Theory.Convex Calculus.
    3. If
      • $latex f &s=3$ is convex
      • $latex \text{epi}\,f &s=3$ is a closed subset of $latex \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R} &s=3$,
      then $latex f^{**} = f &s=3$.


    Example. We will compute the conjugate function of

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) &= e^x\\ \text{dom}\,f &= \mathbb{R}. \end{aligned} &s=3$


    Thus, let

    $latex \begin{aligned} h_y(x) &= yx - f(x) \\ &= yx - e^x . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Case $latex y<0 &s=3$: $latex h_y(x) &s=3$ is unbounded on $latex \text{dom}\,f = \mathbb{R}&s=3$ since

    $latex h_y(x) \to +\infty &s=3$ as $latex x \to -\infty &s=3$.

    Thus

    $latex \sup\{yx-e^x: x \in \text{dom}\,f\} = +\infty &s=3$.



    Case $latex y>0 &s=3$: Compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} h_y'(x) &= y - e^x =0 \text{ when } x=\log y \\ h_y''(x)&=-e^x \leq 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus $latex x=\log y &s=3$ maximizes $latex h_y &s=3$ and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} \sup\{yx-e^x:x \in \text{dom}\,f\} &= \max\{yx-e^x:x \in \text{dom}\,f \}\\ &= h_y(\log y) \\ &= y\log y - y \end{aligned} &s=3$



    Case $latex y=0 &s=3$: Compute

    $latex h_0(x) = -e^x&s=3$,

    which evidently has least upper bound $latex 0 &s=3$ and so

    $latex \sup\{ -e^x : x \in \text{dom}\,f \} = 0. &s=3$



    Conclusion: Since

    $latex yx-e^x &s=3$

    is bounded on $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ only for $latex y \geq 0 &s=3$, it follows that

    $latex \text{dom}\, f^* = \mathbb{R}_{+} &s=3.$

    Putting everything together:

    $latex f^*(y) = \sup\{yx-e^x\} = y\log y - y&s=3$ for $latex y \in \mathbb{R}_+ &s=3$,

    where we take $latex 0 \log 0 = 0 &s=3$.















    Legendre Transform Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ be convex, differentiable and with $latex \text{dom}\,f = \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.
    Then, the Fenchel conjugate $latex f^* &s=3$ of $latex f &s=3$ is often called the Legendre transform of $latex f &s=3$.


    Proposition. If $latex f &s=3$ as above, $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex y = \nabla f(z)&s=3$, then

    $latex f^*(y) = z^T \nabla f(z) - f(z) &s=3$.

    Proof.
    Step 1. Let

    $latex h_y(x) = y^T x - f(x) &s=3$.

    and note

    $latex x^\star \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ maximizes $latex h_y &s=3$ iff $latex \nabla h_y(x^\star) = 0 &s=3$

    since $latex h_y &s=3$ is a sum of concave functions and hence concave.
    Step 2. Using Step 1. and

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla(y^T x) &= y\\ \nabla h_y(x) &= \nabla(y^Tx-f(x)) = y - \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    conclude

    $latex y = \nabla f(x^\star) &s=3$ iff $latex x^\star &s=3$ maximizes $latex h_y &s=3$.

    (In particular, $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ maximizes $latex h_{\nabla f(z)} &s=3$.)
    Step 3. Letting $latex z, y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ satisfy

    $latex \begin{aligned} y &= \nabla f(z) \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    and using Steps 1. and 2., we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} f^*(y) &= \sup \{ y^T x - f(x) : x \in \text{dom}\, f\}\\ &= \max \{ h_y(x) : x \in \text{dom}\,f \}\\ & = z^T \nabla f(z) - f(z) \end{aligned} &s=3$,

    as desired.


    Example 1. Let

    $latex f(x) = e^x &s=3$,

    and compute

    $latex f'(x) = e^x &s=3$.

    Given $latex z \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, let

    $latex y = f'(z) = e^z &s=3$; i.e., $latex z = \log y &s=3$.

    Thus

    $latex f^*(y) = z f'(z) - f(z) = y \log y - y &s=3$,

    which agrees with our calculation for $latex f^* &s=3$ in a previous example.


    Example 2. Fix $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$ and let

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) &= \frac{1}{2} x^T Q x\\ \text{dom}\,f&= \mathbb{R}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    We will compute

    $latex f^*(y) = \frac{1}{2}y^T Q^{-1}y &s=3$.

    Step 0. Observe
    1. $latex f &s=3$ is convex:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2 f(x) = Q \succ 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      (Justification) Consider case $latex n = 2 &s=3$.
      Let $latex Q = \begin{bmatrix}a&b\\b&d\end{bmatrix}&s=2$.
      Thus $latex f(x_1,x_2) = \frac{1}{2}(ax_1^2 + 2 b x_1 x_2 + c^2 x_2^2) &s=3$.
      Easy now to see $latex \nabla^2 f = Q &s=3$.


    2. $latex Q \succ 0 &s=3$ implies $latex Q &s=3$ is invertible since then $latex \det Q > 0 &s=3$.




    Step 1. Using

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x) &=\nabla(\frac{1}{2}x^TQx) \\ &= Qx, \end{aligned} &s=3 $

    we conclude

    $latex y = \nabla f(z) \iff y = Qz \iff z = Q^{-1}y. &s=3 $





    Step 2. Let $latex y = \nabla f(z) &s=3$.
    By preceding proposition and Step 1., there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} f^*(y) &= z^T \nabla f(z) - f(z)\\ &= (Q^{-1}y)^T y - \frac{1}{2}(Q^{-1}y)^TQ(Q^{-1}y)\\ &= y^T Q^{-1}y - \frac{1}{2} y^T Q^{-1} y\\ &= \frac{1}{2}y^T Q^{-1} y. \end{aligned} &s=3 $
















    Other Notions of Convexity There are two other important notions of convexity that we will return to if needed.
    Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ be given.

    Quasiconvexity: $latex \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ and the sublevel sets

    $latex \{x \in \text{dom}\,f: f(x) \leq \alpha \}&s=3$

    are convex for all $latex \alpha \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$.
    Features:
    1. Quasiconvex problems may sometimes be suitably approximated by convex problems.
    2. Local minima need not be global minima


    Log-convexity: $latex f>0 &s=3$ on $latex \text{dom}\, f&s=3$ and $latex \log f &s=3$ is convex; equivalently

    $latex f(tx+(1-t)y) \leq f(x)^tf(y)^{1-t} &s=3$ for all $latex t \in [0,1]&s=3$.
















    Generalized Convexity Let $latex K \subset \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$ be a proper cone and let $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$.

    $latex K &s=3$-convexity: for all $latex x,y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$, there holds

    $latex f(tx + (1-t)y) \preceq_K t f(x) + (1-t)f(y) &s=3$.

    Strict $latex K &s=3$-convexity: for all $latex x \neq y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex t \in (0,1) &s=3$, there holds

    $latex f(tx + (1-t)y) \prec_K t f(x) + (1-t)f(y) &s=3$.

    Examples
    1. (CO Example 3.47)
      Let $latex K = \mathbb{R}_+^n &s=3$.
      Then $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$ is $latex K&s=3$-convex iff: $latex \text{dom}\, f&s=3$ is convex and for all $latex x,y \in \text{dom}f &s=3$ and $latex t \in[0,1]&s=3$, there holds

      $latex f(tx+(1-t)y) \preceq tf(x) + (1-t)f(y) &s=3$

      which holds iff

      $latex f_i(tx+(1-t)y) \leq tf_i(x) + (1-t)f_i(y) &s=3$

      for each $latex i = 1,\ldots, m &s=3$, i.e., iff $latex f &s=3$ is component-wisely convex.
    2. (CO Example 3.48)
      A function $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \boldsymbol{S}^m &s=3$ is $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^m&s=3$-convex iff : $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ is convex and for all $latex x,y \in \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ and $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$, there holds

      $latex f(tx + (1-t)y) \preceq t f(x) + (1-t)f(y) &s=3$.

      N.B.:
      • this is a matrix inequality and $latex \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$-convexity is often called matrix convexity.
      • $latex f &s=3$ is matrix convex iff $latex z^Tf(x)z &s=3$ is convex for all $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$.
      • The two functions

        $latex \begin{aligned} \mathbb{R}^{n \times m} \ni X &\mapsto XX^T\\ \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n \ni X &\mapsto X^p, \quad 1 \leq p \leq 2, -1 \leq p \leq 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

        are matrix convex.
    Basics of Optimization Problems
    General Optimization Problems By an optimization problem (OP) we mean the following:

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize } & f_0(x) \quad \text{(objective)}\\ \text{subject to }& f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m \quad \text{(inequality constraints)}\\ & h_i(x) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p \quad \text{(equality constraints)} \end{cases}.&s=3$

    We call

    $latex f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ the objective function;
    $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ the optimization variable or parameters;
    $latex f_i:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$, $latex i=1,\ldots,m&s=3$, the inequality constraint functions; and
    $latex h_i:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$, $latex i=1,\ldots,p&s=3$, the equality constraint functions.

    The domain of (OP) is the intersection

    $latex D = \bigcap_{i=0}^{m} \text{dom} \, f_i \cap \bigcap_{i=1}^{p} \text{dom} \, h_i&s=3$.
















    Feasibility Consider an (OP) as above.
    Feasible point: those $latex x \in D&s=3$ satisfying

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x)&\leq 0\quad\text{for } i =1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x) &= 0 \text{ for }i=1,\ldots,p. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Feasible set: the subset $latex F \subset D &s=3$ consisting of the feasible points.
    Feasible problem: A problem with nonempty feasible set, i.e., $latex F \neq \emptyset&s=3$.
    Infeasible problem: A problem with empty feasible set; i.e., there are no $latex x \in D &s=3$ which satisfy the inequality and equality constraints.

    Remark.
    1. A feasible problem need not have a solution; e.g., $latex f(x) = e^x &s=3$ has no minimizer nor minimum on $latex \mathbb{R} &s=3$.
    2. An infeasible problem never has a solution--there are no parameters $latex x &s=3$ to even test.















    Basic Example Consider the problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize } & \log(1-x^2-y^2)\\ \text{subject to }& (x-1)^2+(y-1)^2-1\leq0\\ & (x-y-1)^2+(y-1)^2-1\leq0 \end{cases}.&s=3$

    The objective function is

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x,y) &= \log(1-x^2-y^2)\\ \text{dom}\,f_0 &= \{ (x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : x^2+y^2<1\} \end{aligned} &s=3$,

    The inequality constraint functions are

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_1(x,y) &=(x-1)^2 + (y-1)^2-1\\ f_2(x,y) &=(x-y-1)^2 + (y-1)^2 - 1\\ \text{dom}\,f_1 &= \text{dom}\,f_2 = \mathbb{R}^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    The domain of the problem is

    $latex D = \text{dom}\, f_0 \cap \text{dom}\, f_1 \cap \text{dom} f_2 = \{ x^2+y^2<1\}. &s=3$.

    The feasible set: Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \text{dom}\,f_0\\ B &= \{(x-1)^2+(y-1)^2-1\leq0\}\\ C &= \{(x-y-1)^2 +(y-1)^2-1\leq0\} \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    These three sets are depicted in the image below.
    Note that the darkest region given by $latex A \cap B \cap C &s=3$ is the feasible set.
    Can we solve the problem? Noting
    1. $latex \log(1-x^2-y^2) \to -\infty &s=3$ as $latex (x,y) &s=3$ approaches a point on the circle $latex \{ x^2+y^2 = 1 \} &s=3$, and
    2. such sequences exist in the feasible set,
    we conclude the problem does not have a solution.















    The Feasibility Problem Feasibility problem: Given an (OP) with

    $latex \begin{aligned} &\text{inequality constraint functions } f_i, i = 1,\ldots,m\\ &\text{equality constraint functions } h_i, i = 1, \ldots,p \end{aligned} &s=3$

    solve

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} & x\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, i = 1, \ldots, m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i = 1,\ldots, p \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Viz.: the feasibility problem determines whether the constraints are consistent.

    Example 1. The problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} & (x,y)\\ \text{subject to} &f_1(x,y) = x^2 + y^2 - 1 \leq 0\\ &f_2(x,y) = (x-1)^2 + y^2 - 1 \leq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    has a solution since the two inequality constraints describe two intersecting disks.
    This is depicted below.


    Example 2. The problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} & (x,y)\\ \text{subject to} &f_1(x,y) = x^2 + y^2 - 1 \leq 0\\ &f_2(x,y) = (x-1)^2 + y^2 - 1 \leq 0\\ &h_1(x,y) = (x-\frac{1}{2})^2 + y^2 - 1 =0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    has no solution since the circle given by $latex h_1=0 &s=3$ lies outside of the intersection of the two disks.
    This is depicted below, where the red circle is given by $latex h_1=0 &s=3$.















    Optimal Value and Solvability Recall:

    $latex \begin{aligned} F &= \text{ feasible set of problem}\\ D &= \text{ domain of problem }. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Optimal value: The value

    $latex p^\star = \inf \{ f_0(x) : x \in F \}&s=3$,

    i.e., $latex p^\star &s=3$ is the largest $latex p \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ such that $latex p\leq f_0(x) &s=3$ for all $latex x \in F &s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex p^\star \in \mathbb{R} \cup \{ -\infty, + \infty \} &s=3$.

    Example: Below depicts the graph of $latex f(x) = \frac{1}{x} &s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}_{++} &s=3$.
    Evidently, $latex \inf \{ \frac{1}{x} : x \in \mathbb{R}_{++} \} = 0 &s=3$.




    Solvable: When the problem satisfies

    there exists $latex x^\star \in F&s=3$ with $latex f_0(x^\star) = p^\star&s=3$,

    i.e., the minimum value $latex p^\star&s=3$ is attainable.

    Example: Below depicts the graph of a quartic $latex q(x) &s=3$.
    The problem of minimizing $latex q(x) &s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R} &s=3$ is solvable with solution given by the minimal point $latex A &s=3$.
    N.B.: Point $latex B &s=3$ is a local minimum and hence does not give a solution.






    Remarks.
    1. $latex p^\star = \min\{f_0(x):x \in F \} &s=3$ iff the (OP) is solvable.
      Indeed, $latex \min\{f_0(x):x \in F \} &s=3$ is not well-defined unless the (OP) is solvable.
    2. $latex p^\star&s=3$ need not be finite:
      $latex p^\star = -\infty&s=3$ if $latex f_0&s=3$ is unbounded below on the feasible set; and
      $latex p^\star = + \infty&s=3$ if the OP is infeasible.



















    Standard Form Optimization problems need not be placed in the form we defined them.
    We therefore introduce the following definition.

    (OP) in Standard form:

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize } & f_0(x) \\ \text{subject to }& f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m \\ & h_i(x) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}.&s=3$

    (This is how we defined (OP) before.)




    Example: Rewriting in standard form. We can recast more general optimization problems in standard form; e.g., consider

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{maximize } & F_0(x) \\ \text{subject to }& F_i(x) \leq G_i(x), \quad i=1,\ldots,m \\ & H_i(x) = K_i(x), \quad i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Indeed, taking
    $latex f_0 = -F_0&s=3$ (noting $latex \min f_0 = -\max F_0&s=3$)
    $latex f_i = F_i - G_i&s=3$ for $latex i=1,\ldots,m&s=3$
    $latex h_i = H_i - K_i&s=3$ for $latex i=1,\ldots,p&s=3$
    we readily recast (OP2) into the standard form (OP).















    Equivalent Problems Suppose we are given two OP's: (OP1) and (OP2).
    We say (OP1) and (OP2) are equivalent if: solving (OP1) allows one to solve (OP2), and vice versa.
    N.B.: Two problems being equivalent does not mean the problems are the same nor that they have the same solutions.

    Example. Consider the two problems:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x) = x^2\\ \text{subject to}& x \in [1,2] \end{cases} \quad \text{and} \quad \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&g(x) = (x+1)^2+1 \\ \text{subject to}&x \in [0,1] \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Observing

    $latex x^\star \in [1,2] &s=3$ minimizes $latex f(x) &s=3$ on $latex [1,2] &s=3$

    iff

    $latex x^\star - 1 \in [0,1] &s=3$ minimizes $latex g(x) &s=3$ on $latex [0,1] &s=3$,

    we readily see the two problems are equivalent.
    Indeed, if we find the solution $latex x^\star = 1 &s=3$ to the first problem, we readily obtain the solution $latex x^\star - 1 = 0 &s=3$ to the second problem, and vice versa.















    Change of Variables Suppose $latex \phi:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ is an injective function with $latex D \subset \phi(\text{dom} \,\phi)&s=3$.
    Then, under the change of variable $latex x \mapsto \phi(x) &s=3$, we have

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases} &s=3$

    is equivalent to

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(\phi(x))\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(\phi(x)) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(\phi(x)) = 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$

    N.B.: such a change of variables does not change the optimal value $latex p^\star &s=3$.
    Moreover, injectivity may be dropped.

    Justification. Indeed,
    • if $latex x &s=3$ solves (OP1), then $latex \phi^{-1}(x) &s=3$ solves (OP2).
      (More generally, $latex z &s=3$ such that $latex \phi(z) = x &s=3$ solves (OP2).)
    • if $latex z &s=3$ solves (OP2), then $latex \phi(z) &s=3$ solves (OP1).




    Example Consider the problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & e^x\\ \text{subject to} & \sqrt{x} - y \leq 0\\ &y-5 \leq 0\\ &x-5\leq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$.

    In the image below, the shaded region is the feasible set and the curve is the graph of $latex f_0(x) = e^x &s=3$. Consider the change of variables

    $latex \phi(x) = x^2 &s=3$.

    The objective and constraints change as follows:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x) = e^x & \to f_0(\phi(x)) = e^{x^2}\\ \sqrt{x}-y \leq 0 & \to |x| - y \leq 0\\ y-5 \leq 0 & \to y - 5 \leq 0\\ x - 5 \leq 0 & \to x^2 - 5 \leq 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The new feasible region and objective function are plotted below. Evidently, this change of variable changed a nonconvex (OP) into a convex one.















    Eliminating Linear Constraints Let $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}&s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$ and $latex x_0 \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ a solution to $latex Ax=b&s=3$.
    Let $latex B \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times k}&s=3$ be such that $latex \text{range}\, B = \text{kernel}\, A&s=3$.
    Then $latex Ax=b&s=3$ iff $latex x=By + x_0&s=3$ for some $latex y \in \mathbb{R}^{k}&s=3$.

    Consequently

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

    is equivalent to

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(By+x_0)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(By+x_0) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ \end{cases}. &s=3$

    N.B.: this can reduce dimension of problem by $latex \text{rank}\, A &s=3$ many variables. (Recall: $latex n = \text{rank}\,A + \text{null}\,A&s=3$.)

    Justification. Indeed,
    • if $latex x&s=3$ solves (OP1), then any $latex y \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$ with $latex x = By+x_0&s=3$ solves (OP2), and
    • if $latex y&s=3$ solves (OP2), then $latex x = By + x_0&s=3$ solves (OP1)


    Example. Consider the minimization problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & x^2 + y^2 \\ \text{subject to} &x\geq0\\ & y-x=1 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    We may eliminate the variable $latex y &s=3$ by simply using $latex y=x+1 &s=3$.

    But, to match with above: let

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x,y)=x^2 +y^2, &\quad A = \begin{bmatrix}-1&1\end{bmatrix}, \quad b = 1 \\ x_0 = \begin{bmatrix}0\\1\end{bmatrix},&\quad B = \begin{bmatrix} 1\\1\end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=2$.

    Thus

    $latex A \begin{bmatrix}x\\y\end{bmatrix}=b \iff \begin{bmatrix}x\\y \end{bmatrix} = Bt + x_0 = \begin{bmatrix}t\\t+1\end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    for some $latex t \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, and so

    $latex f_0(Bt + x_0) = f_0(t,t+1) = t^2 + (1+t)^2 &s=3$.



    Therefore, the minimization problem becomes

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & t^2 + (t+1)^2 \\ \text{subject to} &t\geq0\\ \end{cases}, &s=3$

    which has the obvious solution $latex t^\star = 0 &s=3$ with optimal value $latex p^\star = 1 &s=3$.
    Thus, the original problem has solution

    $latex \begin{bmatrix}x\\y\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}t\\t+1\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}0\\1\end{bmatrix} &s=3$.
















    Slack Variables Given $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ with $latex f(x) \leq 0 &s=3$, then there is a variable $latex s \geq 0 &s=3$ such that $latex f(x) + s = 0 &s=3$; such a variable $latex s&s=3$ is called a slack variable.

    Using slack variables $latex s_i,i=1\ldots,m &s=3$, the problem

    $latex \text{(OP1)}\begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i = 1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$

    is equivalent to the problem

    $latex \text{(OP2)}\begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & s_i \geq 0, i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & f_i(x)+s_i = 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$



    Remarks.
    1. All of the $latex x &s=3$ which may satisfy the constraints of (OP2) are the same as those which satisfy the constraints of (OP1); this justifies the equivalence.
    2. Let $latex F_1 &s=3$ be the feasible set of (OP1) and $latex F_2 &s=3$ that of (OP2).
      Then $latex F_1 \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex F_2 \subset \mathbb{R}^{n+m} &s=3$; i.e., the feasible sets are not the same object.
    3. Example: in the images below, the disk depicts a feasible set $latex F_1 = \{ x^2+y^2-1 \leq 0 \} \subset \mathbb{R}^2 &s=3$ and the paraboloid-type set depicts the feasible set $latex F_2 = \{x^2+y^2-1+s =0, s \geq 0 \} &s=3$ with slack variable $latex s &s=3$.
      N.B.: the permissible $latex (x,y) &s=3$ coordinates are the same for both sets.


    Main point: Solving the system of equations

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x)+s_i &= 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x) &= 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and considering only those solutions with $latex s_i \geq 0 &s=3$ may be easier than solving the system of inequalities

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x)&\leq0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x) &= 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{aligned}. &s=3$



    Example. Consider

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x,y)\\ \text{subject to} & a_1 x + b_1 y - c_1 \leq 0\\ & a_2 x + b_2 y - c_2 = 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Introduce slack variable $latex s\geq0&s=3$ satisfying

    $latex a_1 x + b_1 y - c_1 +s = 0&s=3$.

    Then (OP1) is equivalent to the problem

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x,y)\\ \text{subject to} & s\geq0\\ & a_1 x + b_1 y - c_1 + s = 0\\ & a_2 x + b_2 y - c_2 = 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Thus, finding feasible $latex (x,y,s)&s=3$ is just a matter of solving a system of equations and choosing those $latex (x,y,s) &s=3$ with $latex s\geq0 &s=3$.

    Moreover, one can solve the problem

    $latex \text{(OP3)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x,y)\\ \text{subject to} & a_1 x + b_1 y - c_1 + s = 0\\ & a_2 x + b_2 y - c_2 = 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    and just choose solutions with $latex s \geq 0 &s=3$ to obtain solutions to (OP2), and hence (OP1).















    Epigraph Form Recall: $latex \text{epi}\, f = \{(x,t): x \in \text{dom}\,f, t \geq f(x) \}&s=3$.

    The optimization problem

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases} &s=3$

    is equivalent to its epigraph form

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & t\\ \text{subject to} &f_0(x) - t \leq0\\ & f_i(x) \leq 0, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases} &s=3$

    Viz., minimizing $latex f_0 &s=3$ subject to constraints is equivalent to finding the smallest $latex t &s=3$ such that $latex (x,t) \in \text{epi}\, f&s=3$ for some feasible $latex x &s=3$.

    Proof by picture. The dark curve and shaded region below indicate the epigraph of a function $latex f &s=3$.
    The red dot indicates the minimum point $latex (x^\star,p^\star) &s=3$.
    The black dots indicate points $latex (x^\star,t) \in \text{epi}\,f &s=3$ for different values of $latex t &s=3$.
    Evidently, the smallest $latex t^\star &s=3$ for which $latex (x^\star,t^\star) \in \text{epi}\, f &s=3$ is given by $latex t^\star = p^\star &s=3$.















    Fragmenting a Problem Proposition. Given $latex f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ and sets $latex F,F_1,\ldots,F_q &s=3$ with $latex F = F_1 \cup \cdots \cup F_q, &s=3$ let

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star &= \inf \{f(x):x \in F\}\\ p_i^\star &= \inf\{ f(x): x \in F_i\}, \quad i = 1,\ldots, q. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Then

    $latex p^\star = \min\{p_i^\star: i = 1,\ldots,q\}&s=3$.

    (Assuming $latex \min\{-\infty,a\} = -\infty &s=3$ for any real number $latex a &s=3$.)

    Viz., to minimize a function on a set $latex F&s=3$, one may instead minimize $latex f &s=3$ over pieces of $latex F &s=3$ and then take the minimum optimal value of this procedure.


    Example. Consider the (OP)

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & x \in F \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex F \subset \text{dom}\, f &s=3$ and where the feasible set $latex F &s=3$ is depicted below. Consider breaking up $latex F &s=3$ into three regions $latex F1,F2,F3 &s=3$ as indicated below. Now formulate the (OP)'s

    $latex \text{(OPi)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & x \in Fi \end{cases} &s=3$

    for $latex i = 1,2,3 &s=3$, and let $latex p_i^\star &s=3$ be the optimal value for (OPi).
    Using the preceding proposition, the optimal value of (OP) is given by

    $latex p^\star = \min \{ p_1^\star, p_2^\star, p_3^\star \} &s=3$.

    Conclusion: solving (OP), whose feasible set is not convex, may be achieved by solving three subproblems (OP1),(OP2),(OP3) whose feasible sets are convex.















    Basics of Convex Optimization
    Convex Optimization Problems Abstract convex optimization problem: A problem involving minimizing a convex objective function on a convex set.
    Convex optimization problem: a problem of the form

    $latex \text{(COP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & a_i^T x = b_i, i =1,\ldots, p \end{cases}, &s=3$

    where
    $latex f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ and $latex f_i:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ are convex; and
    $latex a_i \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ and $latex b_i \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$ are fixed.

















    Some Remarks
    Remark 1. As defined, a (COP) is an (OP) in standard form; naturally, there are nonstandard form (OP)'s equivalent to (COP)'s.
    E.g., the abstract (COP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x,y) \\ \text{subject to} & (x+y+1)^2 = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    is readily seen to be equivalent to the standard form (COP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x,y) \\ \text{subject to}& \begin{bmatrix}1&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\y\end{bmatrix} + 1 = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$.







    Remark 2. We emphasize: the equality constraints are assumed to be affine constraints.
    Moreover, the equality constraints

    $latex a_i^T x = b_i , \quad i=1,\ldots,p &s=3$

    can be rewritten as

    $latex A x = b &s=3$,

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \begin{bmatrix} a_1^T\\a_2^T\\\vdots\\a_p^T\end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}, \quad b = \begin{bmatrix}b_1\\b_2\\\vdots\\b_p\end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{p} \end{aligned} &s=3$.







    Remark 3. The affine assumption on the equality constraints can be lifted at the possible expense of an intractable theory/numerical analysis.
    E.g., if $latex h: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ is quasilinear, then $latex h(x) = 0 &s=3$ defines a convex set.





    Remark 4. Generally, $latex h(x) &s=3$ convex does not imply the level set $latex h(x) = 0 &s=3$ is convex; e.g., $latex h(x,y)=x^2+y^2-1 &s=3$ gives a sphere.





    Remark 5. The common domain

    $latex D = \bigcap_{i=0}^m \text{dom}\, f_i&s=3$

    is convex since it is an intersection of convex sets.















    Optimality for Convex Optimization Problems Assume throughout that $latex f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$ is the objective function for some given (COP) and that $latex F &s=3$ is the feasible set.

    Proposition 1. If $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a feasible local minimizer for a (COP), then it is the global minimizer for the (COP).
    Proof. We will follow a proof by contradiction; i.e., we will show that assuming $latex x^\star &s=3$ is not a global minimizer leads to a contradiction.
    Step 1. $latex x^\star &s=3$ being a feasible local minimizer means $latex x^\star \in F &s=3$ and that there is a $latex R>0 &s=3$ such that

    $latex f_0(x^\star) = \inf\{ f_0(z) : z \in F, \quad \Vert x-x^\star \Vert_2 \leq R \} &s=3;$

    i.e., $latex f_0(x^\star) \leq f_0(z) &s=3$ for all $latex z \in F &s=3$ with a distance at most $latex R &s=3$ of $latex x^\star &s=3$.



    Step 2. Supposing $latex x^\star &s=3$ is not a global minimizer, then there exists $latex y \in F &s=3$ such that $latex f_0(y) < f_0(x^\star) &s=3$.
    By choice of $latex R &s=3$, there must also hold $latex \Vert{y-x^\star}\Vert_2 > R &s=3$.



    Step 3. Set

    $latex \begin{aligned} z &= (1-t)x^\star + ty\\ t &= \frac{R}{2\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    noting that $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$ by Step 2. and so $latex z \in F &s=3$ since $latex F &s=3$ is convex.
    It follows that

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert z - x^\star \Vert_2 &= \Vert (1-t)x^\star + ty - x^\star \Vert_2\\ &= \Vert t(y-x^\star ) \Vert_2\\ &= \frac{R}{2\Vert y-x^\star\Vert_2} \Vert y-x^\star\Vert_2\\ &= \frac{R}{2}\\ &< R \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Step 4. Since $latex z &s=3$ is a convex combination of feasible points, since $latex f_0 &s=3$ is convex and since $latex f_0(y)<f_0(x^\star) &s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(z) &= f_0((1-t)x^\star + ty) \\ &\leq (1-t)f_0(x^\star) + t f_0(y)\\ & < (1-t) f_0(x^\star) + t f_0(x^\star)\\ &= f_0(x^\star). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    But, since $latex x^\star &s=3$ minimizes $latex f_0 &s=3$ on

    $latex \{x \in F : \Vert x - x^\star \Vert_2 \leq R \}&s=3$

    and since

    $latex \Vert z - x^\star \Vert_2 \leq R &s=3$

    we also have

    $latex f_0(x^\star) \leq f_0(z)&s=3$.

    This is a contradiction and so $latex x^\star &s=3$ must be a global minimizer.








    Proposition 2. If $latex f_0 &s=3$ is differentiable on $latex F &s=3$, then $latex x^\star \in F &s=3$ is a minimizer iff for all $latex y \in F &s=3$ there holds

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T (y - x^\star) \geq 0 &s=3$.

    Proof.
    Step 0. N.B.: since $latex f_0 &s=3$ is differentiable and convex on $latex F &s=3$, then for each $latex x,y \in F &s=3$ there holds

    $latex f_0(y) \geq f_0(x) + \nabla f_0(x)^T (y-x) &s=3$.

    (C.f., Convex Function Theory.First Order Characterization.)



    Step 1.($latex \implies &s=3$) Suppose $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a minimizer and suppose for contradiction that

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star)<0 &s=3$

    for some $latex y \in F &s=3$.
    Set $latex z_t = ty+(1-t)x^\star &s=3$, noting that $latex z_t \in F &s=3$ since $latex F &s=3$ is convex.
    Using

    $latex \frac{d}{dt} f_0(z_t)|_{t=0} = \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T (y-x^\star) < 0 &s=3$,

    we conclude $latex f_0 &s=3$ is decreasing near $latex z_0 = x^\star &s=3$ in the direction $latex y-x^\star&s=3$ and so $latex f_0(z_t)< f_0(x^\star) &s=3$ for small $latex t &s=3$.
    Since $latex z_t \in F &s=3$, this contradicts $latex x^\star &s=3$ being a minimizer.
