    Additional justification Since $latex z_t &s=3$ defines a line passing through $latex x^\star &s=3$ with direction $latex y-x^\star &s=3$, it follows that $latex \frac{d}{dt} f_0(z_t)|_{t=0} &s=3$ is the directional derivative in direction $latex (y-x^\star)&s=3$, i.e., $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star)&s=3$.




    Step 2.($latex \impliedby &s=3$) Supposing

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)(y-x^\star) \geq 0 &s=3$

    for all $latex y \in F &s=3$ and using the first order characterization at $latex x^\star &s=3$, namely,

    $latex f_0(y) \geq f_0(x^\star) + \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) &s=3$

    we readily conclude

    $latex f_0(y) \geq f_0(x^\star) &s=3$

    for all $latex y \in F &s=3$; i.e., that $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a minimizer for the problem.








    Corollary In case $latex f_0 &s=3$ is differentiable and $latex F = \text{dom}\,f_0 &s=3$ (equivalently, there are no nontrivial constraints), $latex x^\star \in \text{dom}\,f_0 &s=3$ is a minimizer iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) = 0 &s=3$.

    Proof. By Proposition 2., we have that $latex x^\star \in \text{dom}\,f_0 &s=3$ is a minimizer iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) \geq 0 &s=3$

    for all $latex y \in \text{dom}\, f_0 &s=3$.
    Differentiability of $latex f_0 &s=3$ requires $latex \text{dom}\,f_0 &s=3$ is open and so, for small $latex t \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, there holds

    $latex y: = x^\star - t \nabla f_0(x^\star) \in \text{dom}\,f_0 &s=3$.

    But then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) &= \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(x^\star - t \nabla f_0(x^\star) - x^\star)\\ &= -t\nabla f_0(x^\star)^T\nabla f_0(x^\star)\\ &= -t \Vert \nabla f_0(x^\star) \Vert_2^2\\ &\geq 0, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is only possible for $latex t>0 &s=3$ iff $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) = 0 &s=3$.















    Some Examples
    Example 1. Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} Q \in &\boldsymbol{S}_+^n, \quad a \in \mathbb{R}^n\quad b \in \mathbb{R}\\ f_0(x)&= \frac{1}{2} x^T Q x + a^T x + b. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Consider the unconstrained problem:

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x) = \frac{1}{2} x^T Q x + a^T x + b\\ \text{subject to }& x \in \mathbb{R}^n \end{cases} &s=3$.

    Note that $latex \nabla^2 f_0 = Q \succeq 0 &s=3$ implies $latex f_0 &s=3$ is convex.
    C.f.,Convex Function Theory.Second Order Characterization.


    By the preceding corollary, we have $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a solution to (OP) iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) = Qx^\star + a = 0&s=3$.

    Thus solvability of (OP) rests on whether $latex -a \in \text{range}\, Q&s=3$.

    Three cases:
    1. $latex -a \notin \text{range}\,Q &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex f_0 &s=3$ is unbounded below and hence (OP) is unsolvable;
    2. $latex Q \succ 0 &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex Q &s=3$ is invertible and so $latex x^\star = -Q^{-1}a &s=3$ is the unique solution to (OP);
    3. $latex Q \not\succ 0 &s=3$ and $latex -a \in \text{range}\,Q &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex Qx^\star = -a &s=3$ has an affine set of solutions.






    Example 2. Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}& \text{ be convex and differentiable}\\ A &\in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^{m} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and consider the problem

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$.

    Using a preceding proposition, $latex x^\star &s=3$ satisfying $latex Ax^\star = b &s=3$ is a minimizer iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) \geq0 &s=3$

    for all $latex y &s=3$ satisfying $latex Ay= b&s=3$.


    Two cases
    1. $latex Ax=b &s=3$ is an inconsistent system $latex \implies &s=3$ the problem is infeasible.
    2. $latex Ax=b &s=3$ is a consistent system; then

      $latex Ax^\star = b&s=3$, $latex Ay=b &s=3$

      $latex \iff &s=3$

      $latex y = x^\star + x &s=3$ for some $latex x \in \text{null}A &s=3$.



    In case 2., we have

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) = \nabla f_0(x^\star)^Tx \geq 0 &s=3$

    for all

    $latex x \in \text{null}A &s=3$ and $latex y = x^\star + x &s=3$.

    Since $latex \text{null}A&s=3$ is a linear space, this is only possible iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)^Tx = 0 &s=3$ for all $latex x \in \text{null}\,A &s=3$,

    i.e., iff

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) \perp \text{null}A&s=3$.

    But $latex \text{null}A = \text{range}A^T &s=3$ and so this condition means there exists $latex \nu \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ such that

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) + A^T \nu = 0 &s=3$.

    This is just a Lagrange multiplier condition, as we will see later.















    Linear Programming Linear program: a (COP) of the form

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{miminize} & c^T x \\ \text{subject to} & Gx \preceq h\\ & Ax= b \end{cases} &s=3$,

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} &G \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, \quad A \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times n}\\ &h \in \mathbb{R}^m, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^p, \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The feasible set $latex F &s=3$ is a polyhedron (see below).

    Recall ($latex \preceq &s=3$): For $latex a,b \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ the vector inequality

    $latex a \preceq b &s=3$

    means

    $latex a_1 \leq b_1, \, a_2 \leq b_2, \, \ldots, \, a_n \leq b_n &s=3$.



    Different than: $latex A,B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &s=3$ satisfying the matrix inequality $latex A \preceq B &s=3$, which means $latex B - A &s=3$ is positive semidefinite.







    Determining the Feasible set:
    Step 1. ($latex A x = b &s=3$) Given $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n} &s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$, then

    $latex \{x: Ax=b \}&s=3$

    is an affine subspace of $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ or empty.


    Step 2. Given $latex \gamma \in \mathbb{R}^n, \eta \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$, then

    $latex \{x:\gamma^Tx \leq \eta \}&s=3$

    is a half space in $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.


    Step 3. ($latex G x \preceq h &s=3$) Given

    $latex g_i \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad G = \begin{bmatrix} g_1^T \\ g_2^T \\ \vdots \\ g_m^T \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}, \quad Gx = \begin{bmatrix} g_1^Tx\\g_2^Tx\\ \vdots \\ g_m^Tx \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    Step 2. implies

    $latex \{x : G x \preceq h \} = \{x : g_i^Tx\leq h_i, i = 1,\ldots,m\} &s=3$

    is a finite intersection of half spaces.


    Step 4. Steps 1. and 3. imply the feasible $latex F &s=3$ to (LP) is the finite intersection of half spaces and an affine space, i.e., $latex F &s=3$ is a polyhedron.
    (c.f. Convex Geometry.Polyhedra.)








    Example. Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} c&=\begin{bmatrix}0.1\\1\end{bmatrix},\, G= \begin{bmatrix} 0.8&0.8\\ -1&-1\\ 0&-1\\ 0&1 \end{bmatrix},\, h= \begin{bmatrix} 4\\-3\\-1\\3 \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Consider the resulting (LP):

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{miminize} & c^T x \\ \text{subject to} & Gx\preceq h \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Explicitly, this (LP) is given by

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{miminize} & 0.1x_1 + x_2\\ \text{subject to} & 0.8x_1 + 0.8x_2 \leq 4\\ & -x_1-x_2 \leq -3\\ &-x_2\leq -1\\ &x_2\leq3 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    The feasible set $latex F &s=3$ and the graph of the objective function over $latex F &s=3$ are indicated in the image below.







    Remarks.
    1. Given $latex d \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, have equivalent problem with objective $latex c^Tx + d &s=3$.
      Indeed,

      $latex \begin{aligned} \min\{c^Tx + d :x \in F\} &= \min\{ c^Tx: x \in F \} + d\\ \text{argmin}\{c^Tx + d :x \in F\} &=\text{argmin}\{ c^Tx: x \in F \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      WLOG: can assume $latex d=0&s=3$ to solve problem.


    2. Since

      $latex \begin{aligned} \max\{c^Tx :x \in F\} &= -\min\{ -c^Tx: x \in F \}\\ \text{argmax}\{c^Tx :x \in F\} &=\text{argmin}\{ -c^Tx: x \in F \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      one also calls the problem of maximizing $latex c^Tx &s=3$ over a polyhedron a (LP).















    Example: Integer Linear Programming Relaxation. Integer Linear Program: an (OP) of the form

    $latex \text{(ILP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h\\ & x \in \mathbb{Z}^n \end{cases} &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} &G \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n},\qquad h \in \mathbb{R}^m\\ \mathbb{Z}^n : &= \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n : x_i \text{ an integer for each } i=1,\ldots,n\} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The constraint $latex x \in \mathbb{Z}^n &s=3$ is suitable for parameters which take on discrete quantities.
    N.B.: An (ILP) is not a convex problem, but may be approximated by one (see below).

    The feasible set Let

    $latex F = \{x \in \mathbb{Z}^n: Gx \preceq h \} &s=3$

    denote the feasible set of an (ILP).
    Then $latex F &s=3$ is just the collection of integer vectors in the polyhedron

    $latex P = \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : Gx \preceq h \} &s=3$.



    Example. Consider an (ILP) with constraints given by $latex (x,y) \in \mathbb{Z}^2 &s=3$ satisfying

    $latex \begin{aligned} &-x\leq0\\ &-y\leq0\\ &y-2x\leq1\\ &y\leq2.5\\ &y+0.9x\leq4 \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    The image below depicts the feasible set $latex F &s=3$ (the collection of dots) and polyhedron $latex P &s=3$ (shaded region).







    Remarks.
    1. Can of course also impose equality constraints $latex Ax=b &s=3$:

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h\\ & Ax = b\\ & x \in \mathbb{Z}^n\\ \end{cases} &s=3$

    2. If we impose $latex x_i \in \{0,1\} &s=3$, then the problem is called a boolean linear program:

      $latex \text{(BLP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h\\ & x_i \in \{0,1\} \end{cases} &s=3$

      Suitable for when coordinates of $latex x &s=3$ indicate when something is "off" or "on" or decision is "no" or "yes".
      Can also use $latex x_i \in \{-1,1\} &s=3$ instead.








    Relaxation of (ILP) The LP

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h \end{cases} &s=3$

    is called a relaxation of the (ILP) and is a convex approximation.
    Important points:
    1. The "tightest" convex relaxation is given by

      $latex F=\{ x \in \mathbb{Z}^n : Gx \preceq h \} \to \text{convex hull of } F. &s=3$

      Generally speaking, finding the convex hull is not an efficient way of approaching (ILPs).
    2. The relaxation (LP) of (ILP) is generally easier to solve, though exact algorithms exist for (ILP).
    3. If

      $latex \begin{aligned} p_{LP}^\star &= \text{ optimal value for (LP)}\\ p_{ILP}^\star &= \text{ optimal value for (ILP)} \end{aligned} &s=3$

      then $latex p_{LP}^\star \leq p_{ILP}^\star&s=3$.
      (Indeed the relaxation has larger feasible set.)
    4. If $latex x^\star \in \mathbb{Z}^n &s=3$ solves the (LP), then it solves the (ILP).








    Relaxation of (BLP) Explicitly, a (BLP) is of the form

    $latex \text{(BLP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h\\ &x_i \in \{0,1\}, i=1,\ldots,n \end{cases}. &s=3$

    The (LP)

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^T x\\ \text{subject to}& Gx \preceq h\\ &0 \leq x_i \leq 1, i=1,\ldots,n \end{cases} &s=3$

    is called a relaxation of the (BLP).
    N.B.: the relaxation

    $latex F \to \{x \in [0,1]^n: Gx \preceq h \} &s=3$

    generally provides a better approximation than the relaxation

    $latex F \to \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n: Gx \preceq h \} &s=3$.

    Indeed, the former biases approximate solutions to be close to being binary.







    Example. Problem Given $latex m &s=3$ workers and $latex n &s=3$ locations with $latex m \leq n &s=3$,
    • assign each worker to work at some location
    • assign at most one worker to a location
    • minimize cost of operation and transportation
    Notational set up

    $latex \begin{aligned} c_j &= \text{cost to operate at location }j\\ c_{ij} &= \text{cost to transport worker } i \text{ to location }j\\ x_j &= \begin{cases} 0 & \text{ if location }j\text{ is not operating}\\ 1 & \text{ if location }j\text{ is operating} \end{cases}\\ x_{ij} &= \begin{cases} 0 & \text{ if worker }i \text{ is not working at location }j\\ 1 & \text{ if worker }i \text{ is working at location }j \end{cases}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{Cost vectors} & \begin{cases} c &= (c_j) \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ C &= (c_{ij}) \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} \end{cases}\\ \text{Optimization variables} &\begin{cases} x &= (x_j) \in \{0,1\}^n\\ X &= (x_{ij}) \in \{0,1\}^{m \times n} \end{cases} \end{aligned}. &s=3$



    Construct objective function
    We find

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{total operational cost }&= c^Tx = c_1x_1 + \cdots +c_nx_n\\ \text{total transportation cost }&= \text{tr}\,(C^TX) = \sum_{i=1}^m \sum_{j=1}^n c_{ij}x_{ij}\\ \text{total cost }&= c^Tx + \text{tr}\,(C^TX). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus the objective function is $latex f_0(x,X) = c^Tx + \text{tr}\,(C^TX)&s=3$.

    Construct constraints
    The constraint that $latex x_i,x_{ij}&s=3$ are binary is of course natural for the problem.
    Since each worker is selected only once, we have

    $latex \sum_{j=1}^n x_{ij} = 1, \quad \text{ for each } i=1,\ldots,m&s=3$.

    Lastly, observe $latex x_j=0 \implies x_{ij}=0 &s=3$ since the $latex j &s=3$th location not operating means it cannot host a worker; thus we have $latex x_{ij} \leq x_j &s=3$.

    Formulate Problem
    Putting everything together, the (BLP) formulation of the problem is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx + \text{tr}\,(C^TX)\\ \text{subject to} & x \in \{0,1\}^n\\ &X \in \{0,1\}^{m \times n}\\ &\sum_{j=1}^n x_{ij} =1, i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & x_{ij} \leq x_j, i=1,\ldots,m,j=1,\ldots,n \end{cases}. &s=3$

    An (LP) relaxation (and hence convex approximation) of this (BLP) is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx + \text{tr}\,(C^TX)\\ \text{subject to} & x \in [0,1]^n\\ &X \in [0,1]^{m \times n}\\ &\sum_{j=1}^n x_{ij} =1, i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & x_{ij} \leq x_j, i=1,\ldots,m,j=1,\ldots,n \end{cases}. &s=3$






















    Quadratic Programming Quadratic program: an (OP) of the form

    $latex \text{(QP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&\frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx\\ \text{subject to} &Gx \preceq h\\ &Ax =b \end{cases} &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} &Q \in \boldsymbol{S}^n, \quad G \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, \quad A \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}\\ &q \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad h \in \mathbb{R}^m,\quad b \in \mathbb{R}^p. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    If $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$, then the problem is convex (see remarks).

    Remarks.
    1. As for (LP), the constraints

      $latex \begin{cases} Gx \preceq h\\ Ax =b \end{cases} &s=3$

      describe a polyhedron.








    2. Given $latex d \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, then

      $latex \begin{aligned} \min\{\frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx + d :x \in F\} &= \min\{ \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx: x \in F \} + d\\ \text{argmin}\{\frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx + d :x \in F\} &= \text{argmin}\{ \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx: x \in F \} \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

      WLOG: can assume $latex d=0&s=3$ to solve problem.








    3. If

      $latex f_0(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx &s=3$,

      then

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f_0(x) &= Qx + q\\ \nabla^2 f_0(x) &= Q. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Thus $latex Q \succeq 0 &s=3$ implies $latex f_0 &s=3$ is convex.
      N.B.: The factor $latex \frac{1}{2} &s=3$ is just a convenient normalization.








    4. The generalization

      $latex \begin{aligned} &Gx \preceq h \, \to \, \frac{1}{2} x^T Q_ix+g_i^Tx \leq h_i \\ &Q_i \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}, g_i \in \mathbb{R}^n, h_i \in \mathbb{R}, \quad i =1,\ldots,m \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

      results in quadratically constrained quadratic programming (QCQP).
      Imposing $latex Q,Q_i \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$ ensures the (QCQP) is convex.
      (C.f., Convex Function Theory.Level Sets.)








    5. The generalization

      $latex \begin{aligned} &Ax=b \, \to \, \frac{1}{2} x^T P_ix+a_i^Tx =b_i \\ &P_i \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}, a_i \in \mathbb{R}^n, b_i \in \mathbb{R}, \quad i =1,\ldots,m \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

      also results in a (QCQP), but this can break convexity.
      E.g., The quadratic constraints

      $latex x_i(x_i-1)=0 \text{ or } x_i^2 = 1 &s=3$

      result in a (BLP) since these constraints enforce $latex x_i \in \{0,1\} &s=3$ or $latex x_i \in \{-1,1\}&s=3$, respectively.








    6. There holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} \text{(QCQP)} + Q_i = P_i= 0 &\iff (QP)\\ (QP) + Q=0 &\iff (LP). \end{aligned} &s=3$










    7. The assumption $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$ (i.e., $latex Q = Q^T &s=3$) is not a serious restriction.
      Indeed, first note that, since $latex x^TQx &s=3$ is a scalar, we have

      $latex x^TQx = (x^TQx)^T = x^TQ^Tx &s=3$.



      Let

      $latex f(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^T x &s=3$.



      Thus,

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) &= \frac{f(x) + f(x)}{2}\\ &= \frac{1}{2}\left( \frac{1}{2}x^T Q x + q^Tx + \frac{1}{2}x^T Q^T x + q^Tx \right)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}\left( \frac{1}{2}x^T(Q+Q^T)x + 2 q^Tx \right)\\ &=:\frac{1}{2}x^T \tilde{Q} x + q^T x, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      where

      $latex \tilde{Q} = \frac{1}{2}(Q+Q^T) &s=3$.



      Lastly, observe the symmetry of $latex \tilde{Q} &s=3$:

      $latex \tilde{Q}^T = \left( \frac{1}{2}(Q+Q^T) \right)^T = \frac{1}{2}(Q^T+Q) = \tilde{Q}&s=3$.



      Thus every quadratic

      $latex f(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx &s=3$

      has a "symmetric representation" of the form

      $latex f(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^T\tilde{Q}x + q^Tx &s=3$ with $latex \tilde{Q} \in \boldsymbol{S}^n&s=3$.
















    Example: Least Squares Least squares: an unconstrained (QP) of the form

    $latex \text{(LS)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&\Vert Ax-b\Vert_2^2= x^TA^TAx-2b^TAx + b^Tb \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} &s=3$ and $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$.
    Features:
    • WLOG: may assume columns of $latex A &s=3$ are linearly independent, and so

      $latex m \geq n &s=3$.

    • N.B.: a least norm solution to (LS) is generally given by

      $latex x^\star = A^{\dagger}b &s=3$,

      where $latex A^\dagger&s=3$ is the pseudo-inverse (aka Moore-Penrose inverse) of $latex A &s=3$.
    • Under the WLOG assumption, there holds

      $latex x^\star = (A^TA)^{-1}A^Tb &s=3$.



    Recall (definition of $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$) For $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$, the notation $latex \Vert x \Vert_2&s=3$ means the vector norm

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_2 := \sqrt{x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2 } &s=3$.

    Thus

    $latex \Vert x- y \Vert_2 &s=3$

    is the distance between the two vectors $latex x,y \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.







    Rough Justification of WLOG Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} A &= \begin{bmatrix} a_1 & a_2 & a_3 \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 3}, \quad a_1,a_2,a_3 \in \mathbb{R}^2 \\ x &= \begin{bmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \\ x_3 \end{bmatrix}, \quad b = \begin{bmatrix} b_1 \\ b_2 \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: a set of three 2-dimensional vectors is always linearly dependent and so

    $latex a_3 = c_1 a_1 + c_2 a_2 &s=3$

    for some $latex c_1,c_2 \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$
    Therefore, we may write

    $latex A=: \begin{bmatrix} a & \alpha & u \\ b & \beta & v \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} a & \alpha & c_1 a + c_2 \alpha\\ b & \beta & c_1 b + c_2 \beta \end{bmatrix} &s=3$.



    Computing

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax &= \begin{bmatrix} a & \alpha & c_1 a + c_2 \alpha\\ b & \beta & c_1 b + c_2 \beta \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \\ x_3 \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \begin{bmatrix} ax_1 + \alpha x_2 + c_1 a x_3 + c_2 \alpha x_3\\ bx_1 + \beta x_2 + c_1 b x_3 + c_2 \beta x_3 \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \begin{bmatrix} a(x_1 + c_1 x_3) + \alpha (x_2 + c_2 x_3)\\ b(x_1 + c_1 x_3) + \beta(x_2 + c_2 x_3) \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we see that minimizing

    $latex \Vert Ax-b \Vert_2^2 = \bigg\Vert \begin{bmatrix} a(x_1 + c_1 x_3) + \alpha (x_2 + c_2 x_3)\\ b(x_1 + c_1 x_3) + \beta(x_2 + c_2 x_3) \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} b_1\\b_2 \end{bmatrix} \bigg\Vert_2^2 &s=2$

    is equivalent to minimizing

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert \hat{A}y - b \Vert_2^2 &:= \bigg\Vert \begin{bmatrix} a&\alpha\\b&\beta \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}y_1\\y_2\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}b_1\\b_2\end{bmatrix} \bigg\Vert_2^2\\ &= \bigg\Vert \begin{bmatrix} ay_1 + \alpha y_2\\ by_1 + \beta y_2 \end{bmatrix} -\begin{bmatrix}b_1\\b_2\end{bmatrix} \bigg\Vert_2^2 \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Equivalence follows from the change of variables

    $latex \begin{aligned} y_1 &= x_1 + c_1 x_3\\ y_2 &= x_2 + c_2 x_3 \end{aligned} &s=3$.



    Therefore, a (LS) problem with matrix of size $latex 2 \times 3 &s=3$ is equivalent to a (LS) problem with matrix of size $latex 2 \times 2 &s=3$.

    This argument holds in general: if $latex A &s=3$ has linearly dependent columns, can use change of variables to ensure linear independence.







    Remarks.
    1. If $latex Ax=b &s=3$ has solution $latex x^\star &s=3$, then $latex x^\star &s=3$ solves the (LS) problem.

    2. If $latex Ax=b &s=3$ has no solution, then any $latex x^\star &s=3$ solving the (LS) problem gives a "best" estimate solution to $latex Ax = b&s=3$, where "best" is chosen to mean in terms of the vector norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$.

    3. While minimizing $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2^2 &s=3$ is equivalent to minimizing $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$, the exponent $latex 2 &s=3$ ensures $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2^2 &s=3$ is differentiable (at $latex 0 &s=3$).
      (C.f.: $latex |x|&s=3$ is not differentiable at $latex x=0 &s=3$, but $latex x^2 &s=3$ is.)








    Solving the (LS)
    Step 1. (The problem is convex) The objective function

    $latex f_0(x) = \Vert Ax-b\Vert_2^2 = x^TA^TAx - 2b^TAx+b^Tb &s=3$

    is convex since

    $latex \nabla^2 f_0 = 2 A^TA \succeq 0 &s=3$.

    To see $latex A^TA \succeq 0 &s=3$, observe:
    1. $latex (A^T A)^T = A^T A &s=3$ and so $latex A^TA \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$, i.e., $latex A^TA &s=3$ is symmetric;
    2. for all $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ there holds

      $latex z^T A^T A z = (Az)^T Az = \Vert Az \Vert_2^2 \geq 0 &s=3$,

      i.e., $latex A^TA \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$.




    Step 2. (The critical points) We compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f_0 &= \nabla (x^T A^TA x) - \nabla(2b^TAx) + \nabla(b^Tb)\\ &= 2 A^TAx - 2A^Tb + 0\\ &= 2(A^TAx - A^Tb). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, $latex \nabla f_0 = 0 &s=3$ iff

    $latex A^TAx = A^T b &s=3$.

    This system of equations consists of what are called the normal equations.



    Step 3. (The solution) By corollary proved above: $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a solution iff $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) = 0 &s=3$.
    Moreover, $latex A &s=3$ having linearly independent columns ensures that $latex A^TA &s=3$ is invertible:
    1. linear independent columns $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex Az=0&s=3$ iff $latex z=0&s=3$.
    2. $latex A^TAz = 0 &s=3$ $latex \iff &s=3$ $latex Az=0 &s=3$ $latex \iff &s=3$ $latex z = 0 &s=3$.
    3. Since $latex A^TA &s=3$ is square, can conclude $latex A^TA &s=3$ is invertible.


    Lastly: since

    $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star)= 0 &s=3$ iff $latex A^TA x^\star = A^T b&s=3$

    and since $latex A^TA &s=3$ is invertible, we conclude that the solution to the (LS) is

    $latex x^\star = (A^TA)^{-1}A^Tb &s=3$.
















    Example: Distance Between Convex Sets Let $latex K_1,K_2 \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be convex subsets.
    Nearest Point Problem (NPP): Among $latex x \in K_1, y \in K_2 &s=3$, which pair $latex (x,y) &s=3$ minimizes the distance $latex \Vert x-y \Vert_2^2 &s=3$?



    The (NPP) may be expressed as a standard form (COP).
    Indeed, supposing

    $latex \begin{aligned} K_1 &= \{ f_1(x) \leq 0 \}\\ K_2 &= \{ f_2(x) \leq 0 \}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    with $latex f_1,f_2 &s=3$ convex, then the (NPP) for $latex K_1,K_2 &s=3$ is the (COP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \Vert x - y \Vert_2^2\\ \text{subject to} & f_1(x) \leq 0 \\ &f_2(y) \leq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    N.B.: the feasible set is

    $latex \begin{aligned} F&=\{(x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^n : f_1(x) \leq 0, f_2(y) \leq 0 \}\\ &=K_1 \times K_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Viz.: $latex (x,y) \in F &s=3$ iff $latex x \in K_1, y \in K_2 &s=3$.





    When the $latex K_1,K_2 &s=3$ are polyhedra, then the (NPP) is a (QP).
    Indeed, supposing

    $latex \begin{aligned} K_1 &= \{ G_1 x \preceq h_1 \}\\ K_2 &= \{ G_2 x \preceq h_2 \}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    then the (NPP) for $latex K_1,K_2 &s=3$ is the (QP) given by

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \Vert x - y \Vert_2^2\\ \text{subject to} & G_1 x \preceq h_1\\ & G_2 y \preceq h_2 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Can formulate as constrained least squares problem: defining

    $latex \begin{aligned} B,C \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &\mapsto B \oplus C = \begin{bmatrix} B & \vline & 0\\ \hline 0 & \vline & C \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2n \times 2n}, \\ v,w \in \mathbb{R}^n &\mapsto v \oplus w = \begin{bmatrix} v_1 & \cdots & v_n & w_1 & \cdots & w_n \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n + n}\\ A &= \begin{bmatrix} Id_{n\times n} &\vline & - Id_{n\times n}\\ \hline 0 &\vline& 0 \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we readily see that the problem is equivalent to

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \Vert A(x\oplus y) \Vert_2^2\\ \text{subject to} & (G_1 \oplus G_2) (x \oplus y) \preceq h_1 \oplus h_2 \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Polyhedral Approximation. Let $latex K_1,K_2 &s=3$ be nonempty convex domains, not necessarily polyhedral. Let $latex P_1,P_2,Q_1,Q_2 &s=3$ be polyhedral domains such that

    $latex \begin{aligned} &P_1 \subset K_1 \subset Q_1\\ &P_2 \subset K_2 \subset Q_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Then the (NPP) for the pairs $latex [P_1,P_2] &s=3$ and $latex [Q_1,Q_2] &s=3$ provide (QP) relaxations of the (NPP) for $latex [K_1,K_2] &s=3$ and, respectively, provide upper and lower bounds for the optimal values for the original (NPP).















    Geometric Programming Monomial function: given

    $latex c \geq 0,\, a_i \in \mathbb{R},\, i =1,\ldots,n &s=3$,

    a function of the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} f&: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}\\ f(x)&= c x_1^{a_1} x_2^{a_2} \cdots x_n^{a_n}\\ \text{dom}\,f&=\mathbb{R}_{++}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Example.

    $latex 3x_1^2x_2^{3.4}x_3^{-\pi}&s=3$.









    Posynomial: given

    $latex c_k \geq 0,\, a_{ik}\in \mathbb{R},\, i=1,\ldots,n,\, k=1,\ldots,K &s=3$,

    a function of the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} f&: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}\\ f(x)&= \sum_{k=1}^K c_k x_1^{a_{1k}} x_2^{a_{2k}} \cdots x_n^{a_{nk}}\\ \text{dom}\,f&=\mathbb{R}_{++}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Example.

    $latex f(x) = \sqrt{2}x_1x_2\sqrt{x_3} + 3 x_1^4x_2^{-4}x_3^{-1.5}+x_5 &s=3$









    Geometric Programming: An (OP) of the form

    $latex \text{(GP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}&f_i(x) \leq 1, \, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 1, \, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}, &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0,f_1,\ldots,f_m & \text{ are posynomials}\\ h_1,\ldots,h_p & \text{ are monomials}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: this is an undercover (COP).







    Remarks.
    1. Let

      $latex \begin{aligned} Posy_n &= \text{ set of posynomials on } \mathbb{R}_{++}^n\\ Mon_n &= \text{ set of monomials on } \mathbb{R}_{++}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Since any monomial is a posynomial, we have $latex Mon_n \subset Posy_n &s=3$.







    2. If

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda \geq 0\\ p(x),q(x) &\in Posy_n\\ f(x),g(x) &\in Mon_n , \end{aligned} &s=3$

      then

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda p(x),\, p(x) + q(x),\, p(x) q(x),\, \frac{p(x)}{f(x)} \in Posy_n\\ \lambda f(x),\, f(x)g(x),\, \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \in Mon_n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Thus $latex Mon_n &s=3$ is a group and $latex Posy_n &s=3$ a "conic representation" of $latex Mon_n &s=3$.







    3. As usual, we use the language "writing in standard form" to refer to writing an equivalent (OP) written in the form (GP) above.

      General (OPs) clearly equivalent to a (GP) may be called a geometric program in nonstandard form.

      For example, the geometric program

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}&f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq g_i(x)\\ & h_i(x) = k_i(x) \end{cases} &s=3$

      with

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_i &\text{ are posynomials}\\ f_0,h_i,g_i\not\equiv 0 ,k_i\not\equiv0 & \text{ are monomials} \end{aligned} &s=3$

      is readily rewritten as a standard form (GP):

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&\frac{1}{f_0(x)}\\ \text{subject to} & \frac{f_i(x)}{g_i(x)} \leq 1\\ & \frac{h_i(x)}{k_i(x)} = 1 \end{cases}. &s=3$

















    Rewriting (GP) as a (COP) General (GPs) are not convex (e.g., $latex f_0(x) = \sqrt{x} &s=3$).
    However, any (GP) is easily recast as a (COP) via change of variable.

    Step 1. (The change of variable) We will write $latex x \mapsto y &s=3$ to mean the change of variable given by

    $latex x_i = e^{y_i}. &s=3$







    Step 2. (Monomials $latex \to &s=3$ convex function) Let

    $latex \begin{matrix} c>0, \quad b = \log c,& f(x) \in Mon_n,\\ a = \begin{bmatrix} a_1 & \cdots & a_n \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^n,\quad & f(x) = c x_1^{a_1}x_2^{a_2} \cdots x_n^{a_n}. \end{matrix} &s=3$

    Under the change of variable $latex x \mapsto y&s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) &= f(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\\ &= f(e^{y_1},\ldots, e^{y_n})\\ &= c(e^{y_1})^{a_1} \cdots (e^{y_n})^{a_n}\\ &= e^{\log c}e^{a_1y_1}\cdots e^{a_ny_n}\\ &= e^{a^Ty+b} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    But

    $latex F\, &s=3$ convex $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex e^F\, &s=3$ convex

    and so $latex e^{a^Ty+b}&s=3$ is convex since affine functions are convex.







    Step 3. (Posynomial $latex \to &s=3$ convex function) For $latex k=1,\ldots,K &s=3$, let

    $latex \begin{matrix} c_k>0, \quad b_k = \log c_k,& f(x) \in Posy_n,\\ a_k = \begin{bmatrix} a_{1k} & \cdots a_{nk} \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{n},\quad & f(x) = \sum_{k=1}^K c_k x_1^{a_{1k}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{nk}}. \end{matrix} &s=3$

    By Step 2., there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) = f(y) = \sum_{k=1}^K e^{a_k^Ty + b_k}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is again a convex function.







    Step 4. ((GP) $latex \to &s=3$ (COP)) We explicitly write the (GP) as:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&f_0(x) = \sum_{k=1}^{K_0} c_{0k} x_1^{a_{01k}}\cdots x_n^{a_{0nk}}\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x) = \sum_{k=1}^{K_i} c_{ik} x_1^{a_{i1k}} \cdots x_n^{a_{ink}} \leq 1, \, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) =d_{i}x_1^{b_{i1}}\cdots x_n^{b_{in}} = 1, \, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} a_{ik} &= \begin{bmatrix} a_{i1k} & \cdots a_{ink} \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ b_i &= \begin{bmatrix} b_{i1} & \cdots & b_{in} \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ \alpha_{ik} &= \log c_{ik}\\ \delta_i &= \log d_{i} \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Under the change of variable $latex x \mapsto y &s=3$, this (GP) becomes the (COP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&f_0(y) = \sum_{k=1}^{K_0} e^{a_{0k}^T y + \alpha_{0k}}\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(y) = \sum_{k=1}^{K_i} e^{a_{ik}^T y + \alpha_{ik}} \leq 1, \, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(y) = e^{b_{i}^Ty + \delta_i} = 1, \, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$









    Step 5. ((GP) in convex form) At last, since exponentiation may result in unreasonably large numbers, it is customary to take logarithms, resulting in the geometric problem in convex form:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& \log \left( \sum_{k=1}^{K_0} e^{a_{0k}^T y + \alpha_{0k}} \right)\\ \text{subject to}& \log \left(\sum_{k=1}^{K_i} e^{a_{ik}^T y + \alpha_{ik}}\right) \leq 0, \, i=1,\ldots,m\\ & b_{i}^Ty + \delta_i = 0, \, i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$

    N.B.:
    1. Concavity of $latex \log &s=3$ is too weak to break the convexity of $latex e^{a^Ty+b}&s=3$, and so the problem is still convex.
    2. The constraints are equivalent since $latex \log &s=3$ is monotonic and injective on $latex \mathbb{R}_{++}&s=3$.








    Example. (Taken from Boyd-Kim-Vandenberghe-Hassibi: A tutorial on geometric programming)
    Problem Maximize the volume of a box with
    • a limit on total wall area;
    • a limit on total floor area; and
    • upper and lower bounds on the aspect ratios $latex height/width &s=3$ and $latex depth/width &s=3$.




    Notational set up

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{Optimization Variables}& \begin{cases} w &= \text{width}\\ h &= \text{height}\\ d &= \text{depth} \end{cases}\\ \text{Problem Parameters}& \begin{cases} A_{\text{wall}} &= \text{max wall area}\\ A_{\text{floor}} &= \text{max floor area}\\ \alpha_{-},\alpha_+ &= \text{lower and upper aspect ratio bounds for }h/w\\ \beta_{-},\beta_+ &= \text{lower and upper aspect ratio bounds for }d/w \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Construct objective function
    The volume of the box is

    $latex hwd &s=3$

    and so the objective function is

    $latex f_0(h,w,d) = hwd &s=3$.

    N.B.: $latex f_0 \in Mon_3 &s=3$



    Construct contraints

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{wall area bound }:&\quad 2hw+2hd \leq A_{\text{wall}}\\ \text{floor area bound }:&\quad wd \leq A_{\text{floor}}\\ \text{aspect ratio bounds }:&\quad \alpha_1 \leq \frac{h}{w} \leq \alpha_2\\ &\quad \beta_1 \leq \frac{d}{w} \leq \beta_2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.:

    $latex \begin{aligned} 2hw+2hd &\in Posy_3\\ wd,\, hw^{-1},\, dw^{-1} &\in Mon_3 \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Formulate Problem
    Putting everything together, we realize the problem may be formulated as the following (GP):

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & hwd\\ \text{subject to} & 2hw+2hd\leq A_{\text{wall}}\\ & wd \leq A_{\text{floor}}\\ & \alpha_1 \leq hw^{-1} \leq \alpha_2\\ & \beta_1 \leq dw^{-1} \leq \beta_2 \end{cases}. &s=3$





    To write the problem in standard form: note the following equivalence of constraints

    $latex \begin{aligned} 2hw+2hd\leq A_{\text{wall}} & \iff A_{\text{wall}}^{-1}2hw + A_{\text{wall}}^{-1}2hd \leq 1\\ wd \leq A_{\text{floor}} & \iff A_{\text{floor}}^{-1}wd \leq 1\\ &\\ \alpha_1 \leq hw^{-1} \leq \alpha_2 &\iff \begin{array}{l} \alpha_2^{-1}hw^{-1} \leq 1 \\ \alpha_1 h^{-1}w \leq 1 \end{array}\\ \beta_1 \leq dw^{-1} \leq \beta_2 & \iff \begin{array}{l} \beta_2^{-1} dw^{-1} \leq 1\\ \beta_1 d^{-1}w \leq 1 \end{array} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Moreover, maximizing $latex hwd &s=3$ is equivalent to minimizing $latex h^{-1}w^{-1}d^{-1} &s=3$.



    Therefore, the problem in standard form is given by

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & h^{-1}w^{-1}d^{-1}\\ \text{subject to} & A_{\text{wall}}^{-1}2hw +A_{\text{wall}}^{-1}2hd \leq 1\\ & A_{\text{floor}}^{-1} wd \leq 1\\ & \alpha_2^{-1} hw^{-1}\leq 1 \\ & \alpha_1 wh^{-1} \leq 1\\ & \beta_2^{-1}dw^{-1} \leq 1\\ & \beta_1 wd^{-1} \leq 1 \end{cases}. &s=3$
















    Semidefinite Programming (Heavily influenced by Vandenberghe-Boyd Semidefinite Programming.)
    Linear matrix inequality (LMI): given

    $latex \begin{aligned} &F_0,F_1,\ldots,F_n \in \boldsymbol{S}^m\\ &x = \begin{bmatrix}x_1 & \cdots & x_n \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ F(x) &:= F_0 + x_1 F_1 + \cdots + x_n F_n \end{aligned} &s=3$

    an inequality of the form

    $latex F(x) \succeq 0 &s=3$.

    Recall: for $latex A \in \boldsymbol{S}^m &s=3$, we write $latex A \succeq 0 &s=3$ to mean $latex A &s=3$ is positive semidefinite, i.e., $latex z^TAz \geq 0 &s=3$ for all $latex z \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$.





    Semidefinite program (SDP): an (OP) of the form

    $latex \text{(SDP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & F(x) \succeq 0, \end{cases} &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} &F_0,F_1,\ldots,F_n \in \boldsymbol{S}^m\\ F(x) &:= F_0 + x_1 F_1 + \cdots + x_n F_n\\ c & \in \mathbb{R}^n \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: The LMI $latex F(x) \succeq 0 &s=3$ defines a feasible set which is convex and hence (SDPs) are convex problems.





    Convexity of Feasible Set. To see that (SDP) is a convex problem, first note: if

    $latex t>0 &s=3$ and $latex A \succeq 0, &s=3$

    then

    $latex z^T(tA)z = t ( z^TAz) \geq 0 &s=3$

    and so

    $latex tA \succeq 0 &s=3$.





    Next, observe: for $latex x,y &s=3$ feasible and $latex t \in [0,1] &s=3$, the function

    $latex F(x) = F_0 + x_1 F_1 + \cdots + x_n F_n &s=3$

    evaluated at the convex combination

    $latex tx + (1-t)y &s=3$

    is

    $latex \begin{aligned} F(tx +(1-t)y) &= F_0 + (tx_1 + (1-t)y_1) F_1 + \cdots + (tx_n + (1-t)y_n)F_n. \end{aligned}&s=3$

    Expanding, rearranging and using

    $latex F_0 = tF_0 + (1-t)F_0 &s=3$

    gives:

    $latex \begin{aligned} F(tx +(1-t)y) &= tF_0 + tx_1F_1 + \cdots tx_nF_n \\ &+ (1-t)F_0 + (1-t)y_1F_1 + \cdots + (1-t)y_nF_n\\ &= tF(x) + (1-t)F(y). \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Using $latex F(x),F(y)\succeq 0 &s=3$, we conclude

    $latex F(tx+(1-t)y) = tF(x) + (1-t)F(y) \succeq 0 &s=3$.

    Thus, $latex x,y &s=3$ feasible $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex tx + (1-t)y &s=3$ feasible for $latex t \in [0,1]&s=3$, i.e., the feasible set is convex.







    Example 1. LPs are SDPs Consider the (LP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax + b \succeq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    where

    $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^{m}, \quad c \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$

    and

    $latex Ax+b \succeq 0 &s=3$

    means componentwise inequality.





    Given $latex v = \begin{bmatrix}v_1 & \cdots & v_m \end{bmatrix}^T \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$, define

    $latex \text{diag}(v) = \begin{bmatrix} v_1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & v_2 &\cdots &0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & v_m \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$







    Since $latex A \in \boldsymbol{S}^m &s=3$ satisfies $latex A \succeq 0 &s=3$ iff $latex A &s=3$ has nonnegative eigenvalues, we have

    $latex v \succeq 0 \iff \text{diag}(v) \succeq 0&s=3$.

    (Indeed, the eigenvalues of $latex \text{diag}(v) &s=3$ are the components of $latex v &s=3$.) Therefore,

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax+b \succeq 0 &\iff \text{diag}(Ax+b) \succeq 0 \\ \text{(vector inequality)} & \iff \text{(matrix inequality)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Letting

    $latex A=\begin{bmatrix}a_1 & \cdots & a_n \end{bmatrix}, \quad a_i \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$,

    we have

    $latex Ax + b = b + x_1 a_1 + \cdots + x_n a_n &s=3$.



    Therefore, using

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{diag}(v+\lambda w) &= \begin{bmatrix} v_1 + \lambda w_1 &0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & v_2 + \lambda w_2 & \cdots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & v_n + \lambda w_n \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \begin{bmatrix} v_1 &0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & v_2 & \cdots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & v_n \end{bmatrix} + \lambda \begin{bmatrix} w_1 &0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & w_2 & \cdots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & w_n \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \text{diag}(v) + \lambda \text{diag}(w) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{diag}(Ax + b) &= \text{diag}(b + x_1 a_1 + \cdots + x_n a_n)\\ &= \text{diag}(b) + x_1 \text{diag}(a_1) + \cdots + x_n \text{diag}(a_n). \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Therefore, defining

    $latex \begin{aligned} F_0 &= \text{diag}(b), \quad F_i = \text{diag}(a_i)\\ F(x) &= F_0 + x_1 F_1 + \cdots + x_n F_n = \text{diag}(Ax+b), \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax+b \succeq 0 \iff \text{diag}(Ax+b) \succeq 0 \iff F(x) \succeq 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$.







    In conclusion, we have that the (LP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax + b \succeq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    is equivalent to the (SDP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & F(x)= \text{diag}(Ax+b) \succeq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$









    Example 2. Nonlinear OP as a SDP Consider the nonlinear (COP)

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx}\\ \text{subject to} & Ax + b \succeq 0\\ & d^Tx > 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} &c,d \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ &A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, b \in \mathbb{R}^m. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    We will recast (OP1) as a (SDP).
    N.B.:
    1. $latex d^Tx > 0 \implies &s=3$ choice of domain (a halfspace) of objective and ensures convexity.
    2. $latex d^Tx < 0 \implies &s=3$ concave problem.




    To begin, recall we may recast problem in epigraph form:

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& t\\ \text{subject to} & Ax + b \succeq 0\\ & d^Tx > 0\\ & \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx} \leq t \end{cases}. &s=3$

    N.B.: this introduces the new optimization variable $latex t &s=3$.



    Goal: find a symmetric matrix-valued function

    $latex F(x,t) = F_0 + x_1F_1 + \cdots + x_nF_n + t F_{n+1} &s=3$

    such that the constraints

    $latex \begin{cases} &Ax + b \succeq 0\\ & d^Tx > 0\\ & \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx} \leq t \end{cases} &s=3$

    may be recast as the LMI

    $latex \begin{cases} F(x) \succeq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$





    Idea: we know

    $latex Ax + b \succeq 0 \iff \text{diag}(Ax+b) \succeq 0 &s=3$.

    On the other hand:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx} \leq t &\iff (c^Tx)^2 \leq td^Tx \\ &\iff td^Tx - (c^Tx)^2 \geq0 \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    Recall: if $latex \gamma>0 &s=3$, then

    $latex \begin{bmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\beta&\gamma\end{bmatrix}\succeq0 \iff \alpha\geq0, \alpha\gamma - \beta^2 \geq 0&s=3$

    Therefore, given $latex d^Tx>0&s=3$, we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx} \leq t & \iff \begin{bmatrix}t & c^Tx\\ c^Tx & d^Tx \end{bmatrix} \succeq 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$.





    Using that

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} A &\vline &0\\ \hline 0 & \vline& B \end{bmatrix} \succeq 0 \iff A \succeq 0 , B \succeq 0, &s=3$

    we therefore introduce

    $latex E = \begin{bmatrix} \text{diag}(Ax+b) & 0 & 0\\ 0 & t & c^Tx\\ 0 & c^Tx & d^Tx \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    to capture the problems constraints.
    Indeed, evidently, $latex E \succeq 0 &s=3$ iff

    $latex \text{diag}(Ax+b) \succeq 0 &s=3$ and $latex \begin{bmatrix} t & c^tx\\c^tx & d^tx \end{bmatrix} \succeq 0 &s=3$.





    Therefore,

    $latex \begin{array}{l} Ax + b \succeq 0\\ d^Tx > 0\\ \frac{(c^Tx)^2}{d^Tx} \leq t \end{array} \iff \begin{array}{l} \begin{bmatrix} \text{diag}(Ax+b) & 0 & 0\\ 0 & t & c^Tx\\ 0 & c^Tx & d^Tx \end{bmatrix} \succeq 0 \end{array} &s=3$.

    This is enough to conclude (OP1) may be recast as an (SDP).





    To make it clearer, introduce the notation

    $latex A = \begin{bmatrix} a_1 & \cdots & a_n \end{bmatrix}, \quad a_i \in \mathbb{R}^m&s=3$

    and $latex (m+2)\times(m+2) &s=3$ matrices

    $latex \begin{aligned} F_0 &= \text{diag}(b,0,0) \\ F_i & = \begin{bmatrix} \text{diag}(a_i) &0 &0\\ 0&0&c_i\\ 0&c_i&d_i \end{bmatrix}\\ F_{n+1} & = \begin{bmatrix}0_{m \times m} &0 &0\\0 & 1 & 0\\0 & 0 & 0\end{bmatrix}\\ \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \text{diag}(Ax+b) & 0 & 0\\ 0 & t & c^Tx\\ 0 & c^Tx & d^Tx \end{bmatrix} = F_0 + x_1F_1 + \cdots + x_nF_n + t F_{n+1}:=F(x,t) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so (OP1) is equivalent to the (SDP)

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& t\\ \text{subject to} & F(x,t) \succeq 0. \end{cases} &s=3$
















    Lagrangian Duality

    Throughout, let (OP) denote a given optimization problem of the form

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots,p \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Recall:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{Problem domain: }& D := \bigcap_{i=0}^m \text{dom}\, f_i \cap \bigcap_{i=1}^p \text{dom}\,h_i\\ \text{Optimal value: } & p^\star := \inf\{f_0(x): x \in D , x \text{ feasible}\}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The Lagrange Dual Lagrangian: the function

    $latex \begin{aligned} L&:\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R} \\ \text{dom}\,L &= D \times \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \end{aligned} &s=3$

    given by

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda,\nu) = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i h_i(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    N.B.: for each fixed $latex x \in D &s=3$, the function

    $latex (\lambda,\nu) \mapsto L(x,\lambda,\mu)&s=3$

    is affine.





    Lagrange multipliers: the variables $latex \lambda_i &s=3$ and $latex \nu_i &s=3$.
    The vectors

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda := \begin{bmatrix}\lambda_1\\\lambda_2\\\vdots\\\lambda_m\end{bmatrix}, \quad \nu := \begin{bmatrix}\nu_1\\\nu_2\\\vdots\\\nu_p\end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    are called dual variables.





    Lagrange dual function: the function

    $latex \begin{aligned} g&:\mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

    given by

    $latex g(\lambda,\nu) = \inf\{L(x,\lambda,\nu): x \in D\} &s=3$.

    Those $latex (\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}^m_+ \times \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$ satisfying

    $latex g(\lambda,\nu) > -\infty &s=3$

    are called dual feasible.
    N.B.: as an infimum of affine functions, $latex g &s=3$ is automatically concave.





    Proposition. For

    $latex \lambda \in \mathbb{R}^m_+, \quad \nu \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$,

    there holds

    $latex g(\lambda,\nu) \leq p^\star&s=3$,

    where $latex p^\star &s=3$ is the optimal value for the given (OP).
    Proof.
    1. Let $latex x \in D &s=3$ be feasible.
      Then

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x) &\leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x) &=0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    2. Let $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$ and $latex \nu &s=3$ be arbitrary.
      Then feasibility of $latex x&s=3$ implies

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i h_i(x) = \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) \leq 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Consequently,

      $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda,\nu) = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i h_i(x) \leq f_0(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$







    3. Therefore, for all feasible $latex x &s=3$, for $latex \lambda \succeq 0&s=3$ and for arbitrary $latex \nu &s=3$, there holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu) = \inf\{L(z,\lambda,\nu):z \in D \} \leq L(x,\lambda,\nu) \leq f_0(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex g(\lambda,\nu) \leq p^\star &s=3$.

      (Indeed, $latex g(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ is a lower bound of $latex f_0(x) &s=3$ and $latex p^\star &s=3$ is the greatest lower bound of $latex f_0(x) &s=3$.)






    Lagrangian as underestimator.
    (See CO 5.1.4)
    Define the indicator functions

    $latex I_-(t)= \begin{cases} 0 &t \leq 0\\ +\infty & t> 0 \end{cases},\qquad I_0(t)= \begin{cases} 0 & t=0\\ +\infty & t\neq0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Then

    $latex \begin{aligned} I_-(f_i(x)) &\text{ indicates when the constraint }f_i \text{ is active}\\ I_0(h_i(x)) &\text{ indicates when the constraint }h_i \text{ is active} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and the (OP) is equivalent to

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{ minimize } f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^mI_-(f_i(x)) + \sum_{i=1}^p I_0(h_i(x)). \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: the terms in

    $latex \begin{aligned}\sum_{i=1}^mI_-(f_i(x)) + \sum_{i=1}^p I_0(h_i(x)) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    act as penalties for breaking the desired constraints.





    N.B.: if $latex x&s=3$ is feasible and $latex (\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}_+^m\times\mathbb{R}^p &s=3$, then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i f_i(x) &\leq I_-(f_i(x))\\ \nu_i h_i(x) &\leq I_0(h_i(x)) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and hence

    $latex \begin{aligned}L(x,\lambda,\nu) & = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i h_i(x) \\ &\leq f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^mI_-(f_i(x)) + \sum_{i=1}^p I_0(h_i(x))\end{aligned} &s=3$.

    Viz., $latex L &s=3$ is an underestimater of the objective function

    $latex f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^mI_-(f_i(x)) + \sum_{i=1}^p I_0(h_i(x)) &s=3$

    obtained by "softening" or "weakening" the penalty functions:

    $latex \begin{aligned} I_-(t) & \to ct, \quad c>0\\ I_0(t) & \to bt, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    In particular, for each $latex (\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}_+^m \times \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$, the problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize } L(x,\lambda,\nu) \end{cases} &s=3$

    has optimal value $latex g(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ and provides an underestimation of the original (OP).















    Example 1 Consider the least squares problem

    $latex (LS) \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & x^Tx\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

    for given

    $latex A = \begin{bmatrix} a_1^T \\ \vdots \\ a_p^T \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times n}, \quad a_i \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$.

    N.B.:

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax = b \iff h_i(x) = a_i^Tx - b_i = 0 \text{ for } i=1,\ldots,p \end{aligned}&s=3$.







    Therefore, the Lagrangian $latex L &s=3$ for (LS) is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L&:\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ \text{dom}\,L &= \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p\\ L(x,\nu) &= x^Tx + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i (a_i^Tx - b_i) \\ &= x^Tx + \nu^T(Ax-b) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and the Lagrange dual is

    $latex \begin{aligned} g&:\mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ g(\nu) &= \inf\{ x^Tx + \nu^T(Ax-b) : x \in \mathbb{R}^n \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    N.B.:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2_x L(x,\nu) &= \nabla_x^2 ( x^Tx + \nu^T(Ax-b)) \\ &= 2Id_{n \times n}\\ & \succeq 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so $latex x\mapsto L(x,\nu) &s=3$ is convex.
    Consequently,

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x^\star,\nu) = \inf\{L(x,\nu):x \in \mathbb{R}^n \} = \text{min}\{L(x,\nu):x \in \mathbb{R}^n \} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    iff

    $latex \nabla_x L(x^\star,\nu) = 2x^\star + A^T\nu = 0 &s=3$,

    i.e., iff

    $latex x^\star = -\frac{1}{2}A^T\nu. &s=3$







    In conclusion,

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\nu) &= L(x^\star,\nu)\\ &= (x^\star)^Tx^\star + \nu^T(Ax^\star-b)\\ &= \left(-\frac{1}{2}A^T\nu\right)^T\left(-\frac{1}{2}A^T\nu\right) +\nu^T\left(A\left(-\frac{1}{2}A^T\nu\right) - b\right)\\ &=\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \frac{1}{2}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \nu^Tb\\ &=-\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \nu^Tb. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    In particular,

    $latex -\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - b^T\nu \leq \inf\{x^Tx:Ax=b\}.&s=3$

    for all $latex \nu \in \mathbb{R}^p&s=3$.














    Example 2 Consider the linear program

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b\\ & x\succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    for given

    $latex c \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad A \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$.

    N.B.:
    • equality constraints given by

      $latex h_i(x) = a_i^Tx-b_i =0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p&s=3$.

    • $latex x\succeq 0 &s=3$ iff

      $latex x_i \geq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,n\quad &s=3$

      iff

      $latex \quad f_i(x) = -x_i \leq 0,\quad i=1,\ldots,n&s=3$.







    Therefore, the Lagrangian for (LP) is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L&: \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ \text{dom}\, L &=\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p \\ L(x,\lambda,\nu) &= c^Tx - \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i x_i + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i(a_i^Tx-b_i)\\ &= c^Tx - \lambda^Tx + \nu^T(Ax-b)\\ &=(c - \lambda + A^T\nu)^Tx - \nu^Tb \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Want to compute

    $latex g(\lambda,\nu) = \inf\{ L(x,\lambda,\nu) : x \in \mathbb{R}^n \}, &s=3$

    but

    $latex x \mapsto (c - \lambda + A^T\nu)^Tx - \nu^Tb &s=3$

    is an affine function with domain $latex \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.
    Therefore,

    $latex x \mapsto (c - \lambda + A^T\nu)^Tx - \nu^Tb &s=3$

    is bounded below iff $latex (\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ satisfy

    $latex c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0 &s=3$.







    Therefore, the Lagrange dual is

    $latex \begin{aligned} g&:\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ \text{dom}\, g &= \{(\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p : c - \lambda + A^T\nu= 0\}\\ g(\lambda,\nu) &= \begin{cases} -b^T\nu & c - \lambda + A\nu^T = 0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases}. \end{aligned} &s=3$



    In particular, for dual feasible $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$, there holds

    $latex -b^T\nu \leq c^Tx &s=3$

    for all feasible $latex x &s=3$.














    Return of Conjugate Function Recall: given $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}&s=3$, its conjugate function $latex f^*&s=3$ is the convex function

    $latex f^*(y) = \sup\{y^Tx - f(x) : x \in \text{dom}\,f\}.&s=3$

    Interestingly: the conjugate function is related to the Lagrange dual.



    Example.
    Consider the the (OP)

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}&Ax \preceq b\\ &Cx=d \end{cases}, &s=3$

    for given

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R},\quad A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, \quad b \in \mathbb{R}^m\\ C \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}, \quad d \in \mathbb{R}^p. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    We may write

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax\preceq b &\iff f_i(x) = a_i^Tx - b_i \leq0 , \quad i =1,\ldots, m\\ Cx = d &\iff h_i(x) =c_i^Tx - d_i=0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for suitable

    $latex a_i ,c_i \in \mathbb{R}^n. &s=3$







    The Lagrangian is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L&:\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ \text{dom}\, L &= D\\ L(x,\lambda,\nu) &= f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i(a_i^Tx - b_i) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i(c_i^Tx - d_i)\\ &= f_0(x) + \lambda^T(Ax-b) + \nu^T(Cx-d)\\ &= f_0(x) + \lambda^TAx + \nu^TCx - \lambda^Tb - \nu^Td \end{aligned} &s=3$







    We may now compute the Lagrange dual in terms of the conjugate $latex f_0^* &s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} g&:\mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}\\ g(\lambda,\nu)&= \inf\{L(x,\lambda,\nu):x \in D\}\\ &=\inf\{f_0(x) + \lambda^TAx + \nu^TCx - \lambda^Tb - \nu^Td : x \in D\}\\ &=\inf\{ (A^T\lambda + C^T\nu)^Tx + f_0(x):x \in D\} - \lambda^Tb - \nu^Td \\ &=-\sup\{-(A^T\lambda+C^T\nu)^Tx - f_0(x) : x \in D\} - \lambda^Tb - \nu^Td\\ &=-f_0^*(-A^T\lambda-C^T\nu) - \lambda^Tb - \nu^Td. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Since

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu)>-\infty \iff f_0^*(-A^T\lambda-C^T\nu)< +\infty \end{aligned}&s=3$,

    we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned}\text{dom}\,g = \{(\lambda,\nu)\in \mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{R}^p: -A^T\lambda-C^T\nu \in \text{dom}\,f_0^* \} \end{aligned}. &s=3$
















    Example: A Volume Minimizing Ellipsoid Problem. Given points $latex a_1,\ldots,a_m \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$, among all (closed) origin centered ellipsoids $latex \mathcal{E} &s=3$ satisfying $latex a_i \in \mathcal{E} &s=3$, find those with minimal volume.

    Plan. We will formulate this problem as a convex optimization problem and determine the Lagrange dual function.





    Positive semidefinite representation of ellipsoids.
    Given $latex x' \in \mathbb{R}^n, X \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$, the set

    $latex \mathcal{E}_X := \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : (x-x')^T X (x-x') \leq 1 \} &s=3$

    is an ellipsoid.
    Moreover, the volume of $latex \mathcal{E}_X &s=3$ is proportional to $latex (\det X^{-1})^{1/2}&s=3$.
    (This follows change of variable formula.)
    Justification WLOG: $latex X = \text{diag}(v) &s=3$ for some $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ whose entries are the positive eigenvalues of $latex X &s=3$.
    Then $latex (x-x_0)^TX(x-x_0) \leq 1 &s=3$ implies

    $latex \begin{aligned} (x-x')^TX(x-x') &= \begin{bmatrix} x_1-x'_1 & \cdots & x_n - x_n' \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}v_1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & v_2 & \cdots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\0&0&\cdots&v_n \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix} x_1-x'_1 \\ \vdots \\ x_n - x_n' \end{bmatrix}\\ &=v_1(x_1-x_1')^2 + \cdots + v_n(x_n-x'_n)^2\\ &\leq 1 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is the usual description of a closed ellipsoid with center $latex x' &s=3$.
    E.g., $latex X = r^{-2}\text{diag}(1,1,\cdots,1) &s=3$ gives the ball of radius $latex r &s=3$.






    Problem reformulation. Find those $latex X \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$ satisfying $latex a_i^T X a_i \leq 1 &s=3$ which minimize $latex (\det X^{-1})^{1/2}&s=3$.
    In fact, $latex f_0(X) = \log \det X^{-1} &s=3$ is convex, and so we may reformulate the problem as the following (COP):

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(X) = \log \det X^{-1}\\ &\text{dom} f_0 = \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n\\ \text{subject to} & a_i^T X a_i \leq 1. \end{cases} &s=3$







    Recall
    1. $latex \text{trace}(ABC) = \text{trace}(CAB) &s=3$ for $latex A,B,C \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}&s=3$;
    2. there is a natural way of identifying matrix $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &s=3$ with vector $latex v_A \in \mathbb{R}^{n^2} &s=3$;
    3. Under this identification, we have

      $latex \text{trace}(A^T B) = v_A^T v_B &s=3$.







    Let $latex A_i = a_ia_i^T &s=3$, noting $latex A_i^T = A_i &s=3$.
    Then 1. gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} a_i^TXa_i &= \text{trace}(a_i^TXa_i)\\ &= \text{trace}(a_ia_i^TX)\\ &= \text{trace}(A_iX). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore 2. and 3. allow us to realize the quadratic inequality

    $latex a_i^TXa_i \leq 1 &s=3$

    as the linear inequality

    $latex \text{trace}(A_i X) = v_{A_i}^T v_X \leq 1 &s=3$.

    These observations allow us to appeal to the Lagrange dual formalism.





    It can be shown

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0^*(Y) &= \log\det(-Y)^{-1} - n\\ \text{dom}f_0^* &= - \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    From preceding section, we can conclude the Lagrange dual function of $latex f_0(X) = \log\det X^{-1} &s=3$ is

    $latex g(\lambda) = \begin{cases} \log\det\left(\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_ia_i^T \right) - \boldsymbol{1}_m^T \lambda + n & \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i a_i^T \succ 0\\ -\infty & \text{ else} \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Since $latex g(\lambda) &s=3$ provides a lower bound of the optimal value, we conclude: if $latex V_0 &s=3$ is the optimal volume, then, up to known constant $latex c_0>0 &s=3$, there holds

    $latex c_0V_0 \geq \log\det\left(\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i a_i^T \right) - \boldsymbol{1}^T\lambda + n &s=3$,

    which is a very explicit lower bound depending only on the Lagrange multiplier and the problem data.





    Let

    $latex \mathcal{A} = \begin{bmatrix} v_{A_1}^T\\\vdots\\ v_{A_m}^T \end{bmatrix}, \quad \boldsymbol{1}_n = \begin{bmatrix} 1\\\vdots\\1\end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$.

    Therefore, the problem is equivalent to

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(X) = \log \det X^{-1}\\ &\text{dom} f_0 = \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n\\ \text{subject to} & \mathcal{A} v_X \leq \boldsymbol{1}_m. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Introducing the Lagrange multiplier $latex \lambda &s=3$, observe

    $latex \mathcal{A}^T\lambda = \lambda_1 v_{A_1} + \cdots + \lambda_m v_{A_m} &s=3$.

    Under our chosen identification $latex \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} \cong \mathbb{R}^{n^2} &s=3$, we identify

    $latex \lambda_i v_{A_i} \iff \lambda_i A_i = \lambda_i a_ia_i^T &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \mathcal{A}^T\lambda \iff \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_ia_i^T. &s=3$

    We lastly record the conjugate function of $latex f_0(X) = \log \det X^{-1} &s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_0^*(Y) &= \log\det(-Y)^{-1} - n\\ \text{dom}\,f_0^* &= -\boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    By previous section, the Lagrange dual is given by

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda) &= - f_0^*(-\mathcal{A}^T\lambda) - \lambda^T\boldsymbol{1}_m\\ &= \begin{cases} \log\det(\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i a_i^T ) - \boldsymbol{1}_m + n & \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i a_i^T \succ0 \\ -\infty &\text{else} \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    Since $latex g(\lambda) &s=3$ provides a lower bound of the optimal value, we conclude: if $latex V_0 &s=3$ is the optimal volume, then, up to known constant $latex c_0>0 &s=3$, there holds

    $latex c_0V_0 \geq \log\det\left(\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i a_i^T \right) - \boldsymbol{1}^T\lambda + n &s=3$,

    which is a very explicit lower bound depending only on the Lagrange multiplier and the problem data.
    The Dual Problem Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu) &=\text{ Lagrange dual function of a given (OP)}\\ p^\star &= \text{ optimal value of the (OP)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Recall

    $latex \lambda \succeq 0 \implies g(\lambda,\nu) \leq p^\star &s=3$



    Main point:

    $latex \sup\{ g(\lambda,\nu):\lambda \succeq0,\nu \in \mathbb{R}^p\} \leq p^\star &s=3$,

    suggests considering maximization problem with objective $latex g(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$.
    Gives best underestimate available by Lagrange dual function.





    Lagrange dual problem: the problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & g(\lambda,\nu)\\ \text{subject to} & \lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Remarks
    1. The original problem is called the primal problem.
      Viz.., the dual problem is dual to the primal problem.

    2. $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ feasible to dual problem $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex g(\lambda,\nu)>-\infty&s=3$.
      Viz., $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ is dual feasible.

    3. As stated, the only constraint is $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$; however, domain of $latex g &s=3$ usually has implicit constraints.

    4. Generally: $latex \text{dom}\,g &s=3$ has "dimension" less than or equal to $latex m+p &s=3$.

    5. Recall: $latex g&s=3$ is infimum of family of concave functions.
      Thus, $latex g &s=3$ is concave and $latex -g &s=3$ is convex.
      So,

      maximizing concave $latex g&s=3$ $latex \iff &s=3$ minimizing convex $latex -g &s=3$.


      Therefore, since $latex \lambda \succeq0&s=3$ is convex constraint:

      Dual problems are always convex, even if primal is not.



    6. Solutions $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$ to dual are called dual optimal.
















    Remark on Duality for Equivalent Problems Question: if two primal problems are equivalent, how are their respective duals related?





    Spoiler: The respective dual problems may be quite different; this is demonstrated by example.





    Example. Consider the unconstrained problem

    $latex \text{(OP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(Ax+b). \end{cases} &s=3$

    This problem is equivalent to the constrained problem

    $latex \text{(OP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(y)\\ \text{subject to} & y = Ax+b \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Having no constraints, the Lagrangian for (OP1) is

    $latex L(x) = f_0(Ax+b) &s=3$

    and so the Lagrange dual function is simply

    $latex g = \inf\{f_0(Ax+b)\}= p^\star .&s=3$

    Therefore, the dual problem of (OP1) trivializes to minimizing a constant.





    Having only the equality constraints $latex y = Ax+b &s=3$, the Lagrangian for (OP2) is

    $latex L(x,y,\nu) = f_0(y) + \nu^T(Ax+b-y) &s=3$.

    Observe that $latex L(x,y,\nu) &s=3$ is unbounded below if $latex A^T\nu \neq 0 &s=3$.
    Moreover, if $latex A^T\nu=0&s=3$, then

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\nu) &= \inf\{ f_0(y) + \nu^T b - \nu^T y \}\\ &=\nu^T b - \sup \{ \nu^Ty - f_0(y)\}\\ &= \nu^T b - f_0^*(\nu). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus,

    $latex g(\nu) = \begin{cases} \nu^Tb - f_0^*(\nu) & A^T\nu=0\\ -\infty & \text{else}. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Therefore, the dual problem to (OP2) is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} &b^T\nu - f_0^*(\nu)\\ \text{subject to }& A^T\nu = 0. \end{cases} &s=3$





    Conclusion: the dual of (OP2) is conceivable useful, whereas the dual of (OP1) is useless, even though (OP1) and (OP2) are equivalent.














    Example: Duality of standard form and inequality form LP Recall: a standard form LP is of the form

    $latex \text{(LP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b\\ & x \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$.

    An inequality form LP is of the form

    $latex \text{(LP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax \preceq b \end{cases} &s=3$.

    We will show the dual of (LP1) is (equivalent to a problem) of the form (LP2), and vice versa.





    The dual (LP1)
    We consider first:

    $latex \text{(LP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b\\ & x \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$.

    For (LP1), the Lagrangian is

    $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu) = (c + A^T\nu - \lambda)^Tx - b^T\nu &s=3$

    and Langrange dual function is

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu) &= \begin{cases} -b^T\nu & c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases}\\ \text{dom}\, g &= \{(\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^p : c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0\} \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    (Recall: domain is determined by where $latex L&s=3$ is bounded below.)





    Therefore, the dual problem of (LP1) is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}&g(\lambda,\nu)= \begin{cases} -b^T\nu & c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases}\\ \text{subject to}&\lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    which is evidently equivalent to

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}& -b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}&\lambda \succeq 0\\ & c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    N.B.: the domain of $latex g&s=3$ had the implicit constraint $latex c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0 &s=3$.





    Observe the equivalency of constraints:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} &\lambda \succeq 0\\ & c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0 \end{cases} & \iff \begin{cases} &\lambda \succeq 0\\ & c+ A^T\nu = \lambda \end{cases}\\ & \iff \begin{cases} & c+ A^T\nu \succeq 0 \end{cases}\\ & \iff \begin{cases} & c \succeq -A^T\nu \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Therefore

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}& -b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}&\lambda \succeq 0\\ & c - \lambda + A^T\nu = 0 \end{cases} \iff \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}& - A^T\nu \preceq c \end{cases} &s=3$

    The last problem is of the form (LP2).
    Viz., the dual of a standard form LP is (equivalent to) an inequality form LP.







    The dual of (LP2)
    We now consider

    $latex \text{(LP2)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax \preceq b. \end{cases} &s=3$.

    For (LP2), the Lagrangian is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda) &= c^Tx + \lambda^T(Ax-b) \\ &= (A^T\lambda + c)^Tx - b^T\lambda \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    N.B.: an affine function $latex \alpha^Tx + \beta &s=3$ is bounded below iff $latex \alpha = 0 &s=3$.
    Therefore, the Lagrange dual function is

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda) &= \begin{cases} -b^T\lambda & A^T\lambda + c = 0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases}\\ \text{dom}\,g &= \{\lambda \in \mathbb{R}^m : A^T\lambda + c = 0 \} \end{aligned} &s=3$.





    Therefore, the dual problem of (LP2) is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}&g(\lambda,\nu)= \begin{cases} -b^T\nu & A^T\lambda + c = 0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases}\\ \text{subject to}&\lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    which is evidently equivalent to

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}& -b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}& A^T\lambda + c = 0\\ & \lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Again, the domain of $latex g &s=3$ had an implicit constraint, namely, $latex A^T\lambda + c = 0 &s=3$.





    Observe the equivalency of constraints:

    $latex \begin{cases} &A^T\lambda + c = 0\\ & \lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases} \iff \begin{cases} &A^T\lambda = -c\\ & \lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$





    Therefore, the dual problem is equivalent to

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}& A^T\lambda =-c\\ &\lambda \succeq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    which is a problem of the form (LP1).
    Viz., the dual of an inequality form LP is a standard form LP.














    Weak and Strong Duality Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star &= \text{ optimal value of primal problem}\\ d^\star &= \text{ optimal value of dual problem} \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Weak duality: the property $latex d^\star \leq p^\star &s=3$.
    N.B.: Optimization problems of the form (OP) always satisfy weak duality.
    Strong duality: the property $latex d^\star = p^\star &s=3$.
    N.B.: Having strong duality does not mean the primal and the dual are actually solvable.
    Constraint qualifications: conditions for a given type of problem which ensure strong duality.
    E.g., "A (QCQP) with single quadratic constraint has strong duality if _______."
    Optimal duality gap: the difference $latex p^\star - d^\star &s=3$.






    Remarks
    1. Observe

      $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star = -\infty &\iff \text{ primal unbounded}\\ p^\star = +\infty & \iff \text{ primal infeasible}\\ d^\star = + \infty &\iff \text{ dual unbounded}\\ d^\star = -\infty &\iff \text{ dual infeasible}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    2. Therefore

      $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star = - \infty &\implies d^\star = -\infty \implies \text{ dual is infeasible}\\ d^\star = +\infty &\implies p^\star = +\infty \implies \text{ primal is infeasible}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    3. $latex 0<p^\star-d^\star<\infty &s=3$ is possible.
    4. Primal and dual may be simultaneously infeasible, i.e.,

      $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star &= + \infty\\ d^\star &=-\infty \end{aligned} &s=3$

      may occur.
    5. Convex optimization problems often have strong duality, but not always.















    Slater's Condition Consider the (COP)

    $latex \text{(COP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}&f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ &Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

    with domain $latex D&s=3$.
    Let $latex \text{relint}\,D &s=3$ denote the relative interior of $latex D &s=3$.
    Intuitively: $latex x\in \text{relint}\,D&s=3$ means $latex x \in D &s=3$ is not on the "boundary" of $latex D &s=3$
    Recall: $latex D &s=3$ generally has smaller dimension than number of variables.



    Slater's condition: there exists $latex x \in \text{relint}\,D &s=3$ such that

    $latex f_i(x)<0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m &s=3$ and $latex Ax = b &s=3$.

    Strict feasibility: when a feasible $latex x &s=3$ satisfies Slater's condition.



    Example Consider the inequality constraints

    $latex \begin{cases} &(x_1-1)^2 + x_2^2 \leq 1\\ &(x_1-2)^2 + x_2^2 \leq 4\\ &(x_1-3)^2+x_2^2 \leq 9 \end{cases} &s=3$

    and suppose $latex f_0 &s=3$ has

    $latex \text{dom}\,f_0 = \{ (x_1-3)^2+x_2^2 \leq 9 \} &s=3$.

    Then $latex (x_1,x_2)=(0,0)&s=3$ is feasible but not strictly feasible.
    Moreover, any point in the interior of the smallest disk satisfies Slater's condition.





    Slater's Theorem. If (COP) satisfies Slater's condition, then it is strongly dual and the dual problem is solvable

    Remarks.
    1. Slater's condition is a constraint qualification for convex optimization problems.
    2. In principle, an (OP) may be strongly dual without the dual being solvable.
      Thus, Slater's theorem has the strength of implying there is a dual feasible $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$ with $latex g(\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) = d^\star = p^\star &s=3$.






    Theorem. If Slater's condition holds for all non-affine inequality constraints, then conclusion of Slater's theorem hold.

    Remarks.
    1. Thus, if $latex f_1,\ldots,f_k &s=3$ are affine and $latex f_{k+1},\ldots,f_{m} &s=3$ are not, then it is enough if there holds the weakened Slater condition: there exists a $latex x \in \text{relint}\,D &s=3$ such that

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x) &\leq 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,k\\ f_i(x)&<0,\quad i=k+1,\ldots,m\\ Ax&=b \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    2. Therefore, Slater's condition is really a qualification constraint for non-affine inequality constraints.
















    Remark on Slater's Condition Consider the convex optimization problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Let $latex D \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be the domain.
    Since $latex D &s=3$ is convex, it lies in its affine hull $latex \text{aff}\,(D) &s=3$.
    Given $latex x\in \mathbb{R}^n, r>0 &s=3$,

    $latex B(x,r) = \{ y \in \mathbb{R}^n : |x-y|<r \} &s=3$

    be the Euclidean ball of radius $latex r &s=3$ and center $latex x &s=3$.
    Relative interior: the set

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{relint}(D) = \{ x \in D: \exists r>0 \text{ such that } B(x,r) \cap \text{aff}\,(D) \subset D \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Example. In the image below:
    • $latex D &s=3$ is the ellipse lying in the $latex xy &s=3$-plane.
    • The affine hull $latex \text{aff}\,(D) &s=3$ is the $latex xy &s=3$-plane.
    • The ball depicts a ball $latex B&s=3$ centered at a point in $latex D &s=3$ and with small enough radius so that $latex B \cap \text{aff}\,(D) &s=3$ still lies in $latex D &s=3$.
    • The relative interior $latex \text{relint}(D) &s=3$ is the shaded region of the ellipse excluding the curve bounding the domain.







    Question How can Slater's condition fail?
    I.e., what if there exist no $latex x \in \text{relint}(D)&s=3$ such that

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x) &< 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ Ax&=b? \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Consider the (COP)

    $latex \text{(COP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_1(x) \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b \end{cases}. &s=3$

    where $latex f_0,f_1: \mathbb{R}^3 \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ are convex.
    Suppose
    • $latex f_1 \leq 0 &s=3$ describes a cube:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \{f_1(x_1,x_2,x_3) \leq 0 \} = \{0 \leq x_1, x_2, x_3 \leq 1 \} = : C, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    • $latex \text{dom}\,f_0 = \text{dom}\,f_1 = \{ x_3 \leq 1 \} &s=3$,
    • the solution set

      $latex \{Ax = b \} = \{ x_3 = 1 \} &s=3$

      is a plane intersecting the top face of the cube.






    The images below depict the cube, the plane and their intersection (a square).
    N.B.: the domain is the area below and including the plane.







    Then the domain is $latex D = \{x_3 \leq 1 \} &s=3$ and (COP) fails Slater's condition:

    if $latex x \in \text{relint}(D) = \{x_3<1\} &s=3$, then $latex Ax = b &s=3$ must fail.


    Fix: Can project problem onto a lower dimensional face of $latex C &s=3$.
    Indeed, since

    $latex C \cap \{ Ax=b\}=\{0\leq x_1,x_2 \leq 1, x_3 = 1 \} &s=3$,

    (COP) is equivalent to the problem

    $latex \text{(COP1)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & F_0(x_1,x_2)\\ \text{subject to} & -x_1 \leq 0\\ &x_1 -1 \leq 0\\ &-x_2 \leq 0\\ &x_2 -1 \leq 0 \end{cases}, &s=3$

    where $latex F_0(x_1,x_2) = f_0(x_1,x_2,1) &s=3$.
    Taking $latex x_1=x_2=\frac{1}{2} &s=3$, we see (COP1) satisfies Slater's condition.





    Comparing Duals and KKT Conditions.
    Let $latex L_0 &s=3$ and $latex L_1 &s=3$ be the Lagrangian for (COP) and (COP1) respectively.
    Then

    $latex \begin{aligned} L_0(x,\lambda,\nu) &= f_0(x) + \lambda f_1(x) + \nu(x_3-1)\\ x &= \begin{bmatrix} x_1 &x_2&x_3 \end{bmatrix}^T\\ L_1(x',\lambda') &= F_0(x_1,x_2) -\lambda_1 x_1 + \lambda_2(x_1-1) - \lambda_3 x_2 + \lambda_4 (x_2-1)\\ x' &= \begin{bmatrix} x_1 &x_2 \end{bmatrix}^T\\ \lambda' &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & \lambda_2 & \lambda_3 & \lambda_4 \end{bmatrix}^T. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The respective KKT conditions are

    $latex \begin{cases} \begin{aligned} f_1(x) & \leq 0\\ x_3-1&=0\\ \lambda f_1(x) &=0 \\ \lambda &\geq 0\\ \nabla f_0 + \lambda \nabla f_1 + \begin{bmatrix}0\\0\\\nu\end{bmatrix} &=0 \end{aligned} \end{cases} \quad \begin{cases} \begin{aligned} 0 \leq x_1 &\leq 1\\ 0 \leq x_2 &\leq 1\\ \lambda_1 x_1 = \lambda_2 (x_1-1)&=0\\ \lambda_3 x_2 = \lambda_4 (x_2-1)&=0\\ \lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3,\lambda_4 &\geq 0\\ \nabla F_0 + \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_2-\lambda_1\\\lambda_4 - \lambda_3 \end{bmatrix} &=0 \end{aligned} \end{cases} &s=3$







    Remarks.
    1. While (COP) does not satisfy Slater's conditions, its projection (COP1) does.
    2. $latex f_1 &s=3$ might not even be differentiable, in which the KKT conditions for (COP) would be ill-posed.
    3. Identifying the correct face to project problem to $latex \implies &s=3$ relatively simpler KKT conditions.
      (Not true in general.)






    Geometric description of Slater's condition failing:
    • Consider a general (COP) with convex domain $latex D &s=3$.
    • Suppose the relative boundary of $latex D &s=3$ contains a convex set $latex K &s=3$ (e.g., a polygonal face or $latex D &s=3$ is conic)
    • If $latex \text{aff}(K) = \{Ax=b\}&s=3$ or $latex \{Ax=b\} \cap D = K &s=3$, then the problem fails Slater's condition.
    • Indeed, any feasible point must satisfy $latex Ax=b &s=3$ and lie on the relative boundary (and hence not in the relative interior).
    • N.B.: a problem even "almost" failing Slater's condition can cause numerical issues.
      E.g., a 3-dimensional problem with nearly 2-dimensional domain.















    Examples Example 1.
    Consider the least squares problem

    $latex \text{(LS)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}&x^Tx\\ \text{subject to}& Ax = b \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Recall: the Lagrangian is

    $latex L(x,\nu) = x^Tx + \nu^T(Ax-b) &s=3$

    and Lagrange dual function is

    $latex g(\nu) = -\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \nu^Tb &s=3$.

    Therefore, the dual problem is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}& -\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \nu^Tb \end{cases}. &s=3$







    N.B.: (LP) has no inequality constraints and $latex D = \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.
    Thus, Slater's condition is simply:

    there exists $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ such that $latex Ax=b&s=3$, i.e., $latex b \in \text{range}\,A&s=3$.

    We will analyze this closer.





    Case 1 ($latex b \in \text{range}\,A &s=3$):
    Here, Slater's condition is satisfied since $latex b \in \text{range}\,A &s=3$ implies there exists $latex x &s=3$ with $latex Ax=b&s=3$.
    Therefore, primal is feasible and hence $latex p^\star < +\infty &s=3$.
    Slater's theorem implies

    $latex d^\star = p^\star < +\infty &s=3$.

    In particular, the dual objective

    $latex -\frac{1}{4}\nu^TAA^T\nu - \nu^Tb&s=3$

    is bounded above.





    Case 2 ($latex b \notin \text{range}\,A &s=3$):
    Here, the primal is infeasible and so $latex p^\star = +\infty &s=3$.
    Note $latex b \notin\text{range}\,A &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ exists $latex z &s=3$ such that $latex A^Tz = 0 &s=3$ and $latex b^Tz \neq 0 &s=3$.
    (Recall: $latex \text{ker}\,A^T \perp \text{range}\,A&s=3$.)
    But then

    $latex g(tz)=-\frac{1}{4}tz^TAAtz - b^Ttz = -b^Ttz &s=3$,

    which is unbounded above as a function of $latex t&s=3$, and so $latex d^\star=+\infty &s=3$.






    In conclusion: for (LS), there holds $latex d^\star = p^\star &s=3$, even when $latex p^\star = \infty &s=3$.
    Therefore, (LS) is strongly dual whether feasible or infeasible.







    Example 2.
    Consider the standard form (LP)

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b\\ & x \succeq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    We have already shown that its dual problem is

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& b^T\nu \\ \text{subject to}& - A^T\nu \preceq c \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Since the inequality constraints of (LP) are affine, namely,

    $latex x_i \geq 0 &s=3$

    weakened version of Slater's condition implies problem is strongly dual when feasible.
    Interestingly: (LP) may fail to be strongly dual when infeasible, i.e., there may hold $latex p^\star = +\infty &s=3$ and $latex d^\star = -\infty&s=3$.







    Example 3.
    Consider the QCQP

    $latex \text{(QCQP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_0x + q_0^Tx + r_0\\ \text{subject to}& \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_ix + q_i^Tx + r_i \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m \end{cases} &s=3$

    where
