    $latex Q_0 \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n, \quad Q_i \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n,\quad i=1, \ldots, m. &s=3$

    We now determine the dual problem.





    The Lagrangian of (QCQP) is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda) &= \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_0x + q_0^Tx + r_0\\ &+\sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{2}\lambda_i x^TQ_ix + \lambda_i q_i^Tx + \lambda_i r_i\\ &= \frac{1}{2}x^T\left(Q_0+\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i Q_i \right)x \\ &+ \left(q_0^T + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i q_i^T \right)x \\ &+ r_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i r_i \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Defining

    $latex \begin{aligned} Q(\lambda) &= Q_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i Q_i\\ q(\lambda) &= q_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i q_i\\ r(\lambda) &= r_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i r_i, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we have

    $latex L(x,\lambda) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQ(\lambda)x + q(\lambda)^Tx + r(\lambda) &s=3$.







    We now compute the Lagrange dual function $latex g(\lambda) &s=3$ for $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$.

    To begin, observe: if $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$, then

    $latex Q(\lambda) = Q_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i Q_i \succeq 0 &s=3$

    due to positive semidefiniteness of $latex Q_0 &s=3$.
    So: $latex Q(\lambda) &s=3$ is invertible and

    $latex L(x,\lambda) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQ(\lambda)x + q(\lambda)^Tx + r(\lambda) &s=3$.

    is convex in $latex x&s=3$.
    Therefore, $latex g(\lambda) &s=3$ is determined by critical points of $latex L(x,\lambda)&s=3$.





    Compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x,\lambda) &= \nabla_x \left(\frac{1}{2}x^TQ(\lambda)x\right) + \nabla_x\left( q(\lambda)^Tx \right) + \nabla_x r(\lambda)\\ &=Q(\lambda)x + q(\lambda). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus, $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a minimizer of $latex L(x,\lambda) &s=3$ iff

    $latex \nabla_x L(x^\star,\lambda)=0 \iff x^\star = -Q(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda) &s=3$.





    Therefore,

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda) &= \inf\{L(x,\lambda): x \in \mathbb{R}^n\}\\ &=\min\{L(x,\lambda):x \in \mathbb{R}^n \}\\ &=L(x^\star,\lambda)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(-Q(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda))^TQ(\lambda)(-Q(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda)) \\ &+ q(\lambda)^T(-Q(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda)) + r(\lambda)\\ &=-\frac{1}{2}q(\lambda)^TQ(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda) + r(\lambda). \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Therefore, the primal problem

    $latex \text{(QCQP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_0x + q_0^Tx + r_0\\ \text{subject to}& \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_ix + q_i^Tx + r_i \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m \end{cases} &s=3$

    has dual problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize}&-\frac{1}{2}q(\lambda)^TQ(\lambda)^{-1}q(\lambda) + r(\lambda)\\ \text{subjet to} & \lambda \succeq0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Slater's theorem implies that these two problems are strongly dual if there exists a strictly feasible $latex x&s=3$ satisfying

    $latex \frac{1}{2}x^TQ_ix + q_i^Tx + r_i < 0 &s=3$

    for all $latex i =1,\ldots,m&s=3$.














    Qualitative Uses of Lagrange Duality Recall: a problem has

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{weak duality when }& d^\star \leq p^\star;\\ \text{strong duality when }&d^\star = p^\star. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    These are qualitative properties; e.g., strong duality alone does not provide means to find primal optimal $latex x^\star &s=3$ satisfying $latex f_0(x^\star) = p^\star &s=3$;






    However, strong and weak duality provide three useful "qualitative" uses:

    1. Certification: a dual feasible $latex (\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ provides a certification that $latex g(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ is a suboptimal value: $latex g(\lambda,\nu) \leq p^\star &s=3$.
      Strong duality $latex \implies &s=3$ can (theoretically) certify up to any desirable precision.





      Duality gap: for primal feasible $latex x &s=3$ and dual feasible $latex (\lambda,\nu) &s=3$, the value

      $latex f_0(x) - g(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$.

      N.B.: if $latex x,(\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ feasible, then

      $latex g(\lambda,\nu) \leq d^\star \leq p^\star \leq f_0(x),&s=3$

      i.e.,

      $latex p^\star,d^\star \in [g(\lambda,\nu),f_0(x)] &s=3$

      and the duality gap gives length of interval.



      In particular: if duality gap $latex = 0 &s=3$ at a feasible pair $latex x,(\lambda,\nu) &s=3$, then

      $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu) \leq d^\star &\leq p^\star \leq f_0(x)\\ g(\lambda,\nu) &= f_0(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      give

      $latex p^\star = f_0(x) = g(\lambda,\nu) = d^\star &s=3$,

      i.e., such $latex (\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ certifies that $latex x &s=3$ is optimal, and vice versa.





    2. Stopping Criterion. Observing

      $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda,\nu) \leq p^\star & \iff -p^\star \leq - g(\lambda,\nu)\\ &\iff f_0(x) - p^\star \leq f_0(x) - g(\lambda,\nu), \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and setting

      $latex \begin{aligned} \epsilon = f_0(x) - g(\lambda,\nu) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      we see

      $latex \begin{aligned} (\lambda,\nu) \text{ is dual feasible} \ \implies \text{primal feasible }x \text{ is }\epsilon\text{-suboptimal} \end{aligned}. &s=3$

      Viz.,

      $latex f_0(x) - p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$.

      N.B.: this is showing $latex x &s=3$ is $latex \epsilon &s=3$-suboptimal without even knowing the primal optimal $latex p^\star &s=3$.



      As application: suppose we wish to find optimal $latex x^\star &s=3$ but can settle for feasible $latex x'&s=3$ with $latex f_0(x') &s=3$ at worst $latex \epsilon &s=3$-suboptimal:

      $latex f_0(x') - p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$.

      Suppose we use algorithm producing feasible $latex x^{(k)},(\lambda^{(k)},\nu^{(k)})&s=3$ in search of optimal $latex x^\star &s=3$.
      Letting

      $latex \epsilon_k =f_0(x^{(k)}) - g(\lambda^{(k)},\nu^{(k)}) &s=3$

      denote the resulting duality gaps, we may use the following stopping criterion:

      $latex \text{If } \epsilon_k \leq \epsilon \text{, then stop search} &s=3$.

      Therefore, $latex K &s=3$ with $latex \epsilon_K \leq \epsilon &s=3$ gives feasible $latex x'=x_K&s=3$ within allowed error: $latex f(x') - p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$.



      N.B.:
      1. Re-emphasize: this stopping criterion does not require knowing the primal optimal $latex p^\star &s=3$ in advance.
      2. strong duality $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex \epsilon &s=3$ can be arbitrarily small.






    3. Complementary slackness. Assume problem has strong duality, i.e., $latex p^\star = d^\star &s=3$.
      If $latex x^\star &s=3$ is primal optimal and $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ is dual optimal, then

      $latex \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m&s=3$.

      For example:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} f^T(x^\star)\\ \hline \lambda^{\star T} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} f_1(x^\star) & 0 & 0 & f_4(x^\star) & \cdots & 0 & f_{m-1}(x^\star) & 0\\ \hline 0 & \lambda_2^\star & \lambda_3^\star & 0 & \cdots & \lambda_{m-2}^\star & 0 & \lambda_m^\star \end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      where $latex f &s=3$ is the vector of inequality constraint functions.
      This relationship between the two vectors is called complementary slackness.
      N.B.:
      1. Since $latex \lambda^\star \succeq 0 &s=3$ and $latex f_i(x^\star)\leq0 &s=3$, we have

        $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i^\star > 0 & \implies f_i(x^\star) = 0\\ f_i(x^\star) < 0 & \implies \lambda_i^\star = 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      2. While a qualitative property, complementary slackness can sometimes be used to solve the primal.
      3. Having $latex \lambda_i^\star = f_i(x^\star)= 0 &s=3$ is permissible.






      Justification: for $latex x^\star &s=3$ primal optimal and $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$ dual optimal, we find

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x^\star) &= g(\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)\\ &= \inf\{ f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x) : x \text{ feasible} \}\\ &\leq f_0(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x^\star)\\ &\leq f_0(x^\star). \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Since

      $latex a\leq b \leq c \leq a \implies a=b=c &s=3$,

      and since $latex h_i(x^\star) = 0&s=3$, we conclude

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x^\star) = f(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) = 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$







      Since $latex \lambda^\star \succeq0 &s=3$ and $latex f_i(x^\star) \leq 0 &s=3$, the sum

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      is a sum of nonpositive things and so

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) = 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      implies

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      which is the desired complementary slackness.















    Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) Conditions Assume $latex f_i,h_i &s=3$ are differentiable and have open domains.

    In previous section, we saw: if $latex x^\star,(\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ are optimal with zero optimality gap, then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \inf\{ f_0(x) + &\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x) : x \text{ feasible} \} \\ &= f_0(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x^\star). \end{aligned} &s=3$



    Question: What does this say about relationship between $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu)&s=3$, $latex x^\star &s=3$ and $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$ (under strong duality)?





    Above is equivalent to:

    $latex \inf\{L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star):x \text{ feasible}\} = L(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star), &s=3$.

    or

    $latex x^\star \in \text{argmin}\{L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star): x \text{ feasible} \} &s=3$.

    Viz., if
    • problem is strongly dual,
    • $latex x^\star &s=3$ is primal optimal, and
    • $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ is dual optimal,
    then $latex x^\star &s=3$ minimizes the Lagrangian $latex L(\cdot,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ with dual optimal Lagrange multipliers.





    But, if
    • $latex x^\star &s=3$ minimizes $latex L(\cdot,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ and
    • $latex f_i,h_i &s=3$ are differentiable,
    then $latex x \mapsto L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ is differentiable and $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a critical point:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_xL(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &= \nabla f_0(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^m\lambda^\star_i \nabla f_i(x^\star) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu^\star_i \nabla h_i(x^\star)\\ &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Beware: we do not know a priori if $latex \nabla f_0(x^\star) &s=3$ or $latex \nabla h_i (x^\star) &s=3$ are zero.





    Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) Optimality Conditions:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x^\star) & \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x^\star) & = 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,p\\ \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ \nabla_x L(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Recap:
    • these are necessary conditions for any strongly dual optimization problem admitting primal optimal $latex x^\star &s=3$ and dual optimal $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star); &s=3$
    • the first and second conditions just indicate $latex x^\star &s=3$ is primal feasible;
    • the third condition is the complementary slackness derived in previous section;
    • the fourth condition is standard nonnegativity of Lagrange multiplier $latex \lambda; &s=3$
    • the last condition follows from $latex x^\star &s=3$ minimizing $latex L(\cdot,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$.















    KKT and Convexity Theorem. If the primal problem is differentiable and convex, then the KKT conditions are sufficient for primal and dual optimality and strong duality.

    Viz., for differentiable convex problems, if $latex x' &s=3$ and $latex (\lambda',\nu')&s=3$ satisfy the KKT conditions, then they automatically primal and dual optimal, respectively.
    Proof. Step 1. Suppose $latex x' &s=3$ and $latex (\lambda',\nu')&s=3$ satisfy the KKT conditions:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x') & \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x') & = 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,p\\ \lambda_i' f_i(x') &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda' &\succeq 0\\ \nabla_x L(x',\lambda',\nu') &=0 \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    First two conditions $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex x'&s=3$ is primal feasible.





    Step 2. Observe

    $latex \lambda' \succeq 0 \implies \lambda'_if_i(x)&s=3$ are convex.

    Thus

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda',\nu') = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i' f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i' h_i(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    as a function of $latex x&s=3$ is a sum of convex functions and hence convex.

    Therefore $latex \nabla_x L(x',\lambda',\nu') = 0 \implies &s=3$ $latex x' &s=3$ is a minimizer.
    This also implies $latex (\lambda',\nu') &s=3$ is dual feasible since

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda',\nu') &= \inf\{ L(x,\lambda',\nu'): x \text{ feasible} \} \\ &= L(x',\lambda',\nu')\\ &> -\infty \end{aligned} &s=3$.







    Step 3. By Step 2., feasibility of $latex x' &s=3$ and the complementary slackness

    $latex \lambda_i' f_i(x') = 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m, &s=3$

    there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda',\nu') &= L(x',\lambda',\nu')\\ &= f_0(x') + \sum_{i=1}^m\lambda'_i f_i(x') + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu'_i h_i(x')\\ &=f_0(x'). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, the duality gap $latex f_0(x') - g(\lambda',\nu') &s=3$ vanishes and hence $latex x' &s=3$ is primal optimal and $latex (\lambda',\nu')&s=3$ is dual optimal.

    Indeed, recall:

    $latex g(\lambda',\nu') \leq d^\star \leq p^\star \leq f_0(x')&s=3$

    and so $latex g(\lambda',\nu')=f_0(x') &s=3$ implies

    $latex g(\lambda',\nu') = d^\star = p^\star = f_0(x'). &s=3$









    Corollary. If the primal problem is differentiable, convex and satisfies Slater's condition, then the KKT conditions are necessary and sufficient for primal and dual optimality and strong duality.

    Viz.: in this situation, finding all solutions to KKT conditions provides all solutions to the given problem.





    Remark. In convex optimization, many algorithms are conceived as methods for solving KKT conditions.
    Moreover, some KKT conditions for some problems may be solved analytically.














    Example 1 (CO Example 5.1)
    Consider the quadratic program

    $latex \text{(QP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx + r\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

    with $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$.

    Goal: Derive the KKT conditions for (QP) and solve (QP).





    Step 0. Observe that (QP) is a differentiable convex problem which trivially satisfies Slater's conditions and so the KKT conditions may be used to solve it.






    Step 1. Find the Lagrangian and its gradient:
    (QP) only has the equality constraint $latex Ax = b &s=3$ and so the Lagrangian is

    $latex L(x,\nu) = \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx + r + \nu^T(Ax-b) &s=3$.

    Differentiating with respect to $latex x&s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x,\nu) &= \nabla_x(\frac{1}{2}x^TQx) + \nabla_x(q^Tx) + \nabla_x r + \nabla_x(\nu^TAx - \nu^T b) \\ &= Qx + q +A^T\nu. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Step 2. Construct the KKT conditions:
    Since (QP) has no inequality constraints, the KKT conditions take the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} h_i(x^\star) &= 0, \quad i= 1,\ldots,p\\ \nabla_x L(x^\star,\nu^\star) &= 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Viz., the KKT conditions for (QP) are

    $latex \begin{aligned} Ax^\star &= b \\ Qx^\star + q + A^T\nu^\star&=0. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Rewriting the KKT conditions as

    $latex \begin{aligned} Qx^\star + A^T\nu^\star&=-q\\ Ax^\star &= b, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    the KKT conditions are evidently equivalent to the matrix equation

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x^\star\\ \nu^\star \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -q\\ b \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$







    Conclusion: Solving the quadratic program

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^Tx + r\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

    is equivalent to solving the linear equation

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x^\star\\ \nu^\star \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -q\\ b \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$
















    Example 2 (CO Example 5.2)
    Consider the convex optimization problem

    $latex \text{(WF)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & -\sum_{i=1}^n \log(\alpha_i + x_i)\\ \text{subject to} & \boldsymbol{1}^Tx = 1\\ & x \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex \alpha_i > 0 &s=3$ and $latex \boldsymbol{1} \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ is the vector of 1's.

    Goal: Derive the KKT conditions for (WF) and solve (WF).





    Step 0. Observe
    • the domain of (WF) contains the nonnegative orthant $latex x \succeq 0 &s=3$.
    • (WF) is a differentiable convex problem.
    • The condition $latex x \succeq 0 &s=3$ is equivalent to $latex -x \preceq 0 &s=3$.
    • (WF) satisfies Slater's condition since there exist $latex -x \prec0 &s=3$ with $latex \boldsymbol{1}^Tx = 1 &s=3$ and so (WF) satisfies strong duality.
    • Therefore, we may use KKT conditions to solve (WF).






    Step 1. Find the Lagrangian and its gradient:
    Given the constraints

    $latex \begin{cases}&\boldsymbol{1}^Tx = 1\\ & -x \preceq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    the Lagrangian is

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda,\nu) = -\sum_{i=1}^n \log(\alpha_i + x_i) - \lambda^T x + \nu(\boldsymbol{1}^Tx - 1) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    with Lagrange multipliers

    $latex \lambda \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad \nu \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$.

    Differentiating with respect to $latex x&s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x,\lambda,\nu) &= -\sum_{i=1}^n \nabla_x \log (\alpha_i + x_i) - \nabla_x(\lambda^T x) +\nu \nabla_x(\boldsymbol{1}^Tx) -\nabla_x\nu\\ &= -\frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i} \boldsymbol{1} - \lambda + \nu \boldsymbol{1}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus, $latex x^\star &s=3$ is a critical point of $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ if it satisfies the system of equations

    $latex -\frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i} - \lambda_i + \nu = 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots, n . &s=3$







    Step 2. Construct the KKT conditions:
    Since (WF) has inequality and equality constraints, the KKT conditions take the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x^\star) & \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x^\star) & = 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,p\\ \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ \nabla_x L(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &=0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus, the KKT conditions for (WF) are

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^\star &\succeq 0 \\ \boldsymbol{1}^Tx^\star & = 1\\ \lambda_i^\star x_i^\star &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,n\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ -\frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} - \lambda_i^\star + \nu^\star &= 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots, n . \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Observe:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i^\star x_i^\star &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,n\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ -\frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} - \lambda_i^\star + \nu^\star &= 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots, n . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    is equivalent to

    $latex \begin{aligned} \left(\nu^\star - \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} \right) x_i^\star &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,n\\ \nu^\star &\geq \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star}, \quad i = 1,\ldots, n . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    (In particular, $latex \lambda^\star &s=3$ is acting as a slack variable.)





    Therefore, we wish to solve:

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^\star &\succeq 0 \\ \boldsymbol{1}^Tx^\star & = 1\\ \left(\nu^\star - \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} \right) x_i^\star &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,n\\ \nu^\star &\geq \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star}, \quad i = 1,\ldots, n . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    We will solve for $latex x_i^\star &s=3$ in terms of $latex \nu^\star&s=3$ by considering two cases.





    Case 1: $latex \nu^\star < \frac{1}{\alpha_i} &s=3$.
    Observe

    $latex \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} \leq \nu^\star < \frac{1}{\alpha_i} &s=3$

    is only possible for $latex x\succeq 0&s=3$ if $latex x_i^\star >0 &s=3$.
    Then the complementary slackness

    $latex \left(\nu^\star - \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} \right) x_i^\star = 0 &s=3$

    enforces $latex \nu^\star = \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i}&s=3$.





    Case 2: $latex \nu^\star \geq \frac{1}{\alpha_i} &s=3$
    If $latex x_i >0 &s=3$, then

    $latex \nu^\star - \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star}>0 &s=3$

    and so the complementary slackness

    $latex \left(\nu^\star - \frac{1}{\alpha_i + x_i^\star} \right) x_i^\star = 0 &s=3$

    furnishes the contradiction $latex x_i^\star = 0 &s=3$.
    Thus $latex \nu^\star \geq \frac{1}{\alpha_i} \implies x_i^\star = 0 &s=3$.





    Putting the two cases together:

    $latex \begin{aligned} x_i^\star &= \begin{cases} \frac{1}{\nu^\star} - \alpha_i & \nu^\star < \frac{1}{\alpha_i}\\ 0 & \nu^\star \geq \frac{1}{\alpha_i} \end{cases}\\ &= \max\{ 0 ,\frac{1}{\nu^\star} - \alpha_i \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$





    Next, using $latex \boldsymbol{1}^Tx = 1 &s=3$, we get

    $latex \begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^n \max\{0,\frac{1}{\nu^\star} - \alpha_i\} =1. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    This is enough to solve for $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ and hence $latex x^\star &s=3$.





    Further details: Consider the function

    $latex G(t) = \sum_{i=1}^n \max\{0,t-\alpha_i\} &s=3$

    with $latex 0<\alpha_1 < \alpha_2 < \cdots < \alpha_n &s=3$.
    Observe

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{on }[0,\alpha_1] \text{ there holds }& G(t)=0\\ \text{on }[\alpha_1,\alpha_2] \text{ there holds }& G(t) = t-\alpha_1\\ \text{on }[\alpha_2,\alpha_3] \text{ there holds }& G(t) = t-\alpha_1 + t-\alpha_2 = 2t - \alpha_1 - \alpha_2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so on.
    Moreover, $latex G&s=3$ is continuous.
    Thus $latex G(t) &s=3$ is an increasing continuous piecewise linear function.
    Then $latex G(t) = 1 &s=3$ may be solved by finding when the graph of $latex G(t) &s=3$ crosses the horizontal line $latex y = 1 &s=3$.














    Perturbation Given the OP:

    $latex \text{(OP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p, \end{cases} &s=3$

    a natural question is:

    How do $latex p^\star &s=3$ and $latex x^\star &s=3$ behave under perturbations of constraints?

    More precisely: given $latex u \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$, $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$, consider the perturbed problem

    $latex \text{(OP)}_{uv} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq u_i, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x) = v_i, \quad i=1,\ldots,p, \end{cases} &s=3$

    Observe
    • $latex (u,v)=(0,0) &s=3$ results in $latex \text{(OP)}_{uv} = \text{(OP)}_{00} = \text{(OP)} &s=3$;
    • $latex u_i>0 &s=3$ results in relaxing $latex f_i(x) \leq 0 &s=3$;
    • $latex u_i<0 &s=3$ results in tightening $latex f_i(x) \leq 0 &s=3$;
    • $latex v_i \neq 0 &s=3$ results in "translating" solution set of $latex h_i(x)=0&s=3$.






    Example 1.
    The image below depicts various perturbations in inequality constraints (the shaded regions) and equality constraints (the dashed contours).
    N.B.: perturbing the equality constraint results in using different contours.





    Example 2.
    The three images below depict the constraint

    $latex x^2+y^2 - 1 \leq u &s=3$

    with $latex u=-0.5,0,0.5&s=3$, respectively.
    The three images below depict the constraint

    $latex x+y-1=v &s=3$

    with $latex u=-0.5,0,0.5&s=3$, respectively.






    The optimality function
    For $latex u \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$ and $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$, let $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$ denote the primal optimal value for $latex \text{(OP)}_{uv} &s=3$.
    Can therefore introduce the function

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star(\cdot,\cdot) : \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which assigns perturbation $latex (u,v) &s=3$ the primal optimal value $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$. N.B.:
    • $latex p^\star(0,0)=p^\star= &s=3$primal optimal value of unperturbed problem;
    • If $latex p^\star(u,v)=+\infty &s=3$, then the perturbation $latex (u,v) &s=3$ makes the problem infeasible.
    • If $latex p^\star(u,v)=-\infty &s=3$, then the perturbation $latex (u,v) &s=3$ makes the problem unbounded.
    • If (OP) is convex, then $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$ is convex in $latex (u,v) &s=3$.






    Example
    Consider the problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x) = -\sqrt{x}\\ \text{subject to} & x-1 \leq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Given $latex \text{dom}\,f_0 = \mathbb{R}_+ &s=3$ and the constraint, problem is:

    minimize $latex -\sqrt{x}&s=3$ on $latex [0,1]&s=3$.

    Evidently, $latex x^\star = 1, p^\star = -1 &s=3$.





    Consider now the perturbation

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x) = -\sqrt{x}\\ \text{subject to} & x-1 \leq u \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Viz.,

    minimize $latex -\sqrt{x}&s=3$ on $latex [0,1+u]&s=3$.

    Given $latex \text{dom}\,f_0 = \mathbb{R}_+ &s=3$, perturbed problem is feasible only for $latex u \geq -1 &s=3$.
    Can thus conclude

    $latex p^\star(u) = \begin{cases} -\sqrt{1+u} & u \geq -1\\ +\infty & u<-1 \end{cases}. &s=3$







    The graph of $latex p^\star(u)&s=3$ is plotted below; observe the behavior of $latex p^\star(u) &s=3$ as the constraint is relaxed.














    Sensitivity Theorem. If the primal has strong duality and the dual optimal $latex d^\star &s=3$ is achieved by dual feasible $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$, then

    $latex p^\star(0,0)- \lambda^{\star T}u - \nu^{\star T}v \leq p^\star(u,v) &s=3$

    for any $latex u \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$ and $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$.

    Proof. Fix the perturbation vector $latex (u,v)&s=3$ and let $latex x&s=3$ be feasible for the resulting perturbed problem:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x) &\leq u_i, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x) &= v_i, \quad i=1,\ldots,p. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Observe:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} f_i(x) &\leq u_i\\ \lambda^\star & \succeq 0 \end{aligned} &\implies \lambda_i^\star f_i(x) \leq \lambda_i^\star u_i \\ &\implies \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x) \leq \lambda^{\star T}u\\ h_i(x) = v_i &\implies \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x) = \nu^{\star T} v. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Using this and strong duality gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star(0,0)&=g(\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)\\ &\leq f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i^\star h_i(x)\\ &\leq f_0(x) + \lambda^{\star T}u + \nu^{\star T}v. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Rearranging gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star(0,0)- \lambda^{\star T}u - \nu^{\star T}v \leq f_0(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for all $latex x &s=3$ feasible for the perturbed problem.
    Since LHS independent of $latex x &s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star(0,0)- \lambda^{\star T}u - \nu^{\star T}v \leq p^\star(u,v). \end{aligned} &s=3$










    Remark. Using this theorem, the sizes and signs of $latex \lambda_i,\nu_i&s=3$ may determine the sensitivity of the primal optimal value subjected to perturbation.





    Example Suppose Theorem inequality takes the form

    $latex p^\star(u,v) \geq p^\star(0,0) - \lambda^\star u - \nu^\star v, \quad \lambda,\nu,u,v\in\mathbb{R}&s=3$.

    We make four observations.
    1. The larger $latex \lambda^\star &s=3$ is, the easier tightening $latex f_1(x)\leq0&s=3$ results in increasing $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$.
      Consider $latex \lambda^\star = 100 &s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_1(x) \leq -0.01 &\implies p^\star(-0.01,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 1 \\ f_1(x) \leq -0.1 &\implies p^\star(-0.1,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 10 \\ f_1(x) \leq -1 &\implies p^\star(-1,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 100 \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$








    2. The smaller $latex \lambda^\star &s=3$ is, the more flexibility we have to relax $latex f_1(x) \leq 0 &s=3$ without decreasing $latex p^\star(u,v)&s=3$ too much.
      Consider $latex \lambda^\star = 0.01 &s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_1(x) \leq 1 &\implies p^\star(1,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 0.01 \\ f_1(x) \leq 10 &\implies p^\star(10,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 0.1 \\ f_1(x) \leq 100 &\implies p^\star(100,0) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 1 \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$








    3. When $latex \nu^\star v <0 &s=3$: the larger $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ is, the easier changing $latex v&s=3$ increases $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$.
      Consider $latex \nu^\star = \pm 100 &s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} h_1(x) = \mp 0.01 &\implies p^\star(0,\mp 0.01) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 1 \\ h_1(x) = \mp 0.1 &\implies p^\star(0,\mp 0.1) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 10 \\ h_1(x) = \mp 1 &\implies p^\star(0,\mp 1) \geq p^\star(0,0) + 100 \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$








    4. When $latex \nu^\star v > 0 &s=3$: the smaller $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ is, the more flexibility we have to change $latex v &s=3$ without decreasing $latex p^\star(u,v)&s=3$ too much.
      Consider $latex \nu^\star = \pm 0.01&s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} h_1(x) = \pm 1 &\implies p^\star(0,\pm 1) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 0.01 \\ h_1(x) = \pm 10 &\implies p^\star(0,\pm 10) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 0.1 \\ h_1(x) = \pm 100 &\implies p^\star(0,\pm 100) \geq p^\star(0,0) - 10 \\ \end{aligned} &s=3$
















    Example This example reviews KKT conditions, Slater's condition, perturbation and sensitivity.
    Consider the LP

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}y + 1\\ \text{subject to}& \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq 0 \\ & x+y = R \end{cases}, &s=3$

    where $latex R>0 &s=3$ is fixed.





    The Lagrangian and its gradient
    Since the constraints are

    $latex \begin{cases} & \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq 0 \\ & x+y = R \end{cases}, &s=3$

    the Lagrangian takes the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,y,\lambda,\nu) &= \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}y + 1 \\ &+ \frac{\lambda}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{\lambda}{R^2}y^2 - \lambda \\ &+ \nu x + \nu y - \nu R. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Differentiating with respect to $latex (x,y) &s=3$ gives

    $latex \nabla L = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}x + \nu\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}y + \nu\\ \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$







    KKT Conditions
    Since (LP) has both inequality and equality constraints, its KKT conditions take the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x^\star) & \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots, m\\ h_i(x^\star) & = 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,p\\ \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ \nabla L(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &=0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, we wish to solve

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq 0\\ x+y-R=0\\ \lambda\left(\frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1\right) = 0\\ \lambda \geq 0\\ \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}x + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}y + \nu &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Observe:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda &=0\\ \nabla L &= 0 \end{aligned} \implies \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2} + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \nu &= 0 \end{aligned}, &s=3$

    which is impossible and so $latex \lambda > 0 &s=3$.

    Using complementary slackness and $latex \lambda > 0 &s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda\left(\frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1\right) = 0 \implies \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 =0. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Therefore, $latex x &s=3$ and $latex y &s=3$ must solve

    $latex \begin{aligned} x+y-R &=0\\ \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 &=0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Equivalently,

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{R^2}(R-y)^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 =0, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which has solutions $latex y=0,R &s=3$, and whence $latex x=R,0&s=3$, respectively.





    Using $latex \nabla L = 0&s=3$ and $latex (x,y)=(R,0) &s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}x + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}y + \nu &=0 \end{aligned} \implies \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R} + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \nu &=0, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which has no solution for $latex \lambda>0 &s=3$.
    Therefore, $latex (x,y)=(R,0)&s=3$ is not optimal.





    Using $latex \nabla L = 0&s=3$ and $latex (x,y)=(0,R) &s=3$ gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}x + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R^2}y + \nu &=0 \end{aligned} \implies \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2} + \nu &= 0\\ -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2\lambda}{R} + \nu &=0, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which has solution $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)=(\frac{R}{2},-\frac{1}{2})&s=3$.





    Conclusion.

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{primal optimal point} &= (x^\star,y^\star)=(0,R)\\ \text{dual optimal point} &= (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)=(\frac{R}{2},-\frac{1}{2})\\ \text{primal optimal value} &= p^\star\\ &=\frac{1}{2}\cdot 0 - \frac{1}{2}R + 1\\ & = -\frac{R}{2}+1 \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Sensitivity
    Recall the sensitivity inequality:

    $latex p^\star(0,0)- \lambda^{\star T}u - \nu^{\star T}v \leq p^\star(u,v) &s=3$

    This inequality applies to (LP) since it has strong duality and $latex d^\star &s=3$ is achieved.
    Thus

    $latex -\frac{R}{2} + 1 - \frac{R}{2}u + \frac{1}{2}v \leq p^\star(u,v) &s=3$

    where $latex p^\star(u,v) &s=3$ is the primal optimal for the perturbed problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}y + 1\\ \text{subject to}& \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq u \\ & x+y - R=v \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Remarks.
    • By our previous analysis: if $latex R &s=3$ is large, then the problem ought to be sensitive to making $latex u &s=3$ more and more negative.





    • In hindsight, this is obvious: the inequality

      $latex \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq u &s=3$

      is equivalent to

      $latex x^2 + y^2 \leq R^2 + uR^2 &s=3$

      Therefore, perturbing by making $latex u &s=3$ more and more negative considerably restricts the problem to a smaller and smaller disk.
      In fact, the problem is no longer feasible for any $latex v&s=3$ when $latex u < -1 &s=3$!






    • Explicitly: straightforward to compute

      $latex p^\star(u,0) =-\frac{R^2}{2}\sqrt{2u+1} +1 &s=3$.

      Compare

      $latex p^\star(0,0) =-\frac{R}{2}+1 \ll p^\star(u\sim -\frac{1}{2},0) \sim 1 &s=3$.







    Question: What if we had formulated the problem with the constraint

    $latex x^2 + y^2 - R^2 \leq 0 &s=3$

    instead of

    $latex \frac{1}{R^2}x^2 + \frac{1}{R^2}y^2 - 1 \leq 0 ? &s=3$

    Why is this problem no longer sensitive to small negative perturbations $latex u &s=3$?














    Geometry of Lagrangian Duality Goal: Provide a geometric description of Lagrange duality.

    Restriction: We consider only the one inequality constraint and no equality constraint setting; c.f. CO Section 5.3 for general dimensions.





    One Constraint Setting
    Consider the OP

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_1(x) \leq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    with $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and domain $latex D &s=3$.
    Construct the vector function

    $latex F(x) = \begin{bmatrix} f_1(x)\\ f_0(x) \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    Consider the sets

    $latex \begin{aligned} \mathcal{G} &= \{(u,t) : u=f_1(x),t=f_0(x)\text{ for some }x \in D \}\\ &= F(D)\\ \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}} &= \{(u,t) \in \mathcal{G}: u \preceq 0\}\\ &= \{ (u,t) : u=f_1(x),t=f_0(x) \text{ for some feasible }x\} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}}&s=3$ iff

    there exists $latex x \in D &s=3$ with $latex f_0(x)=t &s=3$ and $latex u=f_1(x)\leq0&s=3$.







    Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} t^\star &= \inf\{t : \exists u\leq0 \text{ with } (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \} \\ &=\inf\{t:(u,t) \in \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}}\}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Intuitively: $latex t^\star&s=3$ is the smallest $latex t &s=3$ such that $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}}&s=3$ for some $latex u \leq 0 &s=3$.
    Thus $latex t^\star&s=3$ is the smallest value $latex f_0(x)&s=3$ can take among feasible $latex x &s=3$.
    Can therefore conclude: $latex p^\star = t^\star &s=3$.
    Explicitly:

    $latex \begin{aligned} t^\star &= \inf\{t : \exists u\leq0 \text{ with } (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \}\\ &= \inf\{t : \exists x \in D \text{ with } f_1(x) \leq 0 , f_0(x)=t \}\\ &= \inf\{f_0(x) : x \text { feasible}\}\\ &=p^\star. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Examples
    1. Consider the problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}s \cos(s)\\ \text{subject to}& 3\log(s-1) - 3 \leq 0 \end{cases}. &s=3$

      Define

      $latex F(s) = \begin{bmatrix} 3\log(s-1) - 3\\ \frac{1}{2}s \cos(s) \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

      Then $latex F(s) &s=3$ describes a parametric curve in $latex \mathbb{R}^2 &s=3$.





      The image $latex \mathcal{G} &s=3$ of $latex F(s) &s=3$ is plotted below:
      Question: Which points (A, B, C or?) corresponds to the optimal value $latex p^\star&s=3$?

      Answer. N.B.: $latex \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}} &s=3$ corresponds to the portion of $latex \mathcal{G} &s=3$ with $latex u \leq 0 &s=3$.
      This portion of $latex \mathcal{G}&s=3$ is the dashed line in the graph below. Observed above: $latex p^\star &s=3$ corresponds to smallest value the $latex t &s=3$-coordinate "can take" for $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}} &s=3$.
      Consequently, B corresponds to the point $latex (u,p^\star) &s=3$.






    2. Consider a problem whose $latex \mathcal{G}&s=3$ is given by the curve and its enclosed region as depicted below: Question: Which points (A, B, C or?) corresponds to the optimal value $latex p^\star&s=3$?

      Answer. $latex p^\star = 3 &s=3$.
      This is the $latex t &s=3$-coordinate for the points A and B.
      N.B.: both A and B belong to $latex \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}} &s=3$.
      Remark: C is not considered because C is not in $latex \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}}&s=3$.






    The Lagrange dual function
    For each $latex \lambda \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$, define the function

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Gamma_\lambda&:\mathcal{G} \to \mathbb{R}\\ \Gamma_\lambda(u,t) &= \begin{bmatrix} \lambda & 1 \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}u \\ t \end{bmatrix} = \lambda u + t. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    But $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} &s=3$ iff $latex u=f_1(x) &s=3$ and $latex t=f_0(x) &s=3$ for some $latex x \in D &s=3$ and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Gamma_\lambda(u,t) = \lambda u + t = \lambda f_1(x) + f_0(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Question: Have we seen this before?
    Answer. $latex \lambda f_1(x) + f_0(x) &s=3$ is exactly the Lagrangian $latex L(x,\lambda) &s=3$!






    Since (this is an equality of sets of real numbers)

    $latex \begin{aligned} \{ \Gamma_\lambda(u,t) : (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \} &= \{ \lambda u + t : (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \}\\ &= \{ L(x,\lambda) : x \in D \} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda) &= \inf \{ L(x,\lambda) : x \in D \}\\ &= \inf \{ \Gamma_\lambda(u,t) : (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \} \\ &= \inf \{ \lambda u + t: (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} \} . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    In particular:

    $latex g(\lambda) \leq \lambda u + t &s=3$

    for all $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} &s=3$; i.e.,

    $latex \{ g(\lambda) = \lambda u + t : (u,t) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \} &s=3$

    is a supporting hyperplane of $latex \mathcal{G} &s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex g(\lambda) &s=3$ is the $latex t &s=3$-intercept of this line.
    (This is all only meaningful if $latex g(\lambda)&s=3$ is finite.)





    Weak duality revisited
    Observe:

    $latex \lambda \geq 0 , \quad u \leq 0 \implies \lambda u \leq 0 &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \lambda u + t \leq t. &s=3$

    Using $latex g(\lambda) \leq \lambda u + t &s=3$ for $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} &s=3$ gives

    $latex g(\lambda) \leq t. &s=3$

    Since this holds for all $latex t &s=3$ with $latex (u,t) \in \mathcal{G} &s=3$, we conclude

    $latex g(\lambda) \leq p^\star. &s=3$

    Since this holds for all $latex \lambda\geq0 &s=3$, we conclude weak duality:

    $latex d^\star \leq p^\star. &s=3$







    Example 1.
    Consider a problem whose $latex \mathcal{G}&s=3$ is given by the curve and its enclosed region as depicted below:





    The image below depicts
    • the optimal value $latex p^\star &s=3$;
    • the line $latex g(\frac{2}{3}) = \frac{2}{3}u + t &s=3$;
    • and the value $latex g(\frac{2}{3}) = 2&s=3$ given as the $latex t&s=3$-intercept of this line.






    The image below depicts the line $latex g(\frac{4}{3}) = \frac{4}{3}u+t &s=3$:





    Remark. Observe that no supporting hyperplane of $latex \mathcal{G}&s=3$ can intersect $latex (0,p^\star)&s=3$.
    Thus, there are no multipliers $latex \lambda^\star &s=3$ such that $latex g(\lambda^\star)= p^\star &s=3$.
    As a result, this problem is not stronlgy dual.
    This is further indicated in the image below.






    Example 2.
    Consider a problem whose $latex \mathcal{G}&s=3$ is given by the curve and its enclosed region as depicted below: Question: Does this problem satisfy strong duality.
    Answer. Yes!
    As the image below depicts, observe that there is a supporting hyperplane passing through $latex (0,p^\star)&s=3$.
    This is enough to conclude $latex d^\star = p^\star &s=3$ and hence strong duality.















    Sketch of Proof of Slater's Theorem Recall:

    Slater's Theorem. If a convex optimization problem satisfies Slater's condition, then it is strongly dual and the dual problem is solvable



    As in the previous section, we consider problems with one inequality constraint:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_1(x) \leq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex x \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and both $latex f_0,f_1 &s=3$ are convex.

    Define the epigraph

    $latex \begin{aligned} \mathcal{A} = \{ (u,t) : f_1(x) \leq u, f_0(x)\leq t\text{ for some }x \in D \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus, if

    $latex \xi = (f_1(x),f_0(x)) \in \mathcal{G}&s=3$,

    then every point "above and to the right" of $latex \xi &s=3$ is in $latex \mathcal{A} &s=3$.





    Example. In the images below, the sets $latex \mathcal{G} &s=3$ and $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$ are given.
    Important remarks:
    • The point $latex (0,p^\star)&s=3$ is generically on the boundary of $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$.
    • Strong duality is prevented exactly because $latex \mathcal{A} &s=3$ is not convex.






    Sketch of Proof of Slater's Theorem.
    Observe: $latex f_0,f_1 &s=3$ convex $latex \implies \mathcal{A} &s=3$ convex.
    Indeed, if $latex (u,t),(u',t') \in \mathcal{A}&s=3$ and $latex s \in [0,1]&s=3$, then
    • $latex f_1(x) \leq u, f_1(x') \leq u'&s=3$ gives

      $latex \begin{aligned}f_1(sx+(1-s)x') &\leq s f_1(x) + (1-s)f_1(x')\\ & \leq su+ (1-s)u' \end{aligned} &s=3$

    • $latex f_0(x)\leq t, f_0(x')\leq t' &s=3$ gives

      $latex \begin{aligned}f_0(sx+(1-s)x') &\leq s f_0(x) + (1-s)f_0(x')\\ & \leq st+ (1-s)t' \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore $latex t(u,t) + (1-t)(u',t') \in \mathcal{A} &s=3$.





    $latex \mathcal{A} &s=3$ convex $latex \implies &s=3$ for each boundary point $latex P \in bd\mathcal{A} &s=3$ of $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$ there is a supporting hyperplane $latex \ell_P &s=3$ of $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$ which intersects $latex P &s=3$.
    This is depicted below.





    Recall: $latex P^\star:=(0,p^\star) \in bd\mathcal{A}&s=3$
    Therefore, there is a supporting hyperplane $latex \ell_{P^\star}&s=3$ of $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$ which intersects $latex P^\star&s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex \ell_{P^\star}&s=3$ lies below $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$.
    This is depicted below.





    Assume Slater's condition: there exists $latex x' \in D &s=3$ with $latex f_1(x')<0 &s=3$.
    Let $latex u'<0 &s=3$ be such that $latex f_1(x') < u'<0&s=3$
    Let $latex t'>f_0(x')&s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex (u',t') &s=3$ is to the right of $latex (f_1(x'),f_0(x'))&s=3$.
    Then $latex (u',t') \in \text{int}(\mathcal{A})&s=3$ and lies above $latex \mathcal{G}_{\text{feas}}&s=3$.
    Slater's condition is what ensures such an interior point exists.
    This is depicted below.





    Since $latex \ell_{P^\star}&s=3$ is a supporting hyperplane below $latex \mathcal{A}&s=3$, it follows that $latex (u',t') &s=3$ has to lie above $latex \ell_{P^\star}&s=3$.
    This ensures $latex \ell_{P^\star}&s=3$ is nonvertical and so it has a finite slope $latex \lambda' &s=3$.
    This is depicted below.





    Evidently,

    $latex g(\lambda') = \inf\{\lambda' u + t: (u,t) \in \mathcal{A}\} = p^\star. &s=3$

    Therefore, strong duality holds.
    N.B.: If $latex g(\lambda') = c \neq p^\star &s=3$ were the case, then the line $latex \{ c=\lambda' u + t \} &s=3$ would fail to be a supporting hyperplane since it will pass through $latex (0,c) \neq (0,p^\star)&s=3$.
    In particular, either $latex c=\lambda' u +t&s=3$ may be decreased or is unachievable.
    The lines for $latex c=p^\star,a,b &s=3$ with $latex a<p^\star<b&s=3$ is depicted below.














    Theorems of Alternatives Recall the feasibility problem:

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} & x\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, i = 1, \ldots, m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i = 1,\ldots, p \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Goal: Use Lagrange duality to study the feasibility problem.





    Observe that the feasibility problem is equivalent to the minimization problem:

    $latex \text{(FP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & 0\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, i = 1, \ldots, m\\ & h_i(x) = 0, i = 1,\ldots, p \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Indeed, a solution to (FP) exists iff the constraints are consistent.

    The optimal value for (FP) is given by

    $latex p^\star = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{(FP) is feasible}\\ +\infty & \text{(FP) is infeasible} \end{cases}. &s=3$







    Duality of Feasibility Problem.
    Let

    $latex f(x) = \begin{bmatrix}f_1(x)\\\vdots\\f_m(x)\end{bmatrix},\quad h(x) = \begin{bmatrix} h_1(x) \\\vdots\\h_p(x) \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    Since the objective function of (FP) is $latex f_0(x)=0 &s=3$, the Lagrangian is

    $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu) = \lambda^T f(x) + \nu^T h(x) &s=3$

    with Lagrange multipliers $latex (\lambda,\nu) \in \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^p&s=3$.
    The Lagrange dual function is thus

    $latex g(\lambda,\nu) = \inf\{ \lambda^T f(x) + \nu^T h(x) : x \in D \}. &s=3$

    The dual of (FP) is therefore

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & g(\lambda,\nu)\\ \text{subject to} & \lambda \succeq 0. \end{cases}.&s=3$







    The dual feasibility problem is thus

    $latex \text{(DFP)} \begin{cases} \text{find} & (\lambda,\nu)\\ \text{subject to} & \lambda \succeq 0\\ & g(\lambda,\nu)>0. \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Observe for $latex t \geq 0 &s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(t\lambda,t\nu) &= \inf\{ \lambda^T f(x) + \nu^T h(x) : x \in D \}\\ &=t\inf\{ t\lambda^T f(x) + t\nu^T h(x) : x \in D \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Using this and that $latex g(0,0)=0&s=3$, we conclude

    $latex d^\star = \begin{cases} +\infty & \text{(DFP) is feasible}\\ 0 & \text{(DFP) is infeasible} \end{cases}.&s=3$

    Justification. Indeed: if $latex \exists (\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ with $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$ and $latex g(\lambda,\nu)>0 &s=3$, then

    $latex g(t\lambda,t\nu) = tg(\lambda,\nu)>0&s=3$

    can be made as large and desirable.
    On the other hand, if no such $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ exist, then the large $latex g(\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ may be is 0.






    Weak Alternatives.
    Recall weak duality asserts $latex d^\star \leq p^\star &s=3$.
    We also just derived

    $latex p^\star = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{(FP) is feasible}\\ +\infty & \text{(FP) is infeasible} \end{cases}, \quad d^\star = \begin{cases} +\infty & \text{(DFP) is feasible}\\ 0 & \text{(DFP) is infeasible} \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Therefore:
    • (FP) feasible $latex \implies&s=3$ $latex p^\star =0 &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex d^\star = 0 &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ (DFP) infeasible.
    • (DFP) feasible $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex d^\star = \infty &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex p^\star = \infty &s=3$ $latex \implies &s=3$ (FP) infeasible.
    In general:

    If at most one of two problems can be feasible at a time, then they are called weak alternatives.

    Therefore, (FP) and (DFP) are weak alternatives.





    Strong Alternatives.
    In general:

    If exactly one of two problems is feasible at a time, then they are called strong alternatives.

    Farkas' Lemma.
    Let $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}&s=3$ and $latex c \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.
    Then the feasibility problems

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} &x\\ \text{subject to} & Ax \preceq0\\ &c^Tx < 0 \end{cases} \quad \text{and}\quad \begin{cases} \text{find} & y\\ \text{subject to}&A^Ty + c =0\\ &y \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    are strong alternatives.
    Proof. Consider the LP

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & c^Tx\\ \text{subject to} & Ax \preceq0. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Its Lagrangian and Lagrange dual are

    $latex \text{(LP)} \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda) &= c^Tx+ \lambda^TAx = (c+A^T\lambda)^Tx\\ g(\lambda) &= \begin{cases} 0 & c+A^T\lambda =0\\ -\infty & \text{else} \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, the dual problem is

    $latex \text{(DLP)} \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & 0\\ \text{subject to} & A^Ty+c=0\\ &y\succeq 0 \end{cases} . &s=3$

    N.B.: (LP) and (DLP) are strongly dual and so their respective optimal values $latex p^\star,d^\star&s=3$ satisfy $latex p^\star=d^\star&s=3$.





    Observe:
    • $latex Ax \preceq 0 , c^Tx<0 &s=3$ infeasible $latex \iff &s=3$ $latex p^\star = 0&s=3$.
    • $latex Ax \preceq 0 , c^Tx<0 &s=3$ feasible $latex \iff &s=3$ $latex p^\star = -\infty&s=3$.
    • $latex A^Ty+c=0, y\succeq0 &s=3$ infeasible $latex \iff&s=3$ $latex d^\star =-\infty&s=3$.
    • $latex A^Ty+c=0, y\succeq0 &s=3$ feasible $latex \iff&s=3$ $latex d^\star =0&s=3$.
    Using strong duality $latex p^\star=d^\star&s=3$, we conclude that the feasibility problems

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{find} &x\\ \text{subject to} & Ax \preceq0\\ &c^Tx < 0 \end{cases} \quad \text{and}\quad \begin{cases} \text{find} & y\\ \text{subject to}&A^Ty + c =0\\ &y \succeq 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    are strong alternatives.















    Descent Algorithms for Unconstrained Minimization
    Overview Unconstrained Minimization.
    We will focus on unconstrained problems of the form

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x). \end{cases} &s=3$

    The main assumptions on $latex f &s=3$ will be
    • $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex (defined below).
    • $latex f &s=3$ is twice continuously differentiable: $latex \nabla^2 f &s=3$ is continuous.
    • $latex f &s=3$ has a closed sublevel set.
    Constrained minimization problems will come later.

    Goal.
    Formulate and study algorithms which search for the minimizing $latex x^\star &s=3$ that solve the problem: $latex p^\star = f(x^\star) &s=3$.







    Idea: Using Descent Methods.
    Find iterative rules

    $latex G_k: \text{dom}\,f \to \text{dom}\,f &s=3$

    so that the sequence

    $latex x^{(k+1)} = G_k(x^{(k)}), \quad k =1, 2, \ldots, &s=3$

    stabilizes and satisfies descent:

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^{(k)} &\to x' \text{ for some }x' \text{ as }k \to \infty\\ f(x^{(k+1)}) &< f(x^{(k)}) \text{ whenever } x^{(k)} \text{ is not optimal}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: such rules are natural for searching for minimizers.
    Without convexity, such rules may get "stuck" at local minimizers.
    Example.
    Consider the iterative rule

    $latex G_k(x^{(k)}) = x^{(k)} + h_k \nu^{(k)} &s=3$

    where $latex h_k \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ are step sizes and $latex \nu^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ are search directions vector.
    Generally, $latex h_k,\nu^{(k)}&s=3$ may depend on $latex x^{(k)} &s=3$.
    Thus $latex G_k(x) &s=3$ determines how far to step and in what direction from $latex x&s=3$.







    Remarks.
    1. If $latex x^{(k)} \to x^\star &s=3$, then continuity would give

      $latex f(x^{(k)}) \to f(x^\star) = p^\star. &s=3$

    2. In general, $latex p^\star &s=3$ need not to be known a priori.
    3. In practice, one specifies tolerance $latex \epsilon>0&s=3$ and terminates search when an iterate $latex x^{(K)} &s=3$ satisfies this tolerance:

      $latex f(x^{(K)})-p^\star \leq \epsilon. &s=3$

    4. To start the search: a suitable starting point $latex x^{(0)}&s=3$ needs to be chosen.
    5. To stop the search: a suitable stopping criterion that ensures tolerance is met needs to be determined.
    6. Generally $latex G_k &s=3$ depends on the step; if $latex G_k = G &s=3$ is independent of the step, then $latex G &s=3$ is called stationary.








    General Descent Algorithm.
    Given iterative rule $latex G_k &s=3$ satisfying descent, a desired tolerance $latex \epsilon > 0 &s=3$, and a stopping criterion

    $latex \sigma(x^{(k)}) = \begin{cases} \text{true} & \text{if } f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq \epsilon\\ \text{false} &\text{ else} \end{cases} &s=3$

    a general descent algorithm takes the form: 
    
given initial $latex x^{(0)} \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$.
repeat: compute $latex x^{(k+1)} = G_k(x^{(k)})&s=2$.
until: $latex \sigma(x^{(k)}) = \text{true}&s=2$.
    An natural kind of stopping criterion may be

    $latex \sigma(x^{(k)}) = \begin{cases} \text{true} & \text{if } \Vert \nabla f(x^{(k)})\Vert_2 \text{ is sufficiently small}\\ \text{false} &\text{ else} \end{cases}. &s=3$

    Indeed, for differentiable convex functions, if $latex \nabla f(x) = 0&s=3$, then $latex x=x^\star &s=3$.














    Mathematical Framework Main Assumptions
    For theoretical convenience, we always assume
    1. $latex f &s=3$ satisfies strong convexity (defined below)
    2. $latex f &s=3$ is twice continuously differentiable.
    3. for the chosen initial point $latex x^{(0)}&s=3$, the sublevel set

      $latex S := \{ x \in \text{dom}\, f : f(x) \leq f(x^{(0)}) \} &s=3$

      is closed.
    N.B.:
    1. Since $latex f(x^\star) = p^\star \leq f(x^{(0)})&s=3$, we have $latex x^\star \in S&s=3$.
    2. $latex S&s=3$ being closed holds in case $latex \text{dom}f\, = \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ and $latex f &s=3$ is continuous.
      However, $latex S&s=3$ being closed may fail for nontrivial and non-pathological cases; e.g., consider the case $latex \text{dom}\,f &s=3$ is an open ball and $latex x_0 = \text{argmax}f&s=3$.
      Then $latex S &s=3$ is an open ball in $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ and therefore not closed.








    Strong convexity.
    We say $latex f &s=3$ satisfies strong convexity on $latex S&s=3$ if there exists a $latex m >0 &s=3$ such that

    $latex m Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) , \quad \forall x \in S. &s=3$

    N.B.:
    1. $latex Id \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &s=3$ indicates the identity matrix.
    2. Fix $latex x \in S&s=3$ and let $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be an eigenvector of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$ with eigenvalue $latex \lambda &s=3$.
      Then

      $latex \nabla^2 f(x) - m Id \succeq0&s=3$

      implies

      $latex 0 \leq v^T (\nabla^2 f(x) - m Id) v = (\lambda-m)\Vert v\Vert_2^2. &s=3$

      Thus

      $latex 0< m \leq \lambda &s=3$ and $latex \nabla^2f(x) \succ 0&s=3$.

    3. In particular, strong convexity is a stronger form of strict convexity!








    Proposition. If $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex and $latex S&s=3$ is closed, then there exists $latex M >0 &s=3$ such that

    $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq M Id , \quad \forall x \in S. &s=3$

    Proof.
    1. The plan is to show that the sublevel set $latex S&s=3$ is closed and bounded (hence compact) and use continuity of $latex \nabla^2 f(x)&s=3$ to conclude each of its matrix entries are bounded.
      This is enough to conclude the inequality.







    2. $latex f&s=3$ twice continuously differentiable implies:
      for each $latex x,y \in S &s=3$ there exists $latex z &s=3$ on the line $latex x\to y&s=3$ such that

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{1}{2}(y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(y-x). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    3. Strong convexity $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \succeq m Id &s=3$ implies

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}(y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(y-x) \geq \frac{m}{2}\Vert y-x \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Taking $latex y \in S &s=3$ and $latex x = x^\star &s=3$ in previous step, there holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^{(0)})& \geq f(y)\\ & \geq f(x^\star) + \nabla f(x^\star)^T(y-x^\star) + \frac{m}{2}\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2\\ &= p^\star + \frac{m}{2}\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    4. Previous step gives

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{2}{m}\left( f(x^{(0)})-p^\star \right) \geq \Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      which implies all $latex y \in S &s=3$ belong to a ball of sufficiently large radius with center $latex x^\star &s=3$, and therefore $latex S &s=3$ is bounded.







    5. Since $latex S &s=3$ is bounded and closed, it is compact.
      Therefore $latex \nabla^2 f&s=3$ is continuous on a compact set.
      Therefore each entry of $latex \nabla^2 f&s=3$ is bounded and hence $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq M Id&s=3$ for sufficiently large $latex M&s=3$.










    Remark.
    Just as $latex mId \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$ gave a lower bound on the eigenvalues of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$, the bound $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq MId&s=3$ provides an upper bound on the eigenvalues.
    The proof is mutatis mutandis the same.







    Proposition. For $latex x \in S &s=3$ there holds

    $latex \frac{1}{2m}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \geq f(x) - p^\star \geq \frac{1}{2M}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. &s=3$

    Proof.
    1. Observe: if a matrix $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} &s=3$ satisfies

      $latex m Id \preceq A \preceq M Id, &s=3$

      then for all $latex v \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ there holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} m\Vert v \Vert_2^2 = v^T(m Id)v \leq v^TAv \leq v^TM Idv = M\Vert v \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$



      Therefore, using

      $latex m Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq M Id, &s=3$

      we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{m}{2} \Vert y-x \Vert_2^2 \leq \frac{1}{2}(y-x) \nabla^2 f(z) (y-x) \leq \frac{M}{2}\Vert y-x \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    2. As above: for each $latex x,y \in S &s=3$ there exists $latex z &s=3$ on the line $latex x\to y&s=3$ such that

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{1}{2}(y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(y-x). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    3. Observe that:

      $latex q(y):= f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T (y-x) + \frac{c}{2}\Vert{y-x}\Vert_2^2 &s=3$

      is a convex quadratic for $latex c>0 &s=3$.
      Moreover,

      $latex \nabla q(y_0) = \nabla f(x) + c(y_0-x) =0 &s=3$

      iff

      $latex y_0 = x - \frac{1}{c} \nabla f(x). &s=3$

      Therefore

      $latex \begin{aligned} \text{min}\, q(y) &= q(y_0)\\ &=q(x - \frac{1}{c} \nabla f(x))\\ &= f(x) - \frac{1}{2c}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$









    4. Using $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq M Id&s=3$, we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) \leq f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{M}{2}\Vert y-x \Vert_2^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and minimizing over $latex y &s=3$ gives

      $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star \leq f(x) - \frac{1}{2M}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      This proves

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2M}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \leq f(x) - p^\star . \end{aligned} &s=3$









    5. Using $latex m Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$ we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) \geq f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{m}{2}\Vert y-x \Vert_2^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and minimizing over $latex y &s=3$ gives

      $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star \geq f(x) - \frac{1}{2m}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      This proves

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2m}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \geq f(x) - p^\star . \end{aligned} &s=3$











    Remark.
    The upper bound provides a stopping criterion: if $latex x^{(K)} &s=3$ satisfies

    $latex \Vert \nabla f(x^{(K)}) \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{2m\epsilon}, &s=3$

    then

    $latex f(x^{(K)}) - p^\star \leq \epsilon. &s=3$

    Viz., $latex x^{(K)}&s=3$ satisfies $latex \epsilon&s=3$-tolerance.
    Yet, $latex p^\star &s=3$ does not even need to be known for this.







    Proposition. For $latex x \in S&s=3$, there holds

    $latex \Vert x^\star - x \Vert_2 \leq \frac{2}{m} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2. &s=3$

    Proof. Again, we use

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{1}{2}(y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(y-x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    by taking $latex y = x^\star &s=3$, which gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} p^\star &= f(x^\star) \\ &= f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(x^\star-x) + \frac{1}{2}(x^\star-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(x^\star-x)\\ &\geq f(x) - \Vert \nabla f(x)^T\Vert_2 \Vert x^\star - x\Vert_2 + \frac{m}{2} \Vert x^\star - x \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Here we used Cauchy-Schwarz to conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert \nabla f(x)^T\Vert_2 \Vert x^\star -x \Vert_2 \geq \nabla f(x)^T(x-x^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} -\Vert \nabla f(x)^T\Vert_2 \Vert x^\star -x \Vert_2 \leq \nabla f(x)^T(x^\star - x) \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Since $latex p^\star - f(x) \leq 0 &s=3$, we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} 0 \geq - \Vert \nabla f(x)^T\Vert_2 \Vert x^\star - x\Vert_2 + \frac{m}{2} \Vert x^\star - x \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and whence

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{2}{m}\Vert \nabla f(x)^T \Vert_2 \geq \Vert x^\star - x \Vert_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$



    Corollary. If $latex x^\star,x^{\star\star}&s=3$ are two minimizers, then $latex x^\star = x^{\star\star} &s=3$ since

    $latex \Vert x^\star - x^{\star\star} \Vert_2 \leq \frac{2}{m} \Vert \nabla f(x^{\star\star}) \Vert_2 = 0. &s=3$
















    General Descent Methods Plan: Further specialize and describe general descent methods.







    Minimizing sequence: a sequence $latex \{ x^{(k)}\} \subset \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ such that

    $latex x^{(k)} \to x^\star &s=3$ as $latex k \to \infty &s=3$.

    Goal: construct a minimizing sequence $latex \{ x^{(k)}\} &s=3$ satisfying
    • each iterate $latex x^{(k+1)}&s=3$ is defined via

      $latex x^{(k+1)} = x^{(k)} + t^{(k)}\Delta x^{(k)} &s=3$

      where

      $latex \begin{aligned} t^{(k)}\geq0 & \text{ is called the step size}\\ \Delta x^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^n & \text{ is called the search direction}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    • the $latex t^{(k)} &s=3$ and $latex \Delta x^{(k)} &s=3$ are chosen so that the sequence satisfies descent:

      $latex f(x^{(k+1)}) < f(x^{(k)}) &s=3$ whenever $latex x^{(k)} \neq x^\star &s=3$.









    Remarks.
    1. $latex \Delta x^{(k)} &s=3$ is generally not assumed to be a unit vector;
    2. A minimizing sequence satisfying descent satisfies

      $latex x^{(k)} \in S := \{ x : f(x) \leq f(x^{(0)} ) \} &s=3$.

    3. Customary to write $latex x:= x+ t \Delta x &s=3$ or $latex x^+ = x + t\Delta x &s=3$ as short hand for $latex x^{(k+1)} = x^{(k)} + t^{(k)} \Delta x^{(k)} &s=3$.








    Necessary condition for descent: since $latex f &s=3$ is convex and differentiable, there holds

    $latex f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) \leq f(y), &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) \geq 0 \implies f(x) \leq f(y). &s=3$



    Therefore, for $latex x:= x+t\Delta x&s=3$ to satisfy descent, it is necessary that

    $latex \nabla f(x^{(k)})^T \Delta x^{(k)} <0. &s=3$

    This follows by multiplying $latex \nabla f(x^{(k)})^T &s=3$ to both sides of

    $latex \Delta x^{(k)} = \frac{1}{t^{(k)}} (x^{(k+1)} - x^{(k)} ). &s=3$

    Descent direction: any search direction $latex \Delta x &s=3$ satisfying

    $latex -\nabla f(x)^T \Delta x > 0. &s=3$

    Viz., $latex \Delta x &s=3$ and $latex - \nabla f(x) &s=3$ form an acute angle.








    General descent stopping criterion.
    Recall: strong convexity $latex m Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$ ensures

    $latex \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{2m\epsilon} \implies f(x)-p^\star \leq \epsilon . &s=3$

    Since it is generally impossible to know the strong convexity constant $latex m &s=3$, one settles with choosing $latex \epsilon'>0 &s=3$ sufficiently small so that

    $latex \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 \leq \epsilon' \implies f(x)-p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$

    is likely to hold.
    Stopping criteria for the descent methods studied here are often of this form.








    General descent algorithm: Using the iterative rule $latex x := x + t \Delta x&s=3$, a general descent algorithm takes the following form. 
    
given initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
repeat:
1. Determine descent direction $latex \Delta x &s=2$.
2. Choose step size $latex t>0&s=3$.
3. Take step: $latex x:= x + t\Delta x &s=2$.
until: stopping criterion holds.















    Line Searching Observe

    $latex \{ x + t \Delta x : t \geq 0 \} &s=3$

    is a ray emanating from $latex x &s=3$ in the direction $latex \Delta x&s=3$.
    Thus Step 2. in the general descent algorithm is to determine where to step onto this line from $latex x &s=3$.
    Step 2. is therefore called a line search.







    Exact line search.
    Let $latex t_{\text{exact}} &s=3$ minimize $latex f &s=3$ along the line $latex \{ x + t \Delta x: t \geq 0 \} &s=3$.
    (Such $latex t_{\text{exact}} &s=3$ exists since $latex f &s=3$ convex.)
    Certainly $latex f(x+t_{\text{exact}}\Delta x) \leq f(x) &s=3$.
    This search for $latex t_{\text{exact}} &s=3$ is called an exact line search.
    A general descent algorithm with exact line search is recorded below. 
    
given initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
repeat:
1. Determine descent direction $latex \Delta x &s=2$.
2. Compute $latex t_{\text{exact}} = \text{argmin}\{f(x+t\Delta x): t \geq 0 \} &s=2$.
3. Take step: $latex x:= x + t_{\text{exact}}\Delta x &s=2$.
until: stopping criterion holds.








    Remarks.
    1. Let $latex t^{(k)}_{\text{exact}} &s=3$ be sequence of exact step sizes and let

      $latex x^{(k+1)} = x^{(k)} + t_{\text{exact}}^{(k)}\Delta x^{(k)} &s=3$

      be resulting sequence of steps.
      Since a search direction is used, each $latex t_{\text{exact}}^{(k)}>0 &s=3$ and so the sequence $latex x^{(k)}&s=3$ satisfies descent:

      $latex f(x^{(k+1)}) < f(x^{(k)})&s=3$.

    2. Using exact search is only reasonable when its computational cost is considerably less than the computational cost of finding search directions $latex \Delta x &s=3$.
      Otherwise, resources can be better spent finding better search directions.








    Examples
    Example 1.
    Consider the objective

    $latex f(x,y) = \frac{1}{2}(x-y)^2 + y &s=3$.

    The following image depicts a portion of the graph of $latex f&s=3$.







    The next figure depicts the restrictions of $latex f&s=3$ to the lines

    $latex \begin{aligned} &\{ (0,0.5) + t (1,0): t \geq 0 \}\\ &\{ (0,1) + t (1,0): t \geq 0 \}\\ &\{ (0,1.5) + t (1,0): t \geq 0 \} \end{aligned} &s=3$









    The last image depicts only these restrictions with the corresponding exact $latex x + t_{\text{exact}} \Delta x&s=3$ obtained from exact line search on each respective line.







    Example 2.
    Consider the same objective

    $latex f(x,y) = \frac{1}{2}(x-y)^2 + y &s=3$.

    The first image below depicts the first step of a general descent method using exact line search where

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^{(0)} &= (0,1.5)\\ \Delta x^{(0)} &= (1,0)\\ t_{\text{exact}}^{(0)} &= 1.5\\ x^{(1)} &= x^{(0)} + t_{\text{exact}}^{(0)} \Delta x^{(0)} = (1.5,1.5). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    The next image below depicts the second step using exact line search where

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^{(1)} &= (1.5,1.5)\\ \Delta x^{(1)} &= (0,-1)\\ t_{\text{exact}}^{(1)} &= 1\\ x^{(2)} &= x^{(1)} + t_{\text{exact}}^{(1)} \Delta x^{(1)} = (1.5,.5). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    The last image below depicts the third step using exact line search where

    $latex \begin{aligned} x^{(2)} &= (1.5,.5)\\ \Delta x^{(2)} &= (-1,0)\\ t_{\text{exact}}^{(2)} &= 1\\ x^{(3)} &= x^{(2)} + t_{\text{exact}}^{(2)} \Delta x^{(2)} = (.5,.5). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Backtracking line search.
    Naturally: exact line search may be too computationally expensive.

    Therefore, we may settle with line search which either
    • decreases the objective $latex f &s=3$ enough or
    • approximately minimizes $latex f &s=3$ in the direction $latex \Delta x&s=3$.


    Idea: given descent direction $latex \Delta x&s=3$ and parameter $latex \beta \in (0,1)&s=3$,
    1. take step $latex x \mapsto x + t \Delta x &s=3$
    2. "backtrack": test smaller steps $latex x \mapsto x + \beta^k t\Delta x &s=3$ until the decrease

      $latex f(x+\beta^k t\Delta x) - f(x) &s=3$

      behaves suitably at each iteration for convergence to hold.
    N.B.: Even if the initial step $latex x \mapsto x+t\Delta x&s=3$ results in an increase in objective, convexity ensures $latex x \mapsto x+\beta^k t\Delta x&s=3$ results in decrease for some $latex k&s=3$.







    Motivation of backtracking line search:
    Throughout, fix
    • $latex f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}&s=3$
    • descent direction $latex \Delta x&s=3$, i.e., $latex \Delta x \in \mathbb{R}&s=3$ and $latex f'(x)\Delta x < 0 &s=3$
    • Parameters $latex \alpha,\beta \in (0,1)&s=3$.








    Observations:
    1. For $latex t &s=3$ small, Taylor's approximation gives

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \approx tf'(x) \Delta x. &s=3$

      In particular, small steps guarantee an approximate decrease by the amount $latex tf'(x) \Delta x&s=3$.








    2. At worst, a general step size $latex t &s=3$ makes

      $latex x\mapsto x+t\Delta x&s=3$

      either overshoot the minimizer

      $latex x + t_{\text{exact}} \Delta x&s=3$

      or is too small for

      $latex x + t\Delta x&s=3$

      to be "near"

      $latex x+t_{\text{exact}}\Delta x &s=3$.









    3. Consider the linear extrapolation

      $latex y(t) = tf'(x) \Delta x + f(x) &s=3$

      as a function of $latex t&s=3$.
      Since $latex y(0) = f(x) &s=3$, the difference $latex y(t) - f(x) &s=3$ is a linear approximation of the difference $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) &s=3$.
      The line searching stopping criterion: 
      
until: $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq  \alpha tf'(x) \Delta x. &s=3$
      amounts to searching for $latex t&s=3$ until $latex f(x) &s=3$ decreases by a fraction of what the linear approximation gives.








    4. By 1. and $latex f'(x)\Delta x < 0 &s=3$, the inequality in 3. is guaranteed for small $latex t &s=3$.
      Indeed

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \approx tf'(x) \Delta x \leq \alpha tf'(x) \Delta x. &s=3$









    5. By 4., we observe that, if

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha tf'(x) \Delta x. &s=3$

      is not satisfied after first step $latex x\mapsto x + t \Delta &s=3$, then

      $latex f(x+\beta^k t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha \beta^k tf'(x) \Delta x &s=3$

      will be satisfied for large enough $latex k &s=3$.
      Can therefore consider the largest step size $latex \beta^k t&s=3$ which guarantees a decrease comparable to the decrease predicted by linear extrapolation.








    Improved Idea:
    1. take step $latex x \mapsto x + t \Delta x &s=3$
    2. "backtrack": find the first $latex k\geq0 &s=3$ such that

      $latex f(x+\beta^k t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha \beta^k tf'(x) \Delta x &s=3$

      is satisfied.
    3. Then (as we will see) we have "suitable decrease" and we proceed with choosing next descent direction.








    The aforementioned observations and ideas motivate a line search called backtracking line search.
    In higher dimension, the differential inequality

    $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha tf'(x) \Delta x &s=3$

    takes the form

    $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha t\nabla f(x)^T \Delta x. &s=3$

    This inequality is called the Armijo-Goldstein inequality or Armijo condition.
    The algorithm for backtracking line search may now be recorded: 
    
given 
$latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
descent direction $latex \Delta x&s=2$ at $latex x&s=2$
parameters $latex \alpha \in (0,0.5), \beta \in (0,1)&s=2$
$latex t = 1&s=2$
while: $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) >  \alpha  t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x &s=2$
update: $latex t:=\beta t&s=2$








    Remarks.
    1. The loop 
      
while: $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) >  \alpha  t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x &s=2$
update: $latex t:=\beta t&s=2$
      creates a sequence of step sizes

      $latex 1, \beta, \beta^2 , \beta^3 , \cdots, \beta^k , \cdots &s=3$

      and the sequence terminates once the exit criterion (Armijo condition) is satisfied.
      N.B.: it is possible for the search to terminate at $latex t=1 &s=3$.







    2. The while condition 
      
while: $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) >  \alpha  t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x &s=2$
      is understood as waiting until the objective is suitably decreased.
      Moreover, if Armijo-Goldstein inequality holds for some $latex t_0 &s=3$, it also holds for all $latex 0 < t \leq t_0 &s=3$.
      Indeed, by

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lim_{t\to0^+} \frac{f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x)}{t} = \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x \leq \alpha \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      there is a small enough $latex t_0>0&s=3$ such that

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x)}{t} \leq \alpha \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x, \quad \text{ for } 0 < t \leq t_0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      This is the Armijo-Goldstein inequality rearranged.







    3. The assumption $latex \alpha \in (0,0.5)&s=3$ and Armijo's condition are sufficient for convergence of gradient descent coupled with backtracking line search, which is detailed below.
      The smaller $latex \beta &s=3$ is, the faster $latex \beta^k &s=3$ decreases and hence the quicker the Armijo-Goldstein inequality holds.
      However, this also results in smaller steps $latex x \mapsto x + \beta^k t\Delta x&s=3$.







    4. One needs to ensure $latex f(x+t\Delta x)&s=3$ is well-defined to start the algorithm, i.e., that $latex x + t \Delta x \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$.
      This can be done by taking $latex t &s=3$ to be the first $latex \beta^k t &s=3$ with $latex x + \beta^k t\Delta x \in \text{dom}\, f&s=3$.







    5. This algorithm always terminates for differentiable and convex $latex f&s=3$.
      The argument follows the one-dimensional argument.
      Indeed Taylor's approximation ensures: for small $latex t &s=3$ there holds

      $latex f(x + t \Delta x) - f(x) \approx t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x < \alpha t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x. &s=3$

      Thus, once $latex k &s=3$ is large enough for $latex t=\beta^k &s=3$ be in the "small $latex t&s=3$" range, the Armijo-Goldstein inequality holds.







    Gradient Descent Recall: $latex \Delta x &s=3$ is called a descent direction provided

    $latex \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x < 0 &s=3$.

    Therefore

    $latex \Delta x = -\nabla f(x) &s=3$.

    provides a natural descent direction associated to the problem.
    This results in the following gradient descent method. 
    
given initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
repeat:
1. Set $latex \Delta x = - \nabla f(x) &s=2$.
2. Perform line search to determine step size $latex t &s=2$.
3. Take step: $latex x:= x + t\Delta x &s=2$.
until: stopping criterion holds.
    We focus on the cases the line search is exact or backtracking.
    Recall: by strong convexity and initial sublevel set $latex S&s=3$ being closed, there exist absolute constants $latex 0 < m \leq M <\infty&s=3$ such that

    $latex m Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq M Id \quad \text{ for } x \in S&s=3$.









    Convergence for exact line search.
    We show
    • gradient descent with exact line search converges.
    • number of iterations needed to achieve tolerance $latex f(x)-p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$ is bounded in terms of the problem data:
      • optimal value $latex p^\star&s=3$ and initial value $latex f(x^{(0)})&s=3$,
      • desired tolerance $latex \epsilon>0&s=3$,
      • and the conditioning of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$.








    Theorem. Suppose $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex with convexity constants $latex m,M&s=3$ and its initial sublevel set $latex S &s=3$ is closed. Then the gradient descent method with exact line search converges. Moreover, if the desired tolerance is $latex \epsilon>0 &s=3$, then

    $latex f(x^{(k)})- p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$

    holds after at most

    $latex -\frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}(f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) \right)}{\log(1 - m/M)} &s=3$

    many iterations.
    Proof. Step 0.
    The main idea is to establish constants $latex c \in (0,1) &s=3$ and $latex A>0 &s=3$ such that

    $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq c^k A &s=3$

    for all $latex k \geq 0 &s=3$.
    Indeed, if this holds,

    $latex \lim_{k} c^k = 0 \implies \lim_k f(x^{(k)}) =p^\star &s=3$.



    For notational simplicity: we forgo indexing by iteration step.
    For given iterate $latex x &s=3$, write $latex t_{\text{exact}}&s=3$ for resulting exact line search step size.
    Write $latex x^+ = x - t_{\text{exact}}\nabla f(x) &s=3$ for the next iterate after $latex x &s=3$ using gradient descent with exact line search.








    Step 1.
    Recall: under strong convexity assumptions on $latex f &s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2 f(x) &\preceq M Id\\ f(y) &\leq f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{M}{2}\Vert x-y\Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Letting $latex y = x - t \nabla f(x) &s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x - t\nabla f(x)) &\leq f(x) - t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 + \frac{Mt^2}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    This holds for all $latex t\geq0 &s=3$ with $latex x - t \nabla f(x) \in S &s=3$.







    Step 2.
    Using exact line search:

    $latex \begin{aligned} t_{\text{exact}} &:= \text{argmin}\{ f(x- t\nabla f(x)): t \geq 0 \}\\ f(x^+)&=f(x-t_{\text{exact}}\nabla f(x)) \leq f(x - t\nabla f(x)) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for all $latex t \geq 0 &s=3$.







    Step 3.
    The convex quadratic

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) - t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 + \frac{Mt^2}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    is minimized at

    $latex t = \frac{1}{M}&s=3$

    and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) - \frac{1}{2M}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \leq f(x) - t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 + \frac{Mt^2}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Step 4.
    Minimizing both sides of the Step 1. inequality

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x - t\nabla f(x)) &\leq f(x) - t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 + \frac{Mt^2}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and using Steps 2. and 3. gives

    $latex f(x^+) = f(x - t_{\text{exact}}\nabla f(x)) \leq f(x) - \frac{1}{2M} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$.

    Therefore

    $latex f(x^+) - p^\star \leq f(x) - p^\star - \frac{1}{2M} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$.









    Step 5.
    Recall we derived

    $latex f(x) - p^\star \leq \frac{1}{2m}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 . &s=3$

    (This was when we derived a natural stopping criterion.)
    Using this and Step 4. gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^+) - p^\star & \leq f(x) - p^\star - \frac{1}{2M} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2\\ &\leq f(x) - p^\star - \frac{1}{2M}2m(f(x)-p^\star)\\ &=c (f(x)-p^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    where

    $latex \begin{aligned} c = 1- \frac{m}{M}<1. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Step 6.
    Let

    $latex x^{++} = x^+ - t'_{\text{exact}}\nabla f(x^+)&s=3$
