    denote the iterate following $latex x^+&s=3$ using gradient descent and exact line search to find $latex t'_{\text{exact}}&s=3$.
    Applying the analysis with $latex (x^{++},x^{+})&s=3$ in place of $latex (x^{+},x)&s=3$, we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^{++}) - p^\star \leq &c (f(x^{+})-p^\star) \\ &\leq c (c (f(x) - p^\star))\\ &= c^2 (f(x) - p^\star). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Letting $latex x^{(k)} &s=3$ denote the $latex k&s=3$th iterate, iterating this argument gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq c^k (f(x^{(0)}) -p^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$.









    Step 7.
    Since $latex c = 1 - \frac{m}{M} < 1 &s=3$, conclude $latex c^k \to 0 &s=3$ as $latex k \to \infty &s=3$.
    Therefore

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lim_{k \to \infty } f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq \lim_{k \to \infty } c^k (f(x^{(0)}) -p^\star) = 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    It follows that $latex f(x^{(k)}) \to p^\star &s=3$ and so there holds convergence.








    Step 8.
    To find $latex k &s=3$ such that

    $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$,

    we solve

    $latex \begin{aligned} c^k (f(x^{(0)}) -p^\star) \leq \epsilon \end{aligned} &s=3$

    in terms of $latex k &s=3$:

    $latex \frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}(f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) \right)}{\log(1/c)} \leq k. &s=3$

    Therefore, as soon as $latex k &s=3$ surpasses this number, the tolerance is satisfied.
    Recall $latex c = 1 - \frac{m}{M}&s=3$.


    Remarks.
    1. Recall: above we showed that

      $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2M} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &\leq f(x) - p^\star \leq \frac{1}{2m}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2\\ \Vert x^\star - x \Vert_2 &\leq \frac{2}{m} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      It follows that $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \to 0 &s=3$ implies $latex x^{(k)} \to x^\star &s=3$.
    2. We can use these a priori estimates to observe

      $latex -\frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}(f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) \right)}{\log(1 - m/M)} \leq -\frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}m^{-1}2^{-1}\Vert \nabla f(x^{(0)}) \Vert_2^2 \right) }{\log(1 - m/M)} &s=3$,

      3. While the RHS is independent of knowing $latex p^\star &s=3$, it is conceivably a worse upper bound on the maximum number of steps required to achieve desired tolerance.








    Convergence for backtracking line search.

    We show
    • gradient descent with backtracking line search converges.
    • number of iterations needed to achieve tolerance $latex f(x)-p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$ is bounded in terms of the problem data:
      • optimal value $latex p^\star&s=3$ and initial value $latex f(x^{(0)})&s=3$,
      • desired tolerance $latex \epsilon>0&s=3$,
      • and the conditioning of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$.








    Theorem. Suppose $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex with convexity constants $latex m,M &s=3$ and its initial sublevel set $latex S &s=3$ is closed. Then the gradient descent method with backtracking line search converges. Moreover, the Armijo-Goldstein inequality holds for $latex 0 < t < \frac{1}{M} &s=3$. Lastly, if the desired tolerance is $latex \epsilon>0 &s=3$, then

    $latex f(x^{(k)})- p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$

    holds after at most

    $latex -\frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}(f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) \right)}{\log(1 - \text{min}\{ 2m\alpha,2\alpha\beta m/M\})} &s=3$

    many iterations.
    Proof. Step 0.
    The main idea is to establish constants $latex c \in (0,1) &s=3$ and $latex A>0 &s=3$ such that

    $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq c^k A &s=3$

    for all $latex k \geq 0 &s=3$.
    Indeed, if this holds,

    $latex \lim_{k} c^k = 0 \implies \lim_k f(x^{(k)}) =p^\star &s=3$.









    Step 1.
    Let $latex 0<t<\frac{1}{M}&s=3$ be fixed but arbitrary and let $latex x^+ = x + t \Delta x&s=3$.
    In the next step, we will show the Armijo-Goldstein inequality holds whenever $latex 0 < t < \frac{1}{M}&s=3$.
    Beforehand: since gradient descent chooses the descent direction

    $latex \Delta x = - \nabla f(x) &s=3$,

    we have

    $latex \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x = - \nabla f(x)^T \nabla f(x) = -\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$

    and so the Armijo-Goldstein inequality takes the form

    $latex f(x^+) - f(x) \leq -\alpha t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. &s=3$









    Step 2.
    Recall strong convexity and $latex S&s=3$ closed gave

    $latex f(y) \leq f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{M}{2}\Vert y-x \Vert_2^2. &s=3$

    Taking $latex y = x^+ = x - t\nabla f(x) &s=3$ gives

    $latex f(x^+) \leq f(x) -t \Vert \nabla f(x)\Vert_2^2 + \frac{Mt^2}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. &s=3$

    Noting

    $latex \begin{aligned} 0 < t < \frac{1}{M} &\implies Mt^2 \leq t\\ & \implies -t + \frac{Mt^2}{2} \leq - \frac{t}{2}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and using $latex \alpha \in (0,0.5) &s=3$, we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^+) &\leq f(x) - \frac{t}{2}\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2\\ &\leq f(x) - \alpha t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    In conclusion: if $latex 0<t<\frac{1}{M}&s=3$, then the Armijo-Goldstein inequality

    $latex f(x^+) - f(x) \leq - \alpha t \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$

    holds.







    Step 3.
    By preceding step: the backtracking line search terminates once $latex 0 < t < \frac{1}{M} &s=3$ or at the initial step with $latex t = 1 &s=3$.
    Supposing
    • $latex \frac{1}{M} < 1 &s=3$
    • line search does not terminate at $latex t = 1&s=3$,
    then line search terminates for some $latex t \geq \frac{\beta}{M}&s=3$.
    Indeed, let $latex k &s=3$ be the largest integer such that the line search does not terminate at $latex t= \beta^k &s=3$.
    Then $latex \beta^k \geq \frac{1}{M}&s=3$ and termination happens at $latex t'=\beta^{k+1}&s=3$, which consequently satisfies $latex t' \geq \frac{\beta}{M}&s=3$.
    The claim follows.







    Step 4.
    If backtracking line search terminates at $latex t=1&s=3$, then

    $latex f(x^+) \leq f(x) - \alpha \Vert\nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$.

    If backtracking line search terminates for some $latex t' \geq \frac{\beta}{M}&s=3$, then

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^+) &\leq f(x) - \alpha t' \Vert\nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 \\ &\leq f(x) - \frac{\alpha \beta}{M} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, if either $latex t=1 &s=3$ or $latex t=t'&s=3$, then

    $latex f(x^+) \leq f(x) - \text{min}\left\{ \alpha, \frac{\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} \Vert\nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$.









    Step 5.
    Recall

    $latex 2m(f(x) - p^\star) \leq \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2 &s=3$.

    The preceding step thus gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x^+) - p^\star &\leq f(x) - p^\star - \text{min}\left\{ \alpha, \frac{\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} \Vert\nabla f(x) \Vert_2^2\\ & \leq f(x) - p^\star - \text{min}\left\{ \alpha, \frac{\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} \cdot 2m(f(x)-p^\star)\\ &\leq \left( 1 - \text{min}\left\{ 2m\alpha, \frac{2m\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} \right) (f(x) - p^\star)\\ &:= c(f(x)-p^\star), \end{aligned} &s=3$

    where

    $latex c= 1 - \text{min}\left\{ 2m\alpha, \frac{2m\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} < 1&s=3$.

    Iterating the argument gives

    $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq c^k (f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) &s=3$

    and whence the desired convergence since $latex c^k \to 0 &s=3$.







    Step 6.
    To find $latex k &s=3$ such that

    $latex f(x^{(k)}) - p^\star \leq \epsilon &s=3$,

    we solve

    $latex \begin{aligned} c^k (f(x^{(0)}) -p^\star) \leq \epsilon \end{aligned} &s=3$

    in terms of $latex k &s=3$:

    $latex \frac{\log\left( \epsilon^{-1}(f(x^{(0)}) - p^\star) \right)}{\log(1/c)} \leq k. &s=3$

    Therefore, as soon as $latex k &s=3$ surpasses this number, the tolerance is satisfied.
    Recall

    $latex c=1 - \text{min}\left\{ 2m\alpha, \frac{2m\alpha\beta}{M} \right\} &s=3$.











    Remark.
    The remarks after the convergence theorem for exact line search apply to this convergence theorem for backtracking line search.














    On the Condition Number Objectives
    1. Define condition numbers for matrices and convex subsets.
    2. Establish connection between strong convexity of a function and the condition number on its (convex) sublevel sets.
    3. Prepare for understanding how conditioning on the Hessian is important for convergence in gradient descent algorithms.








    Condition Number of a Matrix
    Given a matrix $latex A \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$, let

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_{\text{min}}(A) &= \text{ minimum eigenvalue of } A\\ \lambda_{\text{max}}(A) &= \text{ maximum eigenvalue of } A. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Condition number: the ratio

    $latex \kappa(A) := \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)}. &s=3$









    Remarks.
    1. There holds $latex \kappa(A^{-1}) = \kappa(A)&s=3$.
      Indeed:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_{\text{min}}(A^{-1}) &= \frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)}\\ \lambda_{\text{max}}(A^{-1}) &= \frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)} \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \begin{aligned} \kappa(A^{-1})=\frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(A^{-1})}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(A^{-1})} = \frac{1/\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)}{1/\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)} = \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)} = \kappa(A). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    2. For $latex c>0 &s=3$, there holds $latex \kappa(cA) = \kappa(A)&s=3$.
      Indeed:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_{\text{min}}(cA) &= c \lambda_{\text{max}}(A)\\ \lambda_{\text{max}}(cA) &= c \lambda_{\text{min}}(A) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \kappa(cA) = \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(cA)}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(cA)} = \frac{c\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)}{c\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)} = \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(A)} = \kappa(A). &s=3$









    Condition Number of a Convex Subset
    Let $latex K \subset \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$ be a convex subet.
    Directional Width: given a direction

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nu & \in \mathbb{R}^n\\ \Vert \nu \Vert_2 &= 1, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    the number

    $latex \begin{aligned} W(K,\nu) := \sup\{\nu^T x : x \in K\} - \inf\{ \nu^Tx : x \in K \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Remarks.
    1. $latex W(K,\cdot) &s=3$ provides a means of measuring relative eccentricity between any two directions; e.g.,

      $latex W(K,\nu_1)>W(K,\nu_2) &s=3$

      implies $latex K&s=3$ is elongated more in the direction $latex \nu_1&s=3$ than $latex \nu_2&s=3$.
    2. The numerical value $latex W(K,\nu)&s=3$ is independent of the placement of $latex K&s=3$.
      Justification. Given $latex x_0 \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$, let

      $latex K + x_0 = \{x + x_0 : x \in K \} &s=3$.

      Then

      $latex \begin{aligned} W(K+x_0,\nu)&=\sup\{\nu^Tx : x \in K + x_0 \} - \inf\{\nu^Tx : x \in K + x_0 \} \\ &= \sup\{\nu^T(x+x_0) : x \in K \} - \inf\{\nu^T(x+x_0) : x \in K \}\\ &= \sup\{\nu^Tx : x \in K \} + \nu^T x_0 - \inf\{\nu^Tx:x \in K\} - \nu^T x_0\\ &= \sup\{\nu^Tx : x \in K \} - \inf\{\nu^Tx:x \in K\}\\ &= W(K,\nu). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Examples:
    1. Let

      $latex B(0,r) = \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \Vert x \Vert_2 < r \}&s=3$

      be the open ball of radius $latex r>0&s=3$ and center $latex 0 \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
      Then

      $latex \begin{aligned} W(B(0,r),\nu) &= \sup\{\nu^Tx : \Vert x \Vert_2 < r \} - \inf\{\nu^Tx: \Vert x \Vert_2 < r\}\\ &= \nu^T (r\nu) - \nu^T(-r\nu)\\ &= 2r. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      As expected: directional widths of Euclidean balls are independent of the direction $latex \nu&s=3$.








    2. Let $latex K_1,K_2 \subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ be convex subsets with $latex K_1 \subset K_2 &s=3$.
      Using

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sup\{\nu^T x: x \in K_1 \} \leq \sup\{\nu^T x : x \in K_2\}\\ \inf\{\nu^T x: x \in K_1 \} \geq \inf\{\nu^T x : x \in K_2\}\\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

      we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} W(K_1,\nu) &= \sup\{\nu^T x: x \in K_1 \} - \inf\{\nu^T x: x \in K_1 \} \\ &\leq \sup\{\nu^T x : x \in K_2\} - \inf\{\nu^T x : x \in K_2\}\\ &=W(K_2,\nu). \end{aligned} &s=3$

      As expected: larger sets have larger directional widths








    Extremal Widths: the maximum and minimum widths are defined as

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{maximum width} &= W_{\text{max}}(K) = \sup\{W(K,\nu): \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1 \}\\ \text{minimum width} &= W_{\text{min}}(K) = \inf\{W(K,\nu): \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1 \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Condition number: the ratio

    $latex \kappa(K) = \left(\frac{W_{\text{max}}(K)}{W_{\text{min}}(K)} \right)^2. &s=3$

    Remark.
    $latex \kappa(K) >1 &s=3$ measures a lack of symmetry and indicates $latex K &s=3$ is thin (or elongated) in some preferred direction; however, $latex \kappa(K)=1 &s=3$ does not indicate $latex K&s=3$ is a ball.







    Examples.
    1. Let

      $latex B(0,r) = \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \Vert x \Vert_2 < r \}&s=3$

      be the open ball of radius $latex r>0&s=3$ and center $latex 0 \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
      As shown above,

      $latex W(B(0,r),\nu) = 2r&s=3$

      is constant relative to $latex \nu&s=3$.
      Thus

      $latex \begin{aligned} W_{\text{max}}(B(0,r)) = \sup\{W(B(0,r),\nu): \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1 \} = 2r\\ W_{\text{min}}(B(0,r)) = \inf\{W(B(0,r),\nu): \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1 \} = 2r\\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \kappa(B(0,r)) = \left( \frac{W_{\text{max}}(B(0,r))}{W_{\text{min}}(B(0,r))} \right)^2 = \left( \frac{2r}{2r} \right)^2=1. &s=3$

      N.B.: Euclidean balls are not the only convex sets satisfying $latex \kappa = 1&s=3$; e.g., Reuleaux triangles are of constant width.








    2. Let $latex K_1,K_2,\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$ be convex subsets with $latex K_1 \subset \Omega \subset K_2 &s=3$.
      From a previous example above, we have

      $latex W(K_1,\nu) \leq W(\Omega,\nu) \leq W(K_2,\nu). &s=3$

      Thus

      $latex \begin{aligned} W_{\text{min}}(K_1) &= \inf W(K_1,\nu) \leq \inf W(\Omega,\nu) = W_{\text{min}}(\Omega)\\ W_{\text{max}}(\Omega) &= \sup W(\Omega,\nu) \leq \sup W(K_2,\nu) = W_{\text{max}}(K_2) \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \begin{aligned} \kappa(\Omega) = \left( \frac{W_{\text{max}}(\Omega)}{W_{\text{min}}(\Omega)} \right)^2 \leq \left( \frac{W_{\text{max}}(K_2)}{W_{\text{min}}(K_1)} \right)^2 \end{aligned}. &s=3$

      For Example, if $latex K_1 = B(0,r_1) &s=3$ and $latex K_2 = B(0,r_2)&s=3$, then

      $latex \begin{aligned} \kappa(\Omega) \leq \left( \frac{W_{\text{max}}(K_2)}{W_{\text{min}}(K_1)} \right)^2 = \left( \frac{2r_2}{2r_1} \right)^2 = \frac{r_2^2}{r_1^2} \end{aligned}. &s=3$









    3. Given $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$ and $latex x_0 \in \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$, define the set

      $latex \mathcal{E} = \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : (x-x_0)^TQ^{-1}(x-x_0) \leq 1 \}. &s=3$

      N.B.: $latex \mathcal{E}&s=3$ is an ellipsoid and all ellipsoids can be described like this.
      We set out to determine the condition number of $latex \mathcal{E}&s=3$.
      In fact, we prove the following.

      Proposition. Let $latex \mathcal{E} &s=3$ and $latex Q &s=3$ be as above. Then their condition numbers are the same:

      $latex \kappa(\mathcal{E}) = \kappa(Q) = \kappa(Q^{-1})&s=3$.

      Proof. Step 0.
      As observed above, we take WLOG $latex x_0=0&s=3$.
      We need to compute the directional widths $latex W(\mathcal{E},\nu) &s=3$ and therefore need to first compute

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sup\{ \nu^T x: x \in \mathcal{E}\} \quad \text{ and } \quad \inf\{ \nu^T x : x \in \mathcal{E} \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      This can be achieved by solving the optimization problems

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \pm \nu^Tx\\ \text{subject to}& x^TQ^{-1}x \leq 1 \end{cases}. &s=3$

      We employ Lagrangian duality.
      N.B.: these problems are convex and satisfy Slater's condition.







      Step 1.
      The Lagrangian is

      $latex L(x,\lambda) = \pm \nu^Tx + \lambda (x^T Q^{-1} x - 1). &s=3$

      Its gradient is

      $latex \nabla_x L(x,\lambda) = \pm \nu + 2\lambda Q^{-1} x. &s=3$

      The KKT conditions demand

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda( x^T Q^{-1} x - 1) &= 0\\\ \nabla_x L &= 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Observe that $latex \lambda = 0&s=3$ would imply $latex \nu=0&s=3$, which is a contradiction.
      Thus $latex \lambda>0&s=3$ and so complementary slackness implies

      $latex x^T Q^{-1} x = 1 &s=3$

      and $latex \nabla L=0&s=3$ gives

      $latex x = \mp \frac{1}{2\lambda} Q\nu. &s=3$









      Step 2.
      Using

      $latex \begin{aligned} 1&=x^T Q^{-1} x \\ x &= \mp \frac{1}{2\lambda} Q\nu \end{aligned} &s=3$

      we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} 1 &= \left(\mp\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\nu \right)^T Q^{-1} \left(\mp\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\nu \right)\\ &=\frac{1}{4\lambda^2}\nu^T Q \nu. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Solving for $latex \lambda&s=3$ gives

      $latex \lambda =\frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\nu^T Q \nu}= \frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\nu^T Q^{1/2} Q^{1/2}\nu} = \frac{1}{2} \Vert Q^{1/2}\nu \Vert_2. &s=3$









      Step 3.
      Using our evaluation of $latex \lambda &s=3$ gives

      $latex \begin{aligned} x^\star &= \mp \frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\nu\\ &= \mp \frac{1}{\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2} Q\nu\\ p^\star &= \pm \nu^T x^\star\\ &= - \frac{1}{\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2} \nu^TQ\nu\\ &= - \Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      This gives

      $latex \begin{aligned} \sup\{ \nu^Tx : x \in \mathcal{E}\} &= - \inf\{ - \nu^T x : x \in \mathcal{E} \}\\ &=\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2\\ \inf\{ \nu^Tx : x \in \mathcal{E}\} &= -\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2. \end{aligned} &s=3$









      Step 4.
      We can now compute the directional and extremal widths.
      From Step 3. we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} W(\mathcal{E},\nu) & = \sup\{ \nu^Tx : x \in \mathcal{E}\} - \inf\{ \nu^Tx : x \in \mathcal{E}\}\\ &=\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2 - (-\Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2)\\ &= 2 \Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      and so

      $latex \begin{aligned} W_{\text{min}}(\mathcal{E}) &= \inf\{2 \Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2: \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1\}\\ &= 2 \sqrt{\lambda_{\text{min}}(Q)}\\ W_{\text{max}}(\mathcal{E}) &= \inf\{2 \Vert Q^{1/2}\nu\Vert_2: \Vert \nu\Vert_2 = 1\}\\ &= 2 \sqrt{\lambda_{\text{max}}(Q)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      At last, we compute the condition number of $latex \mathcal{E}&s=3$:

      $latex \kappa(\mathcal{E}) = \left(\frac{W_{\text{max}}(\mathcal{E})}{W_{\text{min}}(\mathcal{E})} \right)^2 = \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(\mathcal{Q})}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\mathcal{Q})} = \kappa(Q) &s=3$

      which is what we wanted to show.








    Strong Convexity and Conditioning
    Let $latex f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R} &s=3$ be strongly convex and have closed sublevel set $latex S = \{ x : f(x) \leq f(x^{(0)})\}&s=3$ for some $latex x^{(0)} \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$.
    Recall: it follows that

    $latex mId \preceq \nabla^2f(x) \preceq M Id&s=3$

    for some $latex 0<m \leq M < \infty &s=3$.

    For $latex p^\star < \alpha \leq f(x^{(0)}) &s=3$, let

    $latex S_\alpha = \{ x : f(x) \leq \alpha \} &s=3$

    be the sublevel set corresponding to $latex \alpha&s=3$.
    We set out to prove that the condition number $latex \kappa(S_\alpha)&s=3$ is controlled by the conditioning of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$.








    Remark.
    The condition number of $latex \nabla^2 f(x) &s=3$ depends on $latex x &s=3$.
    However,

    $latex mId \preceq \nabla^2f(x) \preceq M Id&s=3$

    gives the upper estimate of

    $latex \kappa(\nabla^2 f(x)) \leq \frac{M}{m} &s=3$

    for all $latex x \in S&s=3$.
    Important points:
    1. $latex M &s=3$ can be taken to be arbitrarily large and $latex m &s=3$ arbitrarily small, this estimate can be very bad.
    2. $latex M &s=3$ depends on $latex S &s=3$ and hence the initial choice $latex x^{(0)}&s=3$.
      The further $latex x^{(0)}&s=3$ is chosen from $latex x^\star&s=3$, the worse (larger) $latex M &s=3$ can be.








    Little example.
    Let $latex f(x_1,x_2) = e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} &s=3$.
    One computes

    $latex \begin{aligned} e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} Id& \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) \\ &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} \begin{bmatrix} 1+x_1^2 & x_1x_2\\ x_1x_2 & 1+x_2^2 \end{bmatrix}\\ & \preceq e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)}(1+x_1^2+x_2^2) Id \end{aligned} &s=3$

    with the inequalities given by the extremal eigenvalues.
    Evidently $latex \kappa(\nabla^2 f(x))&s=3$ depends on $latex x&s=3$.
    If $latex x^{(0)}=(1,1) &s=3$, then $latex S = \{ x_1^2+x_2^2 \leq 2 \}&s=3$ and so we may take

    $latex \begin{aligned} Id& \preceq \nabla^2 f(x) \preceq 3e Id \end{aligned} &s=3$

    with $latex m=1,M=3e &s=3$.







    Proposition. Let $latex f, m , M, \alpha, S_\alpha &s=3$ be as above. Then

    $latex \kappa(S_\alpha) \leq \frac{M}{m} &s=3$.

    Remark.
    Thus, the better conditioned $latex \nabla^2 f(x)&s=3$ is in the sense

    $latex M/m \approx 1 &s=3$,

    then the more conditioned its sublevel sets are.
    Conversely, if the sublevel sets of $latex f &s=3$ are poorly conditioned in the sense

    $latex \kappa(S_\alpha) \gg 1 &s=3$,

    then $latex \nabla^2 f(x)&s=3$ will be poorly conditioned, i.e.,

    $latex M \gg m &s=3$.

    Proof. Step 1.
    Using

    $latex mId \preceq \nabla^2f(x) \preceq M Id&s=3$

    and

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T(y-x) + \frac{1}{2}(y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(z)(y-x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for suitable $latex z &s=3$, we have (taking $latex x=x^\star&s=3$):

    $latex \frac{m}{2}\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2 + p^\star \leq f(y) \leq \frac{M}{2}\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2 + p^\star &s=3$

    for $latex y \in S&s=3$.






    Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} B_i &= \left\{ y \in S: \Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha - p^\star)}{M}} \right\}\\ B_o &= \left\{ y \in S: \Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha - p^\star)}{m}} \right\} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    We show

    $latex B_i \subset S_\alpha \subset B_o &s=3$.

    By observations above, the conditioning of $latex S_\alpha&s=3$ is estimated in terms of the extremal widths of $latex B_i &s=3$ and $latex B_o &s=3$.







    Step 2.
    To begin, let $latex y \in B_i &s=3$.
    Using

    $latex \Vert y - x^\star \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha-p^\star)}{M}} &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) &\leq \frac{M}{2}\Vert y-x^\star \Vert_2^2 + p^\star\\ &\leq \frac{M}{2}\frac{2(\alpha-p^\star)}{M} + p^\star\\ &=\alpha \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so $latex y \in S_\alpha&s=3$.








    Step 3.
    For the other containment, let $latex y \in S_\alpha &s=3$.
    Using

    $latex f(y) \leq \alpha &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{m}{2}\Vert y - x^\star \Vert_2^2 + p^\star \leq \alpha \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and whence

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert y - x^\star \Vert_2 \leq \sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha-p^\star)}{m}}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    thereby establishing $latex y \in B_o&s=3$.







    Step 4.
    By observations made above, we use $latex B_i \subset S_\alpha \subset B_o &s=3$ to conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} \kappa(S_\alpha) &= \left(\frac{W_{\text{max}}(S_\alpha)}{W_{\text{min}}(S_\alpha)} \right)^2\\ &\leq \left(\frac{W_{\text{max}}(B_o)}{W_{\text{min}}(B_i)} \right)^2\\ &\leq \left(\frac{\sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha-p^\star)}{m}}}{\sqrt{\frac{2(\alpha-p^\star)}{M}}} \right)^2\\ &=\frac{M}{m}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Proposition. Let $latex f, m , M, \alpha, S_\alpha &s=3$ be as above. Then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lim_{\alpha \to p^\star } \kappa(S_\alpha) = \kappa\left(\nabla^2 f(x^\star) \right). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Remark.
    The point is that, as the sublevel sets shrink to $latex x^\star &s=3$, the problem's conditioning is dictated by the conditioning of $latex \nabla^2 f(x^\star)&s=3$.
    Proof (sketch). Step 1.
    Using Taylor approximation at $latex x^\star &s=3$, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) &\approx f(x^\star) + \nabla f(x^\star)^T (y-x^\star) + \frac{1}{2}(y-x^\star)^T \nabla^2 f(x^\star)(y-x^\star)\\ &= p^\star + \frac{1}{2}(y-x^\star)^T \nabla^2 f(x^\star)(y-x^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for $latex y &s=3$ near $latex x^\star &s=3$.







    Step 2.
    Observing that

    $latex \bigcap_{\alpha>p^\star} S_\alpha = S_{p^\star} = \{ x^\star \}&s=3$,

    we can choose $latex \alpha &s=3$ near $latex p^\star &s=3$ so that $latex y \in S_\alpha&s=3$ is near $latex x^\star &s=3$.
    Then the above Taylor approximation concludes $latex y \in S_\alpha&s=3$ iff

    $latex \begin{aligned} \alpha \gtrsim p^\star + \frac{1}{2}(y-x^\star)^T \nabla^2 f(x^\star)(y-x^\star) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which holds iff

    $latex \begin{aligned} 2(\alpha-p^\star) \gtrsim (y-x^\star)^T \nabla^2 f(x^\star)(y-x^\star). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Step 3.
    Previous step indicates: if $latex y \in S_\alpha&s=3$ then $latex y &s=3$ belongs to or nearly belongs to

    $latex \begin{aligned} \{ y : (y-x^\star)^T \nabla^2 f(x^\star)(y-x^\star) \leq 2(\alpha-p^\star) \}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is an ellipsoid defined by the matrix

    $latex \left(\nabla^2 f(x^\star) \right)^{-1} &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \kappa(S_\alpha) \approx \kappa(\nabla^2 f(x^\star)) &s=3$.

    (Recall $latex \kappa(A) = \kappa(A^{-1})&s=3$.)
    Taking $latex \alpha &s=3$ closer to $latex p^\star&s=3$ evidently improves these approximations and so we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lim_{\alpha \to p^\star } \kappa(S_\alpha) = \kappa\left(\nabla^2 f(x^\star) \right). \end{aligned} &s=3$
















    Example (See also CO Example 9.3.2)
    Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x_1,x_2) = e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Goal
    Argue how changing conditioning of sublevel sets of $latex f&s=3$ affect convergence.






    Sublevel set
    Observe

    $latex \begin{aligned} S_\alpha &= \{e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} \leq \alpha \}\\ &= \{ x_1^2 + x_2^2 \leq 2\log \alpha \} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    are disks centered at the origin and with radius $latex \sqrt{2\log\alpha}&s=3$.
    As such, the sublevel sets are well-conditioned:

    $latex \kappa(S_\alpha)=1. &s=3$







    Conditioning of the Hessian
    We compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x_1,x_2) &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} \begin{bmatrix} x_1\\x_2 \end{bmatrix}\\ \nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2) &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} \begin{bmatrix} 1+x_1^2 & x_1x_2\\ x_1x_2 & 1+x_2^2 \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    The extremal eigenvalues of $latex \nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2) &s=3$ are

    $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)) &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)}\\ \lambda_{\text{max}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)) &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)}(1+x_1^2+x_2^2). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore

    $latex \kappa(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)) = \frac{\lambda_{\text{max}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2))}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2))} = 1 + x_1^2+x_2^2 &s=3$

    and so $latex \nabla^2 f&s=3$ is reasonably well-conditioned near $latex x^\star = (0,0)&s=3$.






    Let $latex x^{(0)} = (1,1) &s=3$ be the initial point and define the initial sublevel set

    $latex S = \{ f(x) \leq f(x^{(0)}) = e\}. &s=3$

    On $latex S&s=3$, we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} m=1 &\leq e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)} = \lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)) \\ \lambda_{\text{max}}(\nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)) &= e^{\frac{1}{2}(x_1^2+x_2^2)}(1+x_1^2+x_2^2) \leq 3e =M. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Thus $latex f&s=3$ satisfies the strong convexity bounds

    $latex \begin{aligned} Id \preceq \nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2) \preceq 3e Id, \quad (x_1,x_2) \in S. \end{aligned} &s=3$







    Applying Gradient Descent
    Using gradient descent with exact line search solves the problem in one step.
    This is due to radiality of sublevel sets and $latex \nabla f&s=3$ and $latex f&s=3$ being minimized at the origin.
    Using backtracking line search, the problem is solved without issue.







    Unconditioning the Problem
    Consider the anisotropic dilation: $latex (x_1,x_2) \mapsto (\gamma x_1,x_2)&s=3$ for an arbitrary $latex \gamma > 1 &s=3$.

    Applying this dilation to $latex f&s=3$ results in the function

    $latex f_\gamma (x_1,x_2) = f(\gamma x_1,x_2) = e^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^2 x_1^2 + x_2^2)}. &s=3$

    Observe: the sublevel sets of $latex f_\gamma&s=3$ are of the form

    $latex \begin{aligned} S_\alpha &= \{ e^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^2 x_1^2 + x_2^2)} \leq \alpha \}\\ &=\{ \gamma^2 x_1^2 + x_2^2 \leq 2\log \alpha\}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is an ellipse whose condition number depends on $latex \gamma&s=3$.
    Evidently, the larger $latex \gamma&s=3$ is, the worse $latex S_\alpha&s=3$ is conditioned.
    N.B.: by the anistropy, $latex f_\gamma&s=3$ is more sensitive to change in $latex x_1&s=3$ than $latex x_2&s=3$.







    Now compute

    $latex \nabla f_\gamma(x) = e^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^2 x_1^2 + x_2^2)} \begin{bmatrix} \gamma^2 x_1\\ x_2 \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    The effects of poor conditioning can already be observed: for large $latex \gamma>1,&s=3$ considering the step

    $latex x^+ = x - t e^{\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^2 x_1^2 + x_2^2)} \begin{bmatrix} \gamma^2 x_1\\ x_2 \end{bmatrix}, &s=3$

    we see $latex x^+&s=3$ is obtained from $latex x&s=3$ by stepping significantly further in the $latex x_1&s=3$-coordinate than the $latex x_2&s=3$-coordinate.
    This is despite the fact that the minimizer is still at the origin.







    Applying Gradient Descent
    While exact line search will still find the minimizer for this problem quickly (2 steps), one finds backtracking line search becomes impractical for large $latex \gamma &s=3$.
    Recalling that Armijo-Goldstein's inequality is satisfied for $latex t<1/M&s=3$, this is unsurprising since the larger $latex M&s=3$ is, the smaller $latex t &s=3$ is likely needed to be in order for the Armijo-Goldstein's inequality to hold.














    Steepest Descent Gradient Descent as Steepest Descent:
    Recall: if $latex t>0&s=3$ is small and $latex \Vert \nu \Vert_2=1&s=3$, then Taylor's approximation gives

    $latex f(x+t\nu) - f(x) \approx t \nabla f(x)^T \nu. &s=3$

    If $latex \nu &s=3$ is a descent direction, then

    $latex t \nabla f(x)^T \nu<0 &s=3$

    and this quantity records the approximate decrease in $latex f&s=3$ in the direction $latex \nu&s=3$ upon taking the small step

    $latex x=x + t \nu. &s=3$









    If we want to decrease $latex f&s=3$ efficiently, we ask: for given step size $latex t>0 &s=3$, which direction $latex \nu &s=3$ effects greatest descent?









    Observing

    $latex \begin{aligned} -\frac{1}{\Vert u \Vert_2} u = \text{argmin}\{ u^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert_2 = 1 \} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for any nonzero $latex u \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$, we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} -\frac{1}{\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2} \nabla f(x) = \text{argmin}\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert_2 = 1 \}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, the direction

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}:=-\frac{1}{\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2} \nabla f(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    gives the direction of greatest decrease: for small $latex t>0 &s=3$ we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x + t \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}) - f(x) & \approx t \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}\\ &\leq t \nabla f(x)^T \nu\\ & \approx f(x + t \nu ) - f(x) . \end{aligned} &s=3$

    One may call such a direction a steepest descent direction.







    Quadratic Norm Steepest Descent
    Suppose for $latex \alpha &s=3$ near $latex p^\star&s=3$, the sublevel sets $latex S_\alpha&s=3$ are poorly conditioned.
    Suppose there is a change of variable/coordinates $latex \bar x = P^{1/2} x&s=3$ so that

    $latex \bar f(\bar x) := f(P^{-1/2}\bar x) = f(x) &s=3$

    has well-condition sublevel sets.
    Then gradient descent in $latex \bar x&s=3$-coordinates is likely to behave well when minimizing $latex \bar f(\bar x)&s=3$.








    Compute

    $latex \nabla_{\bar x} \bar f(\bar x) = P^{-1/2} (\nabla f)(P^{-1/2}\bar x) = P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x). &s=3$

    Then the steepest descent direction in $latex \bar x &s=3$-coordinates is

    $latex \Delta \bar x = - \frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x). &s=3$

    Converting back to original coordinates $latex x = P^{-1/2}\bar x&s=3$:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x &= P^{-1/2}\Delta \bar x = - \frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1} \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    But then (in $latex x&s=3$-coordinates)

    $latex \begin{aligned} -\frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1} \nabla f(x) &= - \frac{1}{\Vert \nabla_{\bar x} \bar f(\bar x)\Vert_2} \nabla \bar f(\bar x)\\ &= \text{argmin}\left\{ \nabla \bar f(\bar x)^T \bar\nu : \Vert \bar \nu \Vert_2=1\right\}\\ &= \text{argmin}\left\{ \left( P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x) \right)^T P^{1/2}\nu : \Vert P^{1/2} \nu \Vert_2=1\right\}\\ &= \text{argmin}\left\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert P^{1/2} \nu \Vert_2=1\right\}. \end{aligned} &s=4$

    Therefore, the gradient descent direction obtained by the change of variable is the "steepest descent direction"

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{argmin}\left\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert P^{1/2} \nu \Vert_2=1\right\} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    relative to the norm

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_P := \Vert P^{1/2} x \Vert_2. &s=3$









    In summary:
    • After a change of variable, the problem becomes well-conditioned and gradient descent may be used to obtain a steepest descent direction relative to the norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$ (in the $latex \bar x &s=3$-coordinates).
    • Undoing the change of variable, this steepest descent direction is realized as the steepest descent relative to a different norm.









    The observations above suggest that a change of variable may result in better computational performance (via improving conditioning).
    Also indicated: gradient descent in the new variables was equivalent to finding the steepest descent direction with respect to the norm $latex \Vert P^{1/2} \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$ in the original variables.
    Motivated by this: consider steepest descent with respect to general norms.







    Review on Norms
     A norm on a vector space $latex V &s=3$ is a function $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert: V \to \mathbb{R}_{+}&s=3$ satisfying
    1. triangle inequality: $latex \Vert x+y \Vert \leq \Vert x \Vert + \Vert y \Vert &s=3$ for all $latex x,y \in V &s=3$.
    2. Homogeneity: $latex \Vert c x \Vert = |c| \Vert x \Vert &s=3$ for all $latex c \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$ and $latex x \in V &s=3$.
    3. Positive definiteness: if $latex x \in V&s=3$ satisfies $latex \Vert x \Vert = 0&s=3$, then $latex x = 0 &s=3$.








    Examples of important norms are:
    1. Standard Euclidean norm:

      $latex \Vert x \Vert_2 = \sqrt{x_1^2 + \cdots + x_n^2} &s=3$

    2. Quadratic norms for $latex P \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n&s=3$:

      $latex \Vert x \Vert_P = \Vert P^{1/2} x \Vert_2.&s=3$

    3. $latex \ell_p &s=3$ norms for $latex 1 \leq p < \infty &s=3$:

      $latex \Vert x \Vert_p = \left( |x_1|^p + \cdots + |x_n|^p \right)^{1/p}.&s=3$

    4. Chebyshev/$latex \ell_\infty &s=3$ norm:

      $latex \Vert x \Vert_\infty = \max\{ |x_1|,\ldots,|x_n| \}.&s=3$









    Norm spheres: for a given norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$, the unit norm sphere

    $latex \{ x : \Vert x \Vert = 1 \}&s=3$

    defines a collection of "directions" relative to the norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$.







    Examples of unit norm spheres are indicated below.







    Given a norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$, we define the dual norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_* &s=3$ to be

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_* := \sup\{ x^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert = 1 \} &s=3$.

    (This is the "operator/matrix norm" of $latex x^T &s=3$.)

    Important examples are recorded in the following table.

    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_* &s=3$
    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$
    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_P := \Vert P^{1/2} \cdot \Vert_2&s=3$, for $latex P \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n&s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_{P^{-1}} := \Vert P^{-1/2} \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$
    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_1 &s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_\infty &s=3$
    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_\infty &s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_1 &s=3$
    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_p &s=3$, for $latex 1 < p < \infty &s=3$ $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_q&s=3$, where $latex \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q} = 1&s=3$









    General Steepest Descent
    Fix a norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$ on $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.

    Normalized steepest descent direction for $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$ : any element

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} \in \text{argmin}\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert = 1 \}. &s=3$



    Intuition: the step $latex x=x+t\Delta x_{\text{nsd}}&s=3$ (for small $latex t>0&s=3$) effects the greatest decrease in the objective among all directions $latex \nu &s=3$ satisfying $latex \Vert \nu \Vert =1 &s=3$.
    Recall:

    $latex f(x+t\nu) - f(x) \approx t \nabla f(x)^T \nu. &s=3$









    In practice:
    1. $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$ is chosen depending on problem; however, gradient descent and Newton's method (given below) are common.
    2. Convergence results may depend on choice of $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$.
    3. $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}&s=3$ is generally not unique; e.g. this can occur for $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_1 &s=3$, as indicated below.
      Here, the red "x" indicates $latex -\nabla f(x) &s=3$ and the black dots indicate two distinct $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &s=3$.
      In fact: $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}&s=3$ may be taken to be any point on the unit norm sphere in the first quadrant.








    N.B.: by

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} \in \text{argmin}\{\nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert =1\} &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &= \inf\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert = 1 \} \\ &= -\sup\{ -\nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert = 1 \} \\ &= -\Vert \nabla f(x)\Vert_*. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Recall:

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_* = \sup\{ x^T \nu : \Vert \nu \Vert = 1\}. &s=3$









    Unnormalized steepest descent direction: for any $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &s=3$, a descent direction of the form

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}} = \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}. &s=3$



    N.B.:
    1. This choice of "unnormalization" gives

      $latex \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{sd}} =-\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_*^2. &s=3$

    2. Using $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ instead of $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}&s=3$ uses both the direction of steepest descent (with respect to $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert &s=3$) and the rate of decrease of $latex f&s=3$.
    3. Exact line search does not see choice of normalization.
      Indeed for $latex c>0&s=3$, we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} \text{argmin}\{f(x+tc\Delta x): t \geq 0 \} = \frac{1}{c}\,\text{argmin}\{f(x+t\Delta x): t \geq 0 \} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    4. Theoretically: choice of normalization does not matter.
      Pragmatically: choice of normalization may affect behavior of convergence.








    Steepest Descent Method
    The general steepest descent method may now be recorded. 
    
given initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
repeat:
1. Compute: $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}} &s=3$.
2. Perform line search to determine step size $latex t &s=2$.
3. Take step: $latex x:= x + t\Delta x_{\text{sd}} &s=2$.
until: stopping criterion holds.
    N.B.: for exact line search, either $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ or $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}&s=3$ may be used.
















    Examples For various norms, we will compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} &\Delta x_{\text{nsd}} \\ &\Delta x_{\text{sd}} = \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Example 1
    Let $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert = \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$.
    Observe

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &=\text{argmin}\{ \nabla f(x)^T\nu : \Vert \nu \Vert_2 = 1 \}\\ &= -\frac{1}{\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2} \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Next, using

    $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_* = \Vert \cdot \Vert_2 &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* = \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{sd}} &= \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}\\ &=\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 \left( -\frac{1}{\Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_2} \nabla f(x) \right) \\ &=- \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$


    Conclusion: gradient descent method is the steepest descent with respect to the Euclidean norm $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_2&s=3$.







    Example 2
    Let $latex P \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n&s=3$ and let

    $latex \Vert x \Vert = \Vert x \Vert_P := \Vert P^{1/2} x\Vert_2 .&s=3$

    Compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &= \text{argmin}\left\{ \nabla f(x)^T \nu : \Vert P^{1/2} \nu \Vert_2=1\right\}\\ &= P^{-1/2} \text{argmin}\left\{ \nabla f(x)^T P^{-1/2}\mu : \Vert \mu \Vert_2=1\right\}\\ &= P^{-1/2} \text{argmin}\left\{ (P^{-1/2}\nabla f(x))^T\mu : \Vert \mu \Vert_2=1\right\}\\ &= P^{-1/2}\left( - \frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x) \right)\\ &= - \frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1} \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=4$









    Using that the dual of $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_P&s=3$ is

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_* = \Vert x \Vert_{P^{-1}} := \Vert P^{-1/2} x\Vert_2 &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* = \Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 &s=3$,

    we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{sd}} &= \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_* \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}\\ &= \Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x) \Vert_2 \left( - \frac{1}{\Vert P^{-1/2} \nabla f(x)\Vert_2} P^{-1} \nabla f(x) \right)\\ & = P^{-1}\nabla f(x) \end{aligned} &s=3$.









    Consider the change of variable $latex y = P^{1/2} x&s=3$ and

    $latex \bar f(y) = f(P^{-1/2}y) = f(x) &s=3$.

    The gradient descent step for $latex \bar f&s=3$ in the new variables $latex y &s=3$ is

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta y_{\text{sd}} &= - \nabla \bar f(y) \\ &= -\nabla_y (f(P^{-1/2}y))\\ &= - P^{-1/2} (\nabla f)(P^{-1/2}y) \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    This direction in the original variables $latex x&s=3$ is thus

    $latex \begin{aligned} P^{-1/2}\Delta y_{\text{sd}} &= - P^{-1} \nabla f(x) = \Delta x_{\text{sd}}. \end{aligned} &s=3$.









    Conclusion: obtaining $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ with respect to $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_P &s=3$ is equivalent to standard gradient descent in a different coordinate system.
    N.B.: suitably chosen $latex P&s=3$ may result in a well-conditioned problem in the new variables $latex y = P^{1/2} x&s=3$.







    Example 3
    Let

    $latex \Vert x \Vert = \Vert x \Vert_1 = |x_1| + \cdots + |x_n|, &s=3$

    and recall

    $latex \Vert x \Vert_* = \Vert x \Vert_\infty = \max\{ |x_1|,\ldots,|x_n|\}. &s=3$

    Given $latex x &s=3$, let $latex i &s=3$ be such that

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_\infty &= \max\left\{\left\vert \frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_j} \right\vert: j=1,\ldots,n\right\}\\ &= \left\vert \frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} \right\vert \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Let $latex \left\{e_j\right\} &s=3$ denote the standard basis of $latex \mathbb{R}^n&s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex \Vert \pm e_i \Vert_1 = 1&s=3$.
    One can show

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &= - \text{sgn}\left( \frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} \right) e_i. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Therefore, for $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_1 &s=3$, the steepest descent direction $latex \Delta x_{\text{nsd}} &s=3$ is in the coordinate direction in which $latex f &s=3$ changes the most.







    Lastly, one has

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{sd}} &= \Vert \nabla f(x) \Vert_\infty \Delta x_{\text{nsd}}\\ &= \left\vert \frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} \right\vert \left(- \text{sgn}\left( \frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} \right) e_i \right)\\ &=-\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} e_i \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The resulting steepest descent algorithm is often called a coordinate-descent algorithm.
    Indeed: the step

    $latex x=x-t\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x_i} e_i, \quad t>0 &s=3$

    amounts to increasing or decreasing the $latex i &s=3$th coordinate of $latex x&s=3$ according to the coordinate direction of greatest change.














    Newton's Method Suppose always that $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex and twice continuously differentiable.

    Newton step: the direction

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x). &s=3$

    N.B.: if $latex \nabla f(x) \neq 0 &s=3$, then convexity implies

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &= - \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)<0, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ is a descent direction.
    (Recall strong convexity $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$.)

    N.B.: Computing $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ involves solving the system

    $latex \begin{aligned} Hv &= -g,\\ H = \nabla^2 f(x), &\quad g = \nabla f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Exploiting special matrix structure of $latex H &s=3$ may lead to efficiently solving system.







    Example.
    Let

    $latex f(x_1,x_2) = x_1^2 + e^{x_2}. &s=3$

    Then

    $latex \nabla f(x_1,x_2) = \begin{bmatrix} 2x_1\\ e^{x_2} \end{bmatrix}, \qquad \nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2) = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 0\\ 0 & e^{x_2} \end{bmatrix}, &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &= - \nabla^2 f(x_1,x_2)^{-1} \nabla f(x_1,x_2)\\ &=- \begin{bmatrix} \frac{1}{2} & 0\\ 0 & e^{-x_2} \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 2x_1\\ e^{x_2} \end{bmatrix} \\ &= \begin{bmatrix} -x_1\\ -1 \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Observe:
    • $latex (x_1,x_2) + \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = (x_1,x_2) - (x_1,1) = (0,x_2-1) &s=3$.
      Thus, first step minimizes the quadratic part $latex x_1^2 &s=3$ of $latex f&s=3$:

      $latex f(0,x_2-1) = e^{x_2-1}&s=3$.

    • $latex x^{(k)} = (0,x_2-k) &s=3$ for $latex k>0 &s=3$.
      Thus, sequence decreases the $latex e^{x_2}&s=3$ part of $latex f&s=3$:

      $latex f(x^{(k)}) = e^{x_2-k}&s=3$.

    A couple of steps are plotted below.







    Interpretations of Newton Step
    Steepest Descent:
    Let

    $latex P = \nabla^2 f(x). &s=3$

    Consider the "Hessian norm":

    $latex \begin{aligned} \Vert y \Vert &:= \Vert y \Vert_P \\ &= \Vert P^{1/2} y \Vert_2\\ &= \left( (P^{1/2}y)^T P^{1/2}y \right)^{1/2}\\ &= \left( y^T P y \right)^{1/2}\\ &= \left( y^T \nabla^2 f(x) y \right)^{1/2}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Recall: unnormalized steepest descent direction $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ for quadratic norm $latex \Vert \cdot\Vert_Q &s=3$ is

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}} = - Q^{-1}\nabla f(x) &s=3$.

    Therefore, $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ is the steepest descent direction $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ for $latex \Vert \cdot \Vert_{P} &s=3$:

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) = - P^{-1} \nabla f(x) &s=3$.



    N.B.: finding $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ is equivalent to finding $latex \Delta x_{\text{sd}}&s=3$ after a change of variable that results in well conditioned level sets near optimizer.







    Minimizing Quadratic Approximation:
    For given $latex x &s=3$, define

    $latex F(v) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T v + \frac{1}{2} v^T \nabla^2 f(x) v. &s=3$

    Thus, $latex F(v) \approx f(x+v) &s=3$ is the second order Taylor approximation of $latex f &s=3$ at $latex x &s=3$.
    N.B.: $latex F(v) &s=3$ is a convex quadratic in $latex v&s=3$ since $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n &s=3$.
    Moreover, recalling

    $latex \begin{aligned} P \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n \implies \text{argmin}\{c + b^T v + \frac{1}{2} v^T P v\} = -P^{-1}b \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we conclude $latex F(v) &s=3$ is minimized at

    $latex v = - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) = \Delta x_{\text{nt}}. &s=3$

    Viz., the step $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ exactly minimizes the quadratic approximation.







    Conclusion:
    Given
    • $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ minimizes the quadratic approximation $latex F&s=3$ of $latex f &s=3$ at $latex x&s=3$.
    • $latex f(x+v) \approx F(v) &s=3$ is a good approximation for $latex x \approx x^\star &s=3$.
    we conclude
    • $latex p^\star = f(x^\star) \approx F(\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) &s=3$ and $latex x+\Delta x_{\text{nt}} \approx x^\star &s=3$.
      • This conclusion is depicted below.







      The Newton Decrement
      The Newton decrement at $latex x &s=3$ is the quantity

      $latex \lambda(x) = \left( \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \right)^{1/2}. &s=3$

      Detailed below: $latex \lambda(x) &s=3$ is used to estimate

      $latex \begin{aligned} &f(x) - \inf_{v} F(v)\\ &f(x) - p^\star\\ &f(x+t\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) - f(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$









      Proposition. There holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda(x) &= \Vert \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \Vert_{\nabla^2 f(x)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Proof.

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda(x) &= \left( \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \right)^{1/2}\\ &= \left( \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla^2 f(x) \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \right)^{1/2}\\ &=\left( (-\nabla^2 f(x)^{-1}\nabla f(x))^T \nabla^2 f(x) (-\nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) )\right)^{1/2}\\ &=\left(\Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \right)^{1/2}\\ &= \Vert \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \Vert_{\nabla^2 f(x)}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









      Proposition. There holds

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) - \inf_v F(v) &= \frac{1}{2}\lambda(x)^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Therefore, using

      $latex \begin{aligned} x \approx x^\star &\implies f(x+v) \approx F(v)\\ & \implies F(\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) \approx p^\star \end{aligned} &s=3$

      we conclude that $latex \frac{1}{2}\lambda^2(x) \approx f(x)-p^\star&s=3$.
      Proof.

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) - \inf F(v) &= f(x) - F(\Delta x_{\text{nt}})\\ &= f(x) - f(x) - \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nt}} - \frac{1}{2}\Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ &= \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \\ &\qquad -\frac{1}{2} \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla^2 f(x) \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)\\ &= \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \\ &\qquad - \frac{1}{2} \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)\\ &= \frac{1}{2}\nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)\\ &= \frac{1}{2}\lambda(x)^2 \end{aligned} &s=3$









      Proposition. Armijo's condition for the Newton step is

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) - f(x) \leq -\alpha t \lambda(x)^2 &s=3$

      Proof. Recall: Armijo's condition for backtracking line search is

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x) - f(x) \leq \alpha t\nabla f(x)^T \Delta x. &s=3$

      Taking

      $latex \Delta x = \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x), &s=3$

      we find

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &= \nabla f(x)^T \left( - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) \right)\\ &= -\nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)\\ &= - \lambda(x)^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Thus Armijo's condition for the Newton step is

      $latex f(x+t\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) - f(x) \leq -\alpha t \lambda(x)^2 &s=3$









      Newton's Method
      Using the Newton step and decrement, we may now record Newton's method.
      Traditionally, "Newton's method" uses step size $latex t=1&s=3$.
      The following is thus sometimes called a "damped Newton method." 

      
given 
         initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$
         tolerance $latex \epsilon>0&s=3$
repeat: 
1. Compute 
         $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = - \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1}\nabla f(x)&s=3$ 
and 
         $latex \lambda^2 = \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x) &s=2$.
2. Stopping criterion: quit if $latex \frac{1}{2}\lambda^2 \leq \epsilon &s=2$.
3. Perform line search to determine step size $latex t &s=2$.
4. Take step: $latex x = x + t\Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$.


      N.B.: Stopping criterion comes from

      $latex \begin{aligned} x \approx x^\star &\implies f(x+v) \approx F(v)\\ & \implies F(\Delta x_{\text{nt}}) \approx p^\star \end{aligned} &s=3$

      which implies $latex \frac{1}{2}\lambda^2(x) \approx f(x)-p^\star&s=3$.







      Affine Invariance
      Proposition. If
      • $latex T \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}&s=3$ is nonsingular
      • $latex x = Ty &s=3$
      • $latex \bar f(y):= f(Ty)&s=3$
      then the Newton step $latex \Delta y_{\text{nt}} &s=3$ for $latex \bar f&s=3$ is related to the Newton step $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$ for $latex f &s=3$ by

      $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = T \Delta y_{\text{nt}} &s=3$.

      Proof. Computing

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla \bar f(y) &= T^T \nabla f(x)\\ \nabla^2 \bar f(y) &= T^T \nabla^2 f(x) T, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      we find

      $latex \begin{aligned} \Delta y_{\text{nt}} &= - \nabla^2 \bar f(y)^{-1} \nabla \bar f(y)\\ &=\left( T^T \nabla^2 f(x) T \right)^{-1} T^T \nabla f(x)\\ &=T^{-1} \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1}(T^T)^{-1}T^T \nabla f(x)\\ &=T^{-1}\nabla^2 f(x)^{-1}\nabla f(x)\\ &=T^{-1}\Delta x_{\text{nt}}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









      Remark 1. This proposition asserts that an affine change of variable effects the same affine change in Newton steps.
      In particular:

      $latex x+\Delta x_{\text{nt}} = Ty + T\Delta y_{\text{nt}} = T(y + \Delta y_{\text{nt}}). &s=3$

      Thus, if minimizing $latex f&s=3$ via Newton's method gives $latex x^{(k)}&s=3$, then minimizing $latex \bar f &s=3$ via Newton's method with $latex y^{(0)} = T x^{(0)} &s=3$ gives $latex y^{(k)} = T^{-1} x^{(k)}&s=3$.







      Remark 2. The Newton decrement is also invariant under the affine change $latex x=Ty &s=3$.

      $latex \begin{aligned} \bar\lambda(y)^2&= \nabla \bar f(y)^T \nabla^2 \bar f(y)^{-1} \nabla \bar f(y)\\ &= (\nabla f(x)^T T )( T^{-1} \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} T^{-T} ) T^T \nabla f(x)\\ &= \nabla f(x)^T \nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x)\\ &=\lambda(x)^2. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      In particular: the stopping criterion for Newton's method remains the same after an affine change of variables.














    Descent Algorithms for Equality Constrained Minimization
    Overview Equality Constrained Minimization.
    We will focus on equality constrained problems of the form

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    The main assumptions are
    • $latex f &s=3$ is convex.
    • $latex f &s=3$ is twice continuously differentiable: $latex \nabla^2 f &s=3$ is continuous.
    • An optimal solution $latex x^\star \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ exists.
    • $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}&s=3$ with $latex \text{rank}\,A = p < n&s=3$.
    • $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$ is problem data.
    N.B.:
    1. $latex \text{rank}\,A = p &s=3$ indicates equality constraints are independent (not superfluous).
    2. $latex \text{rank}\, A< n &s=3$ indicates there are fewer equality constraints than variables.








    Goal.
    Demonstrate that Newton's method naturally extends to equality constrained problems.
    Will cover:
    • Feasible star equality constrained Newton's method
    • Infeasible star equality constrained Newton's method








    Warm-up Question.
    Suppose we wish to employ Newton's method for

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Suppose $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$ denotes the to-be-determined Newton step.
    Suppose starting point $latex x^{(0)} \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ is feasible: $latex Ax^{(0)} = b&s=3$.
    What necessary assumption on $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$ should we impose so that each step $latex x = x + t \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ remains feasible?














    Equivalent Unconstrained Formulations Main Idea.
    May apply unconstrained optimization to unconstrained optimization problems equivalent to the equality constrained problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Two ways of achieving this are
    1. eliminating equality constraint by the change

      $latex f(x) \mapsto f(Fz+x_0)&s=3$.

    2. formulating the Lagrangian dual problem.
    N.B.: both reformulations of the equality constrained problem may break problem structure (e.g., sparsity) and hence affect numerics.







    Equality Constraint Elimination.
    N.B.: $latex \text{rank}\,A = p < n \implies &s=3$ $latex \dim\ker A = n-p &s=3$.
    Can find $latex F \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times (n-p)} &s=3$ such that

    $latex \text{range}\,F = \text{ker}\,A. &s=3$

    Let $latex x_0 &s=3$ solve $latex Ax_0 = b&s=3$.
    Then

    $latex \{ x \in \mathbb{R}^n : Ax = b \} = \{ Fz + x_0 : z \in \mathbb{R}^{n-p}\}. &s=3$

    Therefore

    $latex \{ f(x) : Ax = b \} = \{ f(Fz + x_0) : z \in \mathbb{R}^{n-p}\}\\ &s=3$

    and so

    $latex \begin{aligned} \inf\{f(x): Ax=b\} = \inf\{ f(Fz + x_0) : z \in \mathbb{R}^{n-p}\}. \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Thus, if $latex z^\star \in \mathbb{R}^{n-p} &s=3$ solves

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(Fz+x_0) \end{cases} &s=3$

    then $latex x^\star = Fz^\star + x_0 &s=3$ solves

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax=b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    May therefore use unconstrained optimization for $latex f(Fz+x_0) &s=3$ to solve original equality constrained problem.
    N.B.: this confirms that minimizing the restriction of a function to an affine subspace is theoretically equivalent to an unconstrained problem.







    Using Lagrangian Dual.
    Since there are no inequality constraints, the Lagrangian of the problem is

    $latex L(x,\nu) = f(x) + \nu^TAx - \nu^T b. &s=3$

    Using Legendre transform $latex f^* &s=3$, the dual function is

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(\nu) &= \inf\{ L(x,\nu) : x\}\\ &= \inf\{f(x) + \nu^TAx - \nu^T b\}\\ &= - \nu^T b - \sup\{ -(A^T \nu)^T x - f(x)\}\\ &=-\nu^T b - f^*(-A^T\nu). \end{aligned} &s=3$









    The dual problem is therefore the unconstrained optimization problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & -\nu^T b - f^*(-A^T\nu). \end{cases} &s=3$

    N.B.: $latex g(\nu)=-\nu^T b - f^*(-A^T\nu) &s=3$ is not a priori twice continuously differentiable, even if $latex f &s=3$ is.
    If $latex g(\nu) &s=3$ is twice continuously differentiable, then can use unconstrained optimization to find dual optimizer $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ and whence primal optimizer $latex x^\star &s=3$. (Assumption that $latex x^\star &s=3$ exists implies problem satisfies Slater's condition since there are no inequality constraints.)














    Quadratic Model Problem Recall:
    1. Recall

      $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^T Q x + q^T x + r, \\ \quad Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_{++}^n, \quad q \in \mathbb{R}^n, \quad r \in \mathbb{R}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      is minimized at

      $latex x^\star = -Q^{-1}q = -\nabla^2 f(x)^{-1} \nabla f(x). &s=3$

      N.B.: This is the Newton's step for this problem.
    2. For general $latex f &s=3$, Newton's method is to solve problem by solving sequence of quadratic approximation problems:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \text{Perform Quad. approx. at }x &\implies \text{Solve Quad. approx. problem for }\Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ &\implies \text{Take Newton step } x \mapsto x + t \Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ & \implies \text{Perform Quad. approx. at } x + t\Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ &\implies \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Idea.
    Develop Newton-type method for equality constrained problems based on minimizing the quadratic model problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^T Qx + q^Tx + r\\ \text{subject to} & Ax=b, \end{cases} &s=3$

    with $latex Q,q,r,A,b&s=3$ as above.
    Then one may follow unconstrained idea:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{Perform Quad. approx. at }x &\implies \text{Solve Quad. approx. problem for }\Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ &\implies \text{Take Newton step } x \mapsto x + t \Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ & \implies \text{Perform Quad. approx. at } x + t\Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ &\implies \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$

    N.B.: the more general case $latex Q \in \boldsymbol{S}_+^n &s=3$ may also be treated.







    The Model.
    For

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & \frac{1}{2}x^T Qx + q^Tx + r\\ \text{subject to} & Ax=b, \end{cases} &s=3$

    the KKT optimality conditions are

    $latex \begin{cases} \begin{aligned} Ax^\star &= b\\ Qx^\star + q + A^T \nu^\star &=0. \end{aligned} \end{cases} &s=3$

    The second equation follows from differentiating the Lagrangian

    $latex L(x,\nu) =\frac{1}{2}x^T Qx + q^Tx + r + \nu^T Ax + \nu^T b. &s=3$

    (N.B.: Slater's condition is satisfied if $latex Ax=b&s=3$ is consistent.)







    The KKT optimality conditions are equivalent to the KKT system:

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x^\star \\ \nu^\star \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -q\\ b \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    KKT matrix:

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    N.B.:
    • If KKT matrix is nonsingular, then problem has unique solution.
    • If KKT matrix is singular, then either $latex Ax=b&s=3$ is inconsistent, or solutions to problem are not unique.








    Solving the KKT System.
    Suppose KKT matrix is nonsingular.
    Then $latex x^\star &s=3$ is obtained by computing

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} x^\star \\ \nu^\star \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix}^{-1} \begin{bmatrix} -q\\ b \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    But this may be costly.
    Another idea: use KKT conditions directly.

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \begin{aligned} Ax^\star &= b\\ Qx^\star + q + A^T \nu^\star &=0. \end{aligned} \end{cases} &\implies x^\star = -Q^{-1}(q + A^T \nu^\star)\\ & \implies b= A x^\star = -AQ^{-1}(q + A^T \nu^\star)\\ &\implies \nu^\star = - (AQ^{-1}A^T)^{-1}(b + AQ^{-1}q) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Now, computing $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ can be used to compute $latex x^\star&s=3$.







    Main Idea.
    Consider now the general problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Given initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f&s=3$ satisfying $latex Ax=b &s=3$, let

    $latex \begin{aligned} F(v) &= f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T v + \frac{1}{2}v^T \nabla^2 f(x) v &\approx f(x+v) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    be the quadratic approximation $latex f&s=3$ at $latex x&s=3$.
    N.B.: if we want to approximate $latex f &s=3$ at $latex x+v &s=3$ with $latex x+v &s=3$ feasible, then need $latex Av=0 &s=3$:

    $latex b= A(x+v) = Ax+Av = b&s=3$.









    Idea: if feasible $latex x&s=3$ near $latex x^\star &s=3$, then $latex F&s=3$ approximates $latex f &s=3$ well and so, if $latex v^\star &s=3$ solves

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & F(v) = f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T v + \frac{1}{2}v^T \nabla^2 f(x) v\\ \text{subject to} & Av = 0, \end{cases} &s=3$

    then $latex x+v^\star &s=3$ approximates $latex x^\star &s=3$ well.
    N.B.: The point of $latex Av^\star = 0 &s=3$ is so that $latex x+v^\star &s=3$ remains feasible, i.e.,

    $latex A(x+v^\star) = Ax + Av^\star = Ax = b&s=3$.

    Viz., $latex v^\star &s=3$ gives the feasible direction which minimizes the quadratic approximation of $latex f &s=3$.







    Linear Equality Constrained Newton's Method Feasible directions relative to equality constraint $latex Ax=b &s=3$ are any directions $latex v \in \ker A &s=3$.
    N.B.: if $latex v &s=3$ is a feasible direction and $latex x &s=3$ is feasible, then $latex x+tv&s=3$ is feasible for all $latex t \in \mathbb{R} &s=3$. (Viz.: line searching does not break feasibility.)







    Consider now the general problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x)\\ \text{subject to} & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Newton step: a direction $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ such that the system

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f(x) & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ w \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -\nabla f(x)\\ 0 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    is consistent for some $latex w \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$.
    N.B.: we only define $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ when the KKT matrix

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f(x) & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    is nonsingular, which always holds when $latex f &s=3$ is strongly convex.







    Remarks.
    1. The system

      $latex \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f(x) & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1\\z_2 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -\nabla f(x)\\ 0 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

      is the KKT system for the quadratic approximation problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f(x) + \nabla f(x)^T v + \frac{1}{2}v^T \nabla^2 f(x) v\\ \text{subject to} & Av = 0. \end{cases} &s=3$

      Thus, finding $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ amounts to minimizing the equality constrained quadratic approximation of the original problem.








    2. $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ is a feasible descent direction.
      Indeed, $latex A\Delta x_{\text{nt}} = 0&s=3$, and

      $latex \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} + A^T w = - \nabla f(x) &s=3$

      imply

      $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} + \Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T A^T w = - \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nt}}, &s=3$

      which implies

      $latex 0>-\Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = \nabla f(x)^T \Delta x_{\text{nt}}. &s=3$









    Newton Decrement.
    The Newton decrement $latex \lambda(x) &s=3$ for the linear constrained Newton step is

    $latex \lambda(x) = \left( \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \right)^{1/2}. &s=3$

    The interpretations for the unconstrained Newton decrement also hold; e.g.,

    $latex f(x) - p^\star \approx \frac{1}{2}\lambda(x)^2 &s=3$ for $latex x \approx x^\star &s=3$.

    In particular: $latex \frac{1}{2}\lambda(x)^2 < \epsilon &s=3$ gives a suitable stopping criterion as before.







    Linear Equality Constrained Newton's Method.
    The Newton's method for the linear equality constrained problem may now be stated.
    One may call the following algorithm a feasible descent method since each iteration demands the update $latex x = x + t \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$ is feasible. 
    
given 
         initial $latex x \in \text{dom}\,f &s=2$ with $latex Ax=b &s=2$
         tolerance $latex \epsilon>0 &s=2$
repeat: 
1. Solve
         $latex
\begin{bmatrix}
\nabla^2 f(x)  & A^T\\
A & 0 
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}
\Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\
w
\end{bmatrix}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
-\nabla f(x)\\
0
\end{bmatrix}
&s=3$
and compute
         $latex \lambda^2 = \Delta x_{\text{nt}}^T \nabla^2 f(x) \Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=2$.
2. Stopping criterion: quit if $latex \frac{1}{2}\lambda^2 \leq \epsilon &s=2$.
3. Perform line search to determine step size $latex t &s=2$.
4. Take step: $latex x = x + t\Delta x_{\text{nt}} &s=3$.
    Interpretation.
    The equality constrained Newton's method amounts to constructing a sequence of equality constrained quadratic minimization problems whose solutions approximate the solution of the original problem.














    Interior Point Methods
    Problem Setup We will focus on general convex optimization problems of the form

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b. \end{cases} &s=3$

    The main assumptions are
    • $latex f_0 &s=3$ is convex.
    • $latex f_0 &s=3$ is twice continuously differentiable: $latex \nabla^2 f_0 &s=3$ is continuous.
    • An optimal solution $latex x^\star \in \text{dom}\,f_0&s=3$ exists.
    • $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}&s=3$ with $latex \text{rank}\,A = p < n&s=3$.
    • $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$ is problem data.















    Review on Lagrange Duality The main idea of Lagrange duality is detailed in the following steps.
    We assume strong duality holds $latex d^\star = p^\star &s=3$.







    1. Build optimization problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ & h_i(x)=0, \quad i=1,\ldots,p \end{cases} &s=3$









    2. Build Lagrangian

      $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu) = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \sum_{i=1}^p \nu_i h_i(x). &s=3$

      and Lagrange dual function

      $latex g(\lambda,\nu) = \inf \{L(x,\lambda,\nu) : x \text{ in domain of problem} \}. &s=3$









    3. Build Lagrange dual problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{maximize} & g(\lambda,\nu)\\ \text{subject to} & \lambda \succeq 0. \end{cases} &s=3$

      4. Recall: if $latex d^\star&s=3$ is the dual optimal value and strong duality holds, then $latex p^\star = d^\star &s=3$.







    4. Theoretically solve Lagrange dual problem for dual optimal $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$, noting primal optimal $latex x^\star &s=3$ minimizes

      $latex x \mapsto L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star). &s=3$

      If

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) \end{cases} &s=3$

      has unique solution $latex x^\sharp &s=3$ that is primal feasible, then primal optimal is $latex x^\star = x^\sharp &s=3$.








    5. Implementation of previous step is predicated on dual problem being simpler to solve and $latex L(x,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ having unique solution.
      In generality, Lagrange duality introduces the KKT optimality conditions

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_i(x^\star) & \leq 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ h_i(x^\star) & = 0 , \quad i=1,\ldots,p\\ \lambda_i^\star f_i(x^\star) &= 0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda^\star &\succeq 0\\ \nabla_x L(x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      For convex problems, these conditions are necessary and sufficient for $latex (x^\star,\lambda^\star,\nu^\star) &s=3$ to be optimal.








    6. In practice: either
      • $latex x^\star&s=3$ is found by directly integrating KKT system or
      • $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ found first, back substituted, then $latex x^\star &s=3$ is found.
      N.B.: which route is taken or how $latex (\lambda^\star,\nu^\star)&s=3$ is determined is dictated by problem structure (theoretically or numerically).















    Problem Hierarchy and Outline

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{ECQP}:&\\ \text{minimize}& \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + q^T x + r\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \boldsymbol{\subset} \begin{cases} \text{NCOP}:&\\ \text{minimize}& f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \boldsymbol{\subset} \begin{cases} \text{ICOP}:&\\ \text{minimize}& f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& f_i(x)\leq0\\ &Ax=b \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    ECQP:
    Solved via solving KKT system

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} Q & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x^\star \\ \nu^\star \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -q\\ b \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    Either solved directly or one appeals to the dual:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} Qx^\star + A^T \nu^\star & = -q\\ A x^\star &= b \end{aligned} &\implies x^\star = -Q^{-1}(q+A^T \nu^\star)\\ &\implies -AQ^{-1}(q+A^T \nu^\star)=b\\ &\implies \nu^\star =-(AQ^{-1}A^T)^{-1}(b+AQ^{-1}q) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Solving for $latex \nu^\star &s=3$ allows one to compute $latex x^\star&s=3$.







    NCOP:
    Solved via sequence of approximating ECQP's: at each iteration, approximate $latex f_0(x) &s=3$ via

    $latex F(v) = \frac{1}{2}v^T \nabla^2 f_0(x) v + \nabla f_0(x)^T v + f_0(x) &s=3$

    and solve the ECQP

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& \frac{1}{2}v^T \nabla^2 f_0(x) v + \nabla f_0(x)^T v + f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to}& Av=0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    for the Newton step $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = v^\star &s=3$.







    This is equivalent to solving the KKT system

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f_0(x) & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta x_{\text{nt}} \\ w \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -\nabla f_0(x)\\ 0 \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    From above: we may first solve

    $latex w =-(A\nabla^2 f_0(x)^{-1} A^T)^{-1}(A\nabla^2 f_0(x)^{-1} \nabla f_0(x)) &s=3$

    and back substitute to obtain

    $latex \Delta x_{\text{nt}} = -\nabla^2 f_0(x)^{-1}(\nabla f_0(x)+A^T w) &s=3$

    N.B.: $latex A=0&s=3$ recovers unconstrained Newton step.







    ICOP:
    The main idea is to create a sequence of NCOP's whose solutions approximate the solution to the ICOP.
    A first approximation: let

    $latex I_{-}(u) = \begin{cases} 0 & u \leq 0\\ +\infty & u>0. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Then the ICOP is equivalent to the equality constrained convex optimization problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m I_{-}(f_i(x))\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

    N.B.: This new objective function need not be smooth and therefore Newton's need not apply in general.







    Plan:
    Devise an approximation scheme of this nonsmooth equality constrained convex optimization problem.
    This is achieved via solving a sequence of NCOP's whose solutions converge to a solution to the ICOP.














    Logarithmic Barriers Logarithmic Approximation of Indicator Function:
    The indicator function

    $latex I_{-}(u) = \begin{cases} 0 & u \leq 0\\ +\infty & u>0. \end{cases} &s=3$

    is smoothly approximated by the logarithm

    $latex \hat{I}_{-}(u) = \begin{cases} -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u) & u \leq 0\\ +\infty & u>0, \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex t>0 &s=3$ is a parameter dictating the accuracy of approximation.







    The samples $latex t=1,2,4,8,16&s=3$ (solid curves) and $latex I_-&s=3$ (dashed curve) are plotted below.







    N.B.: recall

    $latex s \to 0 \implies c^{s}\to 1&s=3$ for $latex c>0 &s=3$.

    Thus, for large $latex t>0 &s=3$ and fixed $latex u<0 &s=3$, we have

    $latex -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u) = - \log \left((-u)^{1/t} \right)\approx 0 &s=3$

    and for negative $latex u \approx 0 &s=3$ and fixed $latex t&s=3$, we have

    $latex -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u) \approx \infty. &s=3$

    Therefore, $latex -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u)&s=3$ approximates $latex I_-(u)&s=3$ well for large $latex t&s=3$.







    The Logarithmic Barriers:
    With the equivalence

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} \iff \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \sum I_{-}(f_i(x))\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and approximation

    $latex I_-(u) \approx \hat{I}_-(u) := -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u) &s=3$

    we introduce the following approximating logarithmic barrier problems:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} &\iff \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \sum I_{-}(f_i(x))\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \\ &\quad\approx \text{(LBP)} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) -\frac{1}{t} \sum \log(-f_i(x))\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$









    Remarks:
      • The approximating term

        $latex \phi(x):=-\sum_{i=1}^m \log(-f_i(x)) &s=3$

        is called a logarithmic barrier for the problem.
      • N.B.: $latex - \log(-u) >0 &s=3$ for $latex 0<-u<1&s=3$ and so $latex \phi(x) &s=3$ penalizes $latex x&s=3$ closer to boundary of feasible set.
      • The $latex t &s=3$ in $latex f_0(x) + \frac{1}{t}\phi(x)&s=3$ controls this penalty since

        $latex t \approx \infty \implies -\frac{1}{t}\log(-u) \approx 0 &s=3$

        for each fixed $latex u<0&s=3$.








      • If $latex f_0,f_i&s=3$ are twice continuously differentiable, then so is $latex \phi&s=3$ on its domain

        $latex \text{dom}\,\phi = \{ x : f_i(x) < 0,\, i =1,\ldots,m\}. &s=3$

      • Moreover, $latex \psi(x) &s=3$ convex implies $latex -\log \psi(x) &s=3$ is convex, and so $latex \phi &s=3$ is also convex.
      • Therefore, the approximating LBP's are convex and Newton's method is applicable for each $latex t>0 &s=3$.
        ("Applicable" means "can be ran" and not "will necessarily converge".)








    3. Thus each LBP is a NCOP for each $latex t>0 &s=3$.
      Moreover,

      $latex f_0 + \frac{1}{t}\phi \to f_0 + \sum_{i=1}^m I_-(f_i(x))\text{ as } t \to \infty. &s=3$

      This suggests the approximation scheme: for each $latex t>0 &s=3$, use Newton's method to solve the approximating LBP

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \frac{1}{t} \phi(x) \\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

      for primal optimal $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$, and prove $latex x^\star(t) \to x^\star &s=3$ as $latex t \to \infty &s=3$.








    4. Therefore, informally speaking, we have

      $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \frac{1}{t}\phi(x)\\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \to \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} \end{aligned} &s=3$

      as $latex t\to \infty &s=3$.







    5. For sake of notational convenience: there holds the equivalence

      $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& f_0(x) + \frac{1}{t} \phi(x) \\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} \iff \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& tf_0(x) + \phi(x) \\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      In fact, both problems have the same primal optimizers.







    6. To use the KKT conditions for the approximating LBP's, we record the gradient and Hessian of a general logarithmic barrier $latex \phi(x)&s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla \phi(x) &= -\nabla \left( \sum_{i=1}^m \log(-f_i (x)) \right) \\ &=- \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{f_i(x)} \nabla f_i(x)\\ \nabla^2 \phi(x) &= \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{f_i(x)^2} \nabla f_i (x) \nabla f_i(x)^T - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{f_i(x)} \nabla^2 f_i(x). \end{aligned} &s=3$

      N.B.: $latex \nabla f_i (x) \nabla f_i(x)^T &s=3$ is an $latex n \times n &s=3$ matrix.















    Central Path We will always suppose that, for each $latex t>0 &s=3$, the LBP

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& tf_0(x) + \phi(x) \\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

    has a unique solution $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$.
    We will also call this problem a centering problem.
    For concreteness, we will assume this problem is uniquely solvable for each $latex t>0 &s=3$ via Newton's method.







    The $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ form the central path of

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

    which is a path in the feasible set $latex F &s=3$ of this problem.
    Each $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is called a central point.







    An example of a central path is depicted below. Each point along the curve indicates a solution to an approximating centering problem.
    Intuitively, these solutions should converge to the solution of the original problem.















    Therefore, since the problem is convex: for each $latex t>0 &s=3$, a point $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is central iff the following hold:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{strict feasibility}& \begin{cases} Ax^\star(t) = b\\ f_i(x^\star(t)) < 0 \end{cases}\\ \text{KKT}& \begin{cases} 0&=t \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) + \nabla \phi(x^\star(t)) + A^T \nu_t\\ &= t \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{f_i(x^\star(t))} \nabla f_i(x^\star(t)) + A^T \nu_t \end{cases}\\ \end{aligned} &s=3$

    for some $latex \nu_t \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$.
    N.B.: strict feasibility follows from $latex f_i(x) = 0 \implies \phi(x) = \infty&s=3$.














    Convergence and Dual Central Path Objectives:
