    1. show $latex x^\star(t)&s=3$ naturally determines a path of dual feasible points $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$.
    2. show that $latex x^\star(t) \to x^\star &s=3$ and $latex f_0(x^\star(t)) \to p^\star &s=3$ as $latex t \to \infty&s=3$.
    Emphasis: the dual central path $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ can serve as certificates that $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is suboptimal with desired tolerance: $latex f_0(x^\star(t)) - p^\star < \epsilon &s=3$.
    In short: the objectives follow from Lagrange duality and the KKT conditions for the approximating LBP's.







    Theorem. Let $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ be the central path. Then

    $latex f_0(x^\star(t)) - p^\star \leq \frac{m}{t} &s=3$

    and there exists $latex \nu_t \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$ such that

    $latex \lambda^\star_i(t) := -\frac{1}{t f_i(x^\star(t))},\quad \nu^\star(t) := \frac{1}{t}\nu_t &s=3$

    form a path $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ of dual feasible points.







    Remarks.
    1. Recall: $latex m &s=3$ denotes the number of inequality constraints of the original problem.
    2. The estimate $latex f_0(x^\star(t)) - p^\star \leq \frac{m}{t} &s=3$ implies

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_0(x^\star(t)) &\to p^\star\\ x^\star(t) & \to x^\star. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Moreover, it determines exactly which $latex t>0&s=3$ ensures $latex \epsilon &s=3$-suboptimality:

      $latex \frac{m}{\epsilon}\leq t \implies f_0(x^\star(t)) - p^\star \leq \epsilon. &s=3$

    3. Recall: a pair $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ is called dual feasible if $latex \lambda \succeq 0 &s=3$ and $latex g(\lambda,\nu)>-\infty&s=3$, where $latex g&s=3$ is the Lagrange dual function of the original problem.








    Proof.
    Outline
    1. Determine KKT conditions for approximating centering problems; this will determine $latex \nu^\star(t)&s=3$.
    2. Compare with Lagrangian for original problem; this will determine $latex \lambda^\star(t)&s=3$.
    3. Show $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ is dual feasible.
    4. Use Lagrange dual function and duality gap to conclude suboptimality estimate.








    1. For each $latex t>0&s=3$, the Lagrangian for the approximating centering problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize}& tf_0(x) + \phi(x) \\ \text{subject to}& Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

      is

      $latex L_t(x,\lambda) = tf_0(x) + \phi(x) + \nu^T Ax - \nu^T b. &s=3$

      Thus, the KKT conditions are

      $latex \begin{cases} Ax^\star(t)=b\\ t \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) + \nabla \phi(x^\star(t)) + A^T \nu_t = 0, \end{cases} &s=3$

      for some $latex \nu_t \in \mathbb{R}^p &s=3$.
      Set

      $latex \nu^\star(t) := \frac{1}{t}\nu_t. &s=3$

      (Viz.: $latex \nu^\star(t)&s=3$ is a scaled dual optimal Lagrange multiplier for the approximating centering problem at time $latex t&s=3$.)








    2. The Lagrangian for the original problem

      $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & f_0(x)\\ \text{subject to} & f_i(x) \leq 0, \quad i = 1,\ldots,m\\ & Ax = b \end{cases} &s=3$

      is

      $latex \begin{aligned} L(x,\lambda,\nu) = f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i f_i(x) + \nu^T Ax - \nu^T b, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      whose gradient is

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x,\lambda,\nu) = \nabla f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      We show that $latex L(x,\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ is minimized at $latex x^\star(t)&s=3$ for suitably chosen $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$, which will be given by KKT conditions of the approximating centering problems.















      Compare the gradient of $latex L&s=3$ at $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ and the KKT conditions for the approximating centering problems:

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x^\star(t),\lambda,\nu) &= \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla f_i(x^\star(t)) + A^T \nu \\ \frac{1}{t}\nabla_x L_t(x,\nu) & = \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{tf_i(x^\star(t))} \nabla f_i(x^\star(t)) + A^T \frac{1}{t}\nu_t\\ &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

      Matching coefficients, we see choosing

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i &= - \frac{1}{t f_i(x^\star(t))}, \qquad \nu = \frac{1}{t}\nu_t. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      effects

      $latex \nabla L(x^\star(t),\lambda,\nu) = 0. &s=3$









      Indeed, if

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda_i^\star(t) &= - \frac{1}{t f_i(x^\star(t))}, \qquad \nu^\star(t) = \frac{1}{t}\nu_t, \end{aligned} &s=3$

      then

      $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla_x L(x^\star(t),\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)) &= \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star(t) \nabla f_i(x^\star(t)) + A^T \nu^\star(t) \\ &= \nabla f_0(x^\star(t)) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{tf_i(x^\star(t))} \nabla f_i(x^\star(t)) + A^T \frac{1}{t}\nu_t\\ &= \frac{1}{t}\nabla_xL_t(x^\star(t),\nu_t) \\ &=0 \end{aligned} &s=3$









    3. We now show $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ is dual feasible, i.e., that

      $latex \begin{aligned} \lambda^\star(t) & \succeq 0\\ g(\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&> -\infty. \end{aligned} &s=3$

      First observe:

      $latex \begin{aligned} f_i (x^\star(t)) < 0 & \implies \lambda_i^\star (t) = - \frac{1}{t f_i(x^\star(t))} >0\\ &\implies \lambda^\star(t) \succeq 0. \end{aligned} &s=3$









      4. Secondly, observe that $latex x\mapsto L(x,\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ is convex and so, since $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is a critical point of this function, it is a minimizer.
      Consequently, the dual function $latex g(\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ is finite at $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$:

      $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)) &= \inf\{L(x,\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)): x\text{ feasible}\}\\ &= f_0(x^\star(t)) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i^\star(t)f_i(x^\star(t)) + \nu^{\star}(t)^T(Ax^\star(t)-b)\\ &= f_0(x^\star(t)) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{tf_i(x^\star(t))}f_i(x^\star(t)) + \frac{1}{t}\nu_t \cdot 0\\ &= f_0(x^\star(t)) - \frac{m}{t}\\ &>-\infty \end{aligned} &s=3$

















    4. Lastly, we observe that the evaluation of $latex g(\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)) &s=3$ paired with the optimality gap gives:

      $latex \begin{aligned} g(\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)) = &f_0(x^\star(t)) - \frac{m}{t}\\ &\implies f_0(x^\star(t)) - g(\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t)) = \frac{m}{t}\\ &\implies f_0(x^\star(t)) - \sup g(\lambda,\nu) \leq \frac{m}{t}\\ &\implies f_0(x^\star(t)) - p^\star \leq \frac{m}{t}\\ \end{aligned} &s=3$
















    The Barrier Method We are now ready to write out the algorithm which uses logarithmic barriers and centering problems to approximate the solution to an inequality constrained convex optimization problem.

    This algorithm is called the barrier method; aka sequential unconstrained minimization technique (SUMT) by Fiacco-McCormick or path-following method. 
    
given 
         initial strictly feasible $latex x &s=2$
         initial time $latex t > 0 &s=2$
         multiplier $latex \mu>1 &s=2$
         tolerance $latex \epsilon>0 &s=2$
repeat: 
1. Centering step. Find center $latex x^\star(t) &s=2$ by solving
         $latex
\begin{cases}
\text{minimize} & tf_0+\phi\\
\text{subject to} & Ax=b
\end{cases}
&s=2$
   with initial point $latex x&s=2$.
2. Update. $latex x:= x^\star(t) &s=2$.
3. Stopping criterion. quit if $latex \frac{m}{\epsilon}<t &s=2$.
4. Increase $latex t&s=2$. $latex t:=\mu t&s=2$.








    Remarks.
    1. The iterations in Newton's method to solve centering problem are called inner iterations.
      The execution of Step 1. is called an outer iteration.







    2. Size of $latex \mu&s=3$ dictates trade-off between number of inner and outer iterations:
      • $latex \mu &s=3$ small: $latex \mu \approx 1 \implies t \approx \mu t \implies x^\star(t) \approx x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$.
        Thus,
        • $latex x^\star(t)&s=3$ is good initial point to compute $latex x^\star(\mu t) &s=3$
          $latex \implies &s=3$ few Newton steps to move from $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ to $latex x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$.
          $latex \implies &s=3$ few inner iterations per outer iteration.
        • However: $latex x^\star(t) \approx x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$
          $latex \implies &s=3$ algorithm moves along central path slowly
          $latex \implies &s=3$ many outer iterations.
        • Newton steps follow along central path quite well.








      • $latex \mu &s=3$ large: $latex \mu \gg 1 \implies \mu t \gg t \implies x^\star(t) &s=3$ and $latex x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$ quite separated.
        Thus,
        • $latex x^\star(t)&s=3$ poor initial point to compute $latex x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$
          $latex \implies&s=3$ many Newton steps to move from $latex x^\star(t)&s=3$ to $latex x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$
          $latex \implies &s=3$ many inner iterations.
        • However: large separation of $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ and $latex x^\star(\mu t)&s=3$
          $latex \implies &s=3$ algorithm moves along central path quickly
          $latex \implies &s=3$ few outer iterations.
        • Newton steps may diverge far from central path.








    3. Size of $latex t &s=3$ also affects number of inner and outer iterations:
      • The larger $latex t &s=3$ is:
        • the faster the algorithm moves along central path
          $latex \implies &s=3$ the fewer outer iterations needed
        • the closer $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is to $latex x^\star &s=3$
        • However: if initial $latex x&s=3$ far from $latex x^\star &s=3$, then may require many initial inner iterations.
        • (These observations apply to aggressive choice of $latex t = m/\epsilon&s=3$, where algorithm requires one outer iteration.)








      • The smaller $latex t&s=3$ is:
        • the slower the algorithm moves along central path
          $latex \implies &s=3$ the more outer iterations needed
        • the farther $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ is to $latex x^\star &s=3$
        • Moreover: if initial $latex x&s=3$ far from $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ and near $latex x^\star &s=3$, then may require many superfluous initial inner iterations.















    Modified KKT Conditions Recall:
    • the KKT optimality conditions for centering problem at time $latex t&s=3$ are

      $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ t\nabla f_0(x) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{f_i(x)} \nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0. \end{cases} &s=3$

    • Central path $latex x^\star(t) &s=3$ satisfies strict feasibility

      $latex f_i(x^\star(t)) < 0, \quad t = 1,\ldots,m &s=3$

      and defines dual central path $latex (\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$ with

      $latex \lambda^\star (t) = - \frac{1}{t f_i(x^\star(t))}. &s=3$









    Combining the two: $latex x&s=3$ is a central point iff there is a pair $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ satisfying the modified KKT conditions:

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x) < 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda > 0\\ -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}\\ \nabla f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    Main Point.
    Certain interior point methods amount to iteratively solving this system.







    Remarks.
    1. modified KKT conditions are a "continuous" deformation of KKT conditions for original problem.
      Evidently: as $latex t \to \infty &s=3$, the modified KKT conditions converge to the original KKT conditions.
    2. Complementary slackness

      $latex \lambda_i f_i(x) = 0 &s=3$

      is now "almost" complementary slackness

      $latex -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}\\ &s=3$
















    Newton Step for Centering Problem Recall: first step in the barrier method is solving the centering problem

    $latex \begin{cases} \text{minimize} & tf_0+\phi\\ \text{subject to} & Ax=b \end{cases} &s=3$

    where $latex \phi &s=3$ is a log barrier.
    Solving the centering problem using Newton's method amounts to iteratively solving the KKT systems

    $latex \begin{bmatrix} t\nabla^2 f_0(x) + \nabla^2 \phi(x) & A^T\\ A & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta x_{\text{nt}}\\ \nu_{\text{nt}} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -t\nabla f_0(x) - \nabla \phi(x)\\ 0 \end{bmatrix} &s=3$

    Remark.
    Solving this system turns out to be directly related to solving the modified KKT equations, which is generally a nonlinear system.







    To achieve this, first we form

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x) < 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ \nabla f_0(x) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{t f_i(x)}\nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    from the modified KKT conditions

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x) < 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda > 0\\ -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}\\ \nabla f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    by performing the elimination

    $latex -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}. &s=3$









    Then finding the Newton step to solve centering problem is equivalent to finding the Newton step to solve the nonlinear system

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x) < 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ \nabla f_0(x) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{t f_i(x)}\nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0. \end{cases} &s=3$

    Recall (Newton's Method for Nonlinear Equations) One approach to solving a nonlinear system of equations

    $latex \begin{aligned} F&:\mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\\ F(X) &= (F_1(X),\ldots,F_k(X)) = 0 \end{aligned} &s=3$

    is through Newton's method.








    Here, one considers the iteration scheme

    $latex \begin{aligned} X^{(k+1)} &= X^{(k)} + \Delta X_{\text{nt}}\\ \Delta X_{\text{nt}} &= - J_F^{-1}(X^{(k)})F(X^{(k)})\\ J_F(X)&= \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial X_1} & \cdots & \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial X_k}\\ \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ \frac{\partial F_k}{\partial X_1} & \cdots & \frac{\partial F_k}{\partial X_k} \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    We call $latex \Delta X_{\text{nt}}&s=3$ the Newton step for solving the nonlinear system $latex F(X)=0&s=3$.
    Under suitable circumstances, if $latex X^\star &s=3$ solves $latex F(X)=0&s=3$, and $latex X^{(0)}&s=3$ near $Latex X^\star &s=3$, then $latex X^{(k)} \to X^\star &s=3$.















    Primal-Dual Interior Point Method Recall: Newton's method for the inner iterations to solve the centering problems is equivalent to using Newton's method to solve the nonlinear modified KKT conditions

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x)<0, \quad i =1,\ldots,m\\ \nabla f_0(x) - \sum_{i=1}^m \frac{1}{t f_i(x)}\nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    after performing the elimination

    $latex -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}. &s=3$









    If we instead use Newton's method to solve the full modified KKT conditions

    $latex \begin{cases} Ax = b\\ f_i(x) < 0, \quad i=1,\ldots,m\\ \lambda > 0\\ -\lambda_i f_i(x) = \frac{1}{t}\\ \nabla f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla f_i(x) + A^T \nu = 0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    we develop a new algorithm which is an example of a "primal-dual interior point method".







    Main Features.
    1. There are no inner iterations.
    2. Both primal and dual variables are updated each iteration.
    3. Primal and dual iterates need not be feasible.
    4. often outperforms barrier method.








    Primal-dual search direction.
    Define

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) = \begin{bmatrix} f_1(x)\\\vdots\\f_m(x) \end{bmatrix} ,\qquad Df(x)= \begin{bmatrix} \nabla f_1(x)^T\\\vdots\\\nabla f_m(x)^T \end{bmatrix}\\ \text{diag}(\lambda) = \lambda \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 &\cdots & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ 0 & 0 &\cdots & 1 \end{bmatrix} ,\qquad \boldsymbol{1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1\\\vdots\\1\end{bmatrix}\\ r_t(x,\lambda,\nu) = \begin{bmatrix}\nabla f_0(x) + Df(x)^T\lambda + A^T\nu\\ -\text{diag}(\lambda)f(x) - \frac{1}{t}\boldsymbol{1}\\ Ax-b \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$









    N.B.:

    $latex \begin{cases} r_t(x,\lambda,\nu) =0\\ f_i(x)<0 \end{cases} &s=3$

    are exactly the modified KKT conditions.
    Thus, if $latex (x,\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ solves this system, then

    $latex (x,\lambda,\nu) = (x^\star(t),\lambda^\star(t),\nu^\star(t))&s=3$.









    Define the following residuals

    $latex \begin{aligned} \text{dual residual} &= r_{\text{dual}} = \nabla f_0(x) + Df(x)^T \lambda + A^T \nu\\ \text{centrality residual} &= r_{\text{cent}} = -\text{diag}(\lambda)f(x) - \frac{1}{t}\boldsymbol{1}\\ \text{primal residual} &= r_{\text{pri}} = Ax-b. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Remarks.
    1. These residuals are just the blocks of $latex r_t &s=3$:

      $latex r_t = \begin{bmatrix} r_{\text{dual}} \\ r_{\text{cent}} \\ r_{\text{pri}} \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$

    2. $latex r_{\text{dual}}(x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ measures divergence from $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ from being dual feasible.
    3. $latex r_{\text{pri}}(x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ measures divergence from $latex x&s=3$ being primal feasible.
    4. $latex r_{\text{cent}}(x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ measures divergence from $latex x&s=3$ and $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ from being central.
    5. All three $latex \approx 0 &s=3$ means $latex (x,\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ nearly solves modified KKT condition.








    Using Newton's Method.
    Applying Newton's method to solve the nonlinear system

    $latex r_t(x,\lambda,\nu) = 0 &s=3$

    at a point $latex y = (x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ satisfying $latex f(x) \prec 0 \prec \lambda &s=3$ results in a Newton step

    $latex \Delta y = (\Delta x, \Delta \lambda, \Delta \nu) &s=3$

    given by

    $latex \Delta y = -J_{r_t}(y)^{-1}r_t(y), &s=3$

    where

    $latex J_{r_t}(y)= Dr_t(y) = \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla^2 f_i(x) & Df(x)^T & A^T\\ -\text{diag}(\lambda)Df(x) & -\text{diag}(f(x)) & 0\\ A & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}. &s=3$









    Viz.: the Newton step solves

    $latex \begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \nabla^2 f_0(x) + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i \nabla^2 f_i(x) & Df(x)^T & A^T\\ -\text{diag}(\lambda)Df(x) & -\text{diag}(f(x)) & 0\\ A & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta x\\ \Delta \lambda \\ \Delta \nu \end{bmatrix} = - \begin{bmatrix} r_{\text{dual}} \\ r_{\text{cent}} \\ r_{\text{pri}} \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    The solution to this system is called the primal-dual search direction and will be denoted by

    $latex \Delta y_{\text{pd}} = (\Delta x_{\text{pd}},\Delta \lambda_{\text{pd}},\Delta \nu_{\text{pd}}) &s=3$









    Surrogate Duality Gap.
    The primal-dual search directions $latex \Delta y_{\text{pd}}&s=3$ need not form a sequence $latex (x^{(k)},\lambda^{(k)},\nu^{(k)}) &s=3$ of feasible points.
    Therefore, $latex f_0(x^{(k)}) - g(\lambda^{(k)},\nu^{(k)})&s=3$ need not measure the duality gap.

    In place of duality gap, we use the surrogate duality gap: for $latex (x,\lambda) &s=3$ satisfying $latex f(x) \prec 0 \prec \lambda &s=3$, the number

    $latex \hat\eta(x,\lambda) = - f(x)^T \lambda. &s=3$









    N.B.: if $latex x &s=3$ feasible and $latex (\lambda,\nu)&s=3$ dual feasible, then

    $latex \begin{aligned} \hat\eta(x,\lambda) &= -f(x)^T \lambda\\ &= -f_1(x)\lambda_1 - \cdots - f_m(x) \lambda_m\\ &= \frac{1}{t} + \cdots + \frac{1}{t}\\ &= \frac{m}{t}\\ &= f_0(x) - g(\lambda,\nu) \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Viz.: $latex r_{\text{pri}} =0, \, r_{\text{dual}} = 0 \implies &s=3$ $latex \hat\eta(x,\lambda,\nu) &s=3$ is duality gap.
    Therefore, $latex r_{\text{pri}},r_{\text{dual}},\hat\eta&s=3$ can be used to define stopping criterion.
    Indeed: $latex \Vert r_{\text{pri}} \Vert_2 &s=3$ and $latex \Vert r_{\text{dual}} \Vert_2 &s=3$ small $latex \implies &s=3$ $latex \hat\eta &s=3$ nearly duality gap.
    Therefore, all three quantities small $latex \implies &s=3$ small duality gap.







    Primal-dual interior point method.
    We may now introduce the algorithm called primal-dual interior point method. 
    
given 
         initial $latex x &s=2$ satisfying $latex f_1(x)< 0,\ldots, f_m(x) < 0 &s=2$
         initial $latex \lambda \succ 0 &s=2$ and $latex \nu &s=2$
         multiplier $latex \mu>1 &s=2$
         tolerances $latex \epsilon>0, \epsilon_{\text{feas}}>0 &s=2$
repeat: 
1. Determine $latex t &s=2$. Set $latex t = \mu m / \hat\eta &s=2$
2. Compute primal-dual search direction $latex \Delta y_{\text{pd}}&s=2$
3. Perform line search and update. 
         Determine step length $latex s>0 &s=2$ and set $latex y = y+s\Delta y_{\text{pd}} &s=2$.
until: $latex \Vert r_{\text{pri}} \Vert_2 \leq \epsilon_{\text{feas}} &s=2$, $latex \Vert r_{\text{dual}} \Vert_2 \leq \epsilon_{\text{feas}} &s=2$ and $latex \hat\eta \leq \epsilon &s=2$.
    N.B.:
    1. the line search is a modified back-tracking line search which ensures $latex f(x) \prec 0 \prec \lambda &s=3$ always holds.
    2. for feasible parameters, the value $latex t= m/\hat\eta&s=3$ corresponds to a duality gap of $latex \hat\eta&s=3$.















    Appendix
    Differentiating $latex b^Tx &s=3$ Given

    $latex b = \begin{bmatrix}b_1\\b_2\\\vdots\\b_n\end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^n &s=3$,

    define the scalar function

    $latex g(x) = b^Tx = b_1x_1 + \cdots + b_n x_n &s=3$.

    Using the Taylor expansion

    $latex \begin{aligned} g(y) = g(x) + (y-x)^T \nabla g(x) + \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$

    at $latex y = 0&s=3$, we find

    $latex \begin{aligned} 0 = b^Tx - x^T \nabla g(x) + \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$

    and so

    $latex x^T \nabla g(x) = b^T. &s=3$

    To see this computed directly, computing

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k} g(x) &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k}(b_1x_1 + \cdots + b_nx_n)\\ &=b_k, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we conclude

    $latex \nabla g = \begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_1} g(x) \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_2}g(x) \\ \vdots \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_n}g(x) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} b_1 \\ b_2 \\ \vdots \\ b_n \end{bmatrix} = b. &s=3$

    Differentiating $latex \frac{1}{2}x^TQx &s=3$ Given

    $latex Q = \begin{bmatrix} q_{11} & q_{12} & \cdots & q_{1n}\\ q_{21} & q_{22} & \cdots & q_{2n}\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ q_{n1} & q_{n2} & \cdots & q_{nn} \end{bmatrix} \in \boldsymbol{S}^n &s=3$

    define the scalar function

    $latex f(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^T Q x. &s=3$

    Using the Taylor expansion

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(y) = f(x) + (y-x)^T \nabla f(x) + \frac{1}{2} (y-x)^T \nabla^2 f(x) (y-x) + \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$

    at $latex y = 0 &s=3$, we find

    $latex \begin{aligned} 0 &= \frac{1}{2}x^TQx - x^T \nabla f(x) + \frac{1}{2} x^T \nabla^2 f(x)x + \cdots\\ &= x^TQx - x^T \nabla f(x) - \frac{1}{2}x^TQx + \frac{1}{2}x^T\nabla^2 f(x) x + \cdots, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which is evidently satisfied in case

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x) &= Qx\\ \nabla^2 f(x) &= Q. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    To see this computed directly: expanding the matrix multiplication, we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x) = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i,j=1}^{n} x_i x_j q_{ij} \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    Computing

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k} f(x) &= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^n \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k} (x_i x_j q_{ij})\\ &=\frac{1}{2} \left(\sum_{i=1}^n x_i q_{ik} + \sum_{j=1}^n x_j q_{kj} \right)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i(q_{ik}+q_{ki})\\ &=\sum_{i=1}^n x_iq_{ik} \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we have

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x) &= \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_1} f(x)\\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_2} f(x)\\ \vdots\\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_n} f(x) \end{bmatrix} =Qx \end{aligned} &s=3$.

    Taking the second derivative gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_k \partial x_l} f(x) &= \sum_{i=1}^n\frac{\partial}{\partial x_l}x_i q_{ik}\\ &=q_{lk}. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Consequently, there holds

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2 f(x) &= \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1^2}(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1 \partial x_2}(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1 \partial x_n}(x) \\ \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2 \partial x_1 }(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2^2 }(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_2 \partial x_n}(x) \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\ \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n \partial x_1}(x) & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n \partial x_2}(x) & \cdots & \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n^2}(x) \\ \end{bmatrix}\\ & = Q. \end{aligned} &s=2$

    Example. Let

    $latex \begin{aligned} f(x_1,x_2) &= \frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}x_1 & x_2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} a&b\\c&d \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}x_1\\x_2 \end{bmatrix}\\ &=\frac{1}{2} \begin{bmatrix}x_1&x_2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}ax_1+bx_2\\cx_1+dx_2\end{bmatrix}\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(ax_1^2 + (b+c)x_1x_2 + dx_2^2). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_1} f(x_1,x_2) &= \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_1} (ax_1^2 + (b+c)x_1x_2 + dx_2^2)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(2ax_1 + (b+c)x_2)\\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_2} f(x_1,x_2) &= \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_2} (ax_1^2 + (b+c)x_1x_2 + dx_2^2)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}((b+c)x_1 + 2dx_2)\\ \end{aligned}. &s=3$

    Consequently,

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla f(x) &= \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_1} f(x) \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_2} f(x) \end{bmatrix}\\ &=\begin{bmatrix} \frac{1}{2}(2ax_1 + (b+c)x_2)\\ \frac{1}{2}((b+c)x_1 + 2dx_2) \end{bmatrix}\\ &=\frac{1}{2} \left(\begin{bmatrix}ax_1 + bx_2 \\ cx_1 + dx_2 \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} ax_1 + cx_2 \\ bx_1 + dx_2 \end{bmatrix} \right)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}\left( \begin{bmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_1\\x_2\end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix}a&c\\b&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_1\\x_2\end{bmatrix}\right)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(Q+Q^T)x. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Next compute the second derivatives:

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_1^2} f(x_1,x_2) &=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_1}(2ax_1 + (b+c)x_2)\\ &=a\\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_1 \partial x_2} f(x_1,x_2) &=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_2}(2ax_1 + (b+c)x_2)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(b+c)\\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_2 \partial x_1} f(x_1,x_2) &= \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_1}((b+c)x_1 + 2dx_2)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(b+c)\\ &=\frac{1}{2}(b+c)\\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_2^2} f(x_1,x_2) &= \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_2}((b+c)x_1 + 2dx_2)\\ &=d. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Putting this together gives

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla^2 f(x) &= \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_1^2}f & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_1 \partial x_2}f\\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_2 \partial x_1}f & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_2^2}f \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix} a & b+c\\ c+b & d \end{bmatrix}\\ &= \frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix} a & b\\ c& d \end{bmatrix} + \frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}a&c\\b&d\end{bmatrix}\\ &= \frac{1}{2}(Q+Q^T). \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Differentiating $latex b^TAx &s=3$ Given $latex A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} &s=3$, $latex b \in \mathbb{R}^m &s=3$, define the scalar function $latex h(x) = b^TAx. &s=3$
    Using the Taylor expansion

    $latex h(y) = h(x) + (y-x)^T\nabla h(x) + \cdots &s=3$

    at $latex y = 0&s=3$, we get

    $latex \begin{aligned} 0 &= b^TAx - x^T \nabla h(x) + \cdots\\ &= x^T(Ab^T) - x^T \nabla h(x) + \cdots \end{aligned} &s=3$

    which directly implies

    $latex \nabla h(x) = Ab^T &s=3$.

    To see this computed directly, first compute

    $latex \begin{aligned} b^TAx &= \begin{bmatrix}b_1& \cdots & b_m \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} a_1^T\\ \vdots \\ a_m^T \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}x_1\\\vdots\\x_n\end{bmatrix}\\ &=\begin{bmatrix}b_1& \cdots & b_m \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} a_1^Tx\\\vdots\\ a_m^T x\end{bmatrix}\\ &= b_1a_1^Tx + \cdots + b_ma_m^Tx. \end{aligned} &s=3$

    Computing

    $latex \begin{aligned} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k} b^TAx &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x_k}(b_1a_1^Tx + \cdots + b_ma_m^Tx)\\ &= b_1 a_{1k} + \cdots + b_m a_{mk}, \end{aligned} &s=3$

    we conclude

    $latex \begin{aligned} \nabla b^TAx &= \begin{bmatrix} b_1 a_{1k} + \cdots + b_m a_{mk}\\ \vdots\\ b_1 a_{1m} + \cdots + b_m a_{mm} \end{bmatrix}\\ &= A^Tb. \end{aligned} &s=3$
